- [x] `scope3_redefined_choices_and_schedule` — **2026-08-29, direct user decision, after an extended definition/scoping conversation.** Scope #3's decision set is redefined to its final six choices — Reply, Forward, Schedule, Cold email, Flag, Ignore (`leave_alone` internally) — and Route to Scope #1 / Route to Scope #2 are removed as decision types entirely. This isn't a trim, it's a boundary: Scope #3 no longer connects to Scope #1 or #2 in any way, confirmed directly rather than left implicit.

  Planning found the real removal larger than first assumed. It wasn't just a verification helper sitting in `inbox_agent.py` — routing was woven into `RuleLayer.classify()`'s own second branch (a subject/body keyword match against a registered capsule, run immediately after the sender-pattern check, i.e. a primary decision path, not a fallback), into `inbox_features.py`'s actual input feature vector (`rule_hit_scope1`/`rule_hit_scope2` dropped, `sem_sim_schedule`/`sem_sim_cold_email` added in their place — the vector shrank from 13 dimensions to 11, `VERSION` bumped `inbox-features-v1-13d` → `inbox-features-v2-11d` so a stale checkpoint can't silently mismatch shapes), and into `LLMClassifier`'s own system prompt, which used to list `route_scope1`/`route_scope2` as two of the model's choices and threaded a `capsule_hints` parameter through `classify()`/`_build_prompt()` just to describe registered capsules to the LLM. All three removed together, along with the now-pointless dispatch/needs-attention branches in `autonomous_watcher.py` that existed only to act on a routing decision.

  New module `schedule_recorder.py` is the Output step for the "schedule" decision, and it deliberately gets no matching/reuse model at all — unlike Reply's `ReplyMatchNet`. A schedule note is new information every time (a new date, a new task, a new person); there's nothing in a past note that usefully predicts the content of the next one, so the only thing worth learning is "is this schedule-worthy" (already covered by the existing 6-way decision classifier), not any content generation. What gets recorded is simply whatever the human typed, appended verbatim to `data/schedule.txt` — same honesty guarantee `reply_recorder.py` already has: a blank/whitespace-only note saves nothing, never invents content. Wired into `router.py`'s `confirm_suggestion()`/`override_decision()`, and into `reply_trace_translator.py`'s session-routing logic so a finished DemoRecorder session lands in `schedule.txt` or `reply_examples.jsonl` depending on the real recorded decision.

  Cold Email is real and wanted, not dropped — it's sequenced as its own separate follow-up plan, not started yet, no design beyond the earlier conceptual conversation that first proposed it.

  Also fixed while wiring this in, a real training-data-fabrication bug: `reply_trace_translator.py` had a backward-compatibility fallback that silently recorded a message as `"reply"` whenever no decision was found in `routed_history.json` — violating the module's own documented contract ("a translator whose only job is honesty must fail CLOSED: no evidence => don't record"). Removed; a missing or unrecognized decision is now always skipped, never guessed.

  Full suite: 1523 passed, 9 skipped, 0 failed.

  **Extended 2026-08-30/31**: the Schedule decision's `data/schedule.txt` log described above stayed as-is, but it's no longer the whole story — a Schedule confirm now also creates a real Google Calendar event. New module `calendar_client.py` mirrors `gmail_client.py`'s existing mock/real client-swap pattern exactly: same OAuth credentials file, same "mock unless real creds are present" seam. `gmail_client.py`'s `SCOPES` list gained the Calendar scope alongside the Gmail scope it already had, so the one OAuth grant now covers both. The plain-text log is genuinely unchanged — the calendar event is additive, not a replacement, matching this project's own pattern of layering new output on top of an existing one rather than swapping it out. The event's date/time is never inferred from the email body: the human always types the real start/end directly, through two new datetime-local fields added to Inbox Dispatch's reply box, wired the same way the existing reply/schedule text fields already are — the same "never invent" rule this project holds everywhere else (Reply's confidence-gated fast-fill, `reply_trace_translator.py`'s fail-closed recording), just applied to dates instead of text. Also added: a small "View Schedule" button, bundled into the Electron app, that just opens `schedule.txt` in Notepad — a one-line convenience, not a new subsystem.

  **Whole-plan review, 2026-08-31.** Found two real Critical bugs that meant the feature didn't actually work yet, both plan-text defects rather than implementer mistakes: (1) `InboxRouter`'s default calendar client was hardcoded to `MockCalendarClient()` instead of calling `get_calendar_client()` (the actual mock/real swap function) — no production caller ever passed `calendar_client=` explicitly, so `RealCalendarClient` was unreachable even with real credentials present; fixed by defaulting to `get_calendar_client()` instead. (2) The UI's `datetime-local` input produces a value like `"2026-09-03T14:00"` — no seconds, no offset — which is not valid RFC3339 and would have been rejected by the real Calendar API; every test in the plan had used a fabricated, already-offset-aware string that the real UI never produces, so this was invisible until checked against the real input shape. Fixed with a `_to_rfc3339()` helper (`datetime.fromisoformat(v).astimezone().isoformat()`) that interprets the human's typed wall-clock time using the machine's real local offset — invents no time information. Also fixed: an environment-fragile test that would have blocked on a real OAuth browser flow once real credentials exist, missing `/api/override` HTTP-layer test coverage, and `schedule.txt` was missing from `.gitignore`. One re-review finding: the fix round's own self-caught "test landed in the wrong class" mistake left one remnant — a regression test for "a routed message must not reappear on a second poll" had its real assertion stranded as a no-op inside an unrelated new test; restored directly. Full suite (final, post-fix): 1555 passed, 9 skipped, 0 failed. Commit range `4273876d`..`b3f76921` on this branch.

  **OAuth scope migration note:** if a real `credentials/token.json` was ever generated before this change (2026-08-30/31), it was authorized under the old single Gmail-only scope and will need to be deleted so the OAuth flow re-runs and grants the new Calendar scope too — otherwise `RealCalendarClient` will fail with a permissions error at the API call, not at connection time. No real `token.json` exists in this project yet, so this is forward-looking guidance, not an active bug.

- [x] `scope3_gmail_style_actions` — **2026-09-01, direct user pushback after repeated attempts to explain the redesign got misread** ("these are actions, not buttons you press"). The user pointed at a real Gmail screenshot: real Gmail only shows Reply/Forward as buttons under an opened email — Schedule/Cold email/Flag aren't real Gmail concepts, so cramming all six into one action-grid made the page look like a form instead of the real thing it imitates.

  Redesigned both `local_ui/` and `practice_inbox/` to real-Gmail-equivalent controls: Reply/Forward became two pill buttons under the email body (matching the screenshot), opening the same shared textarea+Send flow already built. Leave alone reuses the existing Archive toolbar icon (already correctly labeled, just needed wiring). Schedule reuses the toolbar's Snooze icon (previously disabled). Flag is a new star icon in the toolbar, matching real Gmail's own flag-for-importance star. Cold email was removed from this view entirely — confirmed directly with the user ("Cold email is a different action") — it isn't a reaction to an existing email, it belongs only on its own future page.

  Removed the override dropdown + separate Confirm/Override button pair entirely. Clicking any real icon IS the action now, matching real Gmail — no more "pick a suggestion, then separately click Override." Whether that counts as confirming Intern's own suggestion or overriding it is still decided and recorded correctly behind the scenes (`isConfirm = clicked-decision === suggested-decision`, still hits `/api/confirm` vs `/api/override` respectively) — the project's own accuracy metrics still need that distinction, it's just invisible to whoever's clicking now.

  Real functional bug this surfaced, not just a UI change: `automate_inbox.py`'s Play automation used to blindly click a generic `#confirmBtn` for any non-reply/forward decision — that button no longer exists. Fixed to click the specific real icon matching the decision (`#archiveBtn` or `#flagBtn`); schedule and cold_email now correctly join reply/forward in being left pending for a human, since none of those four have real content this script could type on its own. All 7 real-browser Playwright tests against the live page (`tests/test_automate_inbox.py`) still pass, unchanged in what they assert, proving the real click-path still works end to end.

  Full suite: 1555 passed, 9 skipped, 0 failed. Commit `74120019` on this branch.

  **Extended 2026-09-01 — Schedule's two-field form collapsed to one.** Direct user feedback: "when we schedule we manually do these things by hand: 'I should schedule this person for 4am on X'" — a human deciding to schedule something makes exactly one decision, a moment in time, never a separate duration. Asking for Starts *and* Ends (`#eventStart`/`#eventEnd`) made the form ask a question nobody reading an email actually has an answer to. Replaced with one `#eventWhen` field; the end time is now computed client-side in `app.js` (`addMinutes(value, 30)`) as start + 30 minutes and sent to the existing, unchanged `/api/confirm`/`/api/override` backend — `event_start`/`event_end` still travel over the wire exactly as before, so no backend or `router.py`/`calendar_client.py` change was needed. `sendPending()` now also refuses to send with no time picked, mirroring the existing Forward-needs-a-recipient guard. Two new real-browser Playwright tests (`tests/test_local_server.py::TestScheduleSingleWhenField`) confirm exactly one datetime field renders and that a picked "when" produces a real 30-minute Calendar event. Full suite: 1560 passed, 9 skipped, 0 failed.

- [x] `scope3_cold_email` — **2026-09-01, direct request ("finish Scope #3 ASAP") off the existing spec** (`docs/superpowers/specs/2026-08-30-schedule-calendar-and-cold-email-design.md`, Part 2). Cold email was the last stub decision (logged "not implemented yet"); it's now real, matching Reply/Forward/Schedule/Flag's own "a real action, not a label" bar. Executed via a 4-task SDD plan (`docs/superpowers/plans/2026-09-01-cold-email.md`), all four tasks reviewed clean, plus a final whole-plan review and one fix round.

  Who to email comes from `components/inbox_router/data/task_list.txt` — a boss-style, committed plain text file, deliberately not gitignored (same "real, synthetic, checked-in fixture" treatment as `mock_inbox.json`). New `task_list_parser.py` reads it with line/regex parsing only, no LLM guessing. **Ruling recorded on a spec ambiguity**: the spec's example showed one bare `Cold email:` heading over three targets with no distinguishing text, which would make every pre-filled subject identical and empty — the heading format was fixed as `Cold email: <optional text>`, where any text after the colon becomes that batch's pre-filled subject, so the spec's own literal example still parses correctly (as an empty-but-valid subject) while a real boss-written list with actual context text produces a useful one.

  New `cold_email_sender.py` is the Output step — reuses the existing `create_draft()` call verbatim (same as Reply/Forward), tracks who's already been contacted in a gitignored `cold_email_state.json`, and deliberately does **not** call `decision_recorder.record_example()`: unlike Reply/Forward/Schedule/Flag there's no ambiguous inbox message being classified here, the task list already decided WHO. New page `components/inbox_router/cold_email/` mirrors Practice Inbox's list→detail shape; `local_server.py` gained `/cold-email/*` routes, with `build_router()`/`handle_request()`/`make_handler()` all extended via new trailing-optional parameters so every existing call site keeps working unchanged (confirmed in both the task review and the final review). Electron gained a "Launch Cold Email" button next to "Launch mockups"/"View Schedule", shown only for a capsule carrying `local_server`/`url` (i.e. Inbox Dispatch).

  Final whole-plan review found 4 real Important bugs at the seams between tasks (the category of bug per-task reviews structurally can't see): a parser test hard-coupled to the exact contents of the very file the shipped UI tells users to edit (would go red the first time someone used the feature for real); silent failure on a real Gmail send error (no try/catch client-side, no try/except server-side — a dropped connection with zero user feedback); `send_cold_email()` accepting drafts to any address, not just pending targets, and not being idempotent (a double-click could draft the same person twice); and the two new routes crashing opaquely with no `cold_email_sender` supplied, more reachable than first assumed since `make_handler(router)` with one argument is a real pattern elsewhere in this repo. All 4 fixed in one round, plus 5 smaller polish items (a stale docstring, a theoretical crash on a bare state-file path, missing error-state CSS, a stale sidebar count on load failure, stale "not implemented" log wording). Re-review verdicted all 9 ADDRESSED; one new, purely cosmetic Minor was found (the send route's error message doesn't distinguish "not a pending target" from "blank subject/body") — parked, not fixed, since the underlying behavior is already correct and only the wording is imprecise.

  Full suite (final): 1587 passed, 9 skipped, 0 failed. Commit range `2e6cb99d`..`77d2a1dc` on this branch.

- [x] `scope3_llm_classifier_model_id_bugfix` — **2026-09-02, found live off direct request ("I wanna see Scope #3 actually working please")**. Ran `automate_inbox.py --commit --limit 5 --pace 2.0` against the real Practice Inbox to actually show the AI classifier deciding, not the plumbing around it. It worked mechanically (5 distinct real emails processed, confirming the earlier "latest wins" history fix holds), but every single decision fell back to Flag — the LLM classifier reported "No models loaded" even though LM Studio genuinely had `qwen2.5-7b-instruct` loaded (already proven working through Cold Email's own LM Studio call). This meant the real AI-driven classification had never actually been demonstrated in this whole project — only its safe fallback.

  Root cause, in `llm_classifier.py`: `LLMClassifier.__init__` set `self._llm_model = model_id or _DEFAULT_MODELS.get(provider, "")`, and `_DEFAULT_MODELS["lmstudio"]` was hardcoded to the fake name `"local-model"`. Neither construction site (`local_server.py:103`, `router.py:561`) ever passes `model_id`, so the classifier always asked LM Studio for a model called `"local-model"` — a name that matches nothing LM Studio ever actually loads — and LM Studio correctly refused. **Decision:** apply the exact fix already proven correct in `cold_email_llm.py` — when no `model_id` is explicitly given, ask LM Studio itself for the real loaded model's id (`client.models.list()`) and use that instead of the placeholder. Client construction and the lookup are one fail-closed unit: any failure (LM Studio unreachable, nothing loaded) leaves `_llm_model` on the old placeholder, so `classify()` still fails closed to Flag exactly as before — this fix only removes the *false* failure, not the real fail-closed safety net. An explicitly-passed `model_id` is left untouched, so a caller deliberately targeting one of several simultaneously-loaded models still gets exactly that one.

  New `tests/test_llm_classifier.py` (4 tests): real dynamic resolution, falls back to the placeholder when nothing's loaded, falls back to the placeholder when LM Studio is unreachable (client construction itself failing, not just the list call), and an explicit `model_id` is never overridden. Full suite: 1610 passed, 9 skipped, 0 failed.

  **Verified live, 2026-09-02, after restarting the server.** First live attempt after the restart still showed every decision as Flag with the same "No models loaded" text — traced directly (not guessed): `pending_entries()`/`/api/inbox` only ever reads already-computed history rows off disk, it never re-classifies. All 27 pending mock emails at the time had been classified in earlier sessions, before this fix, so their stale pre-fix rationale was just sitting in the gitignored `routed_history.json`/`mock_state.json` and kept getting redisplayed. Confirmed the fix itself is correct with two direct, isolated calls: `LLMClassifier(provider="lmstudio")._llm_model` resolved to the real `qwen2.5-7b-instruct`, and a hand-built `classify()` call returned a real decision. Reset both disposable state files (gitignored, same "regenerate freely between demos" treatment as `cold_email_state.json`) so every mock email became genuinely unprocessed again, then re-polled — 26 real LLM calls, ~1.75s each, every one a real distinct decision (`reply`/`forward`/`schedule`/`leave_alone`) with a real matching rationale, not a single Flag fallback.

  That surfaced a second, separate real bug in `automate_inbox.py`, also live-found: `main()` clicked the page's Refresh button then waited a fixed 600ms before reading rows off the DOM — fine while `/api/inbox` only ever read cached decisions, but wrong now that a first poll can trigger real classify() calls taking seconds each; the fixed wait raced ahead of the response and read 0 rows, reporting "emails processed 0" even with real mail waiting. Fixed with condition-based waiting (this project's own established technique, matching `systematic-debugging`'s supporting practice) — `page.expect_response()` around the Refresh click, timeout 60s, instead of guessing a fixed delay. New regression test `TestMainWaitsForSlowFirstPoll` (real Playwright browser, an artificially slow `/api/inbox` handler) confirmed RED against the pre-fix code and GREEN after. `_FakeInboxPage` (used by the existing crash-recovery test) gained a matching `expect_response()` stub.

  Full end-to-end live run, both fixes together: `automate_inbox.py --commit --limit 5` against 5 genuinely fresh real mock emails produced `reply`, `forward`, `reply`, `schedule`, `reply` — five different real AI decisions with real, content-matching rationales, the first time this project has ever shown Scope #3's actual LLM classifier working end to end instead of its safe fallback.

  **Frontend cleanup, direct feedback watching the live run**: two elements showing the AI's raw decision text on screen were removed — `local_ui/index.html`'s `Suggested: <strong id="detailDecision">` paragraph (now a hidden `<strong id="detailDecision" hidden>`, kept only because `automate_inbox.py` still needs to read the value programmatically — switched from `page.locator(...).inner_text()`, which reads rendered text and returns `""` for anything hidden, to `.text_content()`, which reads the raw DOM text regardless of visibility) and `app.js`'s per-row `.row-badge` span showing the decision as a small gray pill on every list row (deleted outright, plus its now-dead CSS rule). Neither element had any automation or test dependency beyond that one selector-read fix.

  **Real UX bug, also live-found**: with `dwell_ms=0`, `process_one()` opened and immediately closed each email with zero pause before acting on `--pace`-driven decisions -- a person watching the real visible browser window saw it as one instant flash, not something followable, even though the terminal printed real detail the whole time. Fixed by threading `--pace` into a new `dwell_ms` parameter on `process_one()`, held via `page.wait_for_timeout(dwell_ms)` right after printing an email's decision/rationale and before acting on it -- the email now actually stays on screen long enough to read.

  **Deliberate second rule exception, direct instruction after being told plainly what breaking it costs**: "Break it goddamnit, I literally need to see it full functioning before I can deem this as complete." New `inbox_reply_llm.py` (mirrors `cold_email_llm.py`'s exact shape: same LM Studio client construction, model id read live via `client.models.list()`, fails closed to `""` on any error) generates a real reply body for "reply" decisions specifically. Wired into `automate_inbox.py` behind a new explicit `--auto-draft-reply` flag (requires `--commit`, no effect otherwise) -- scoped exactly like Cold Email's own exception: this capability exists ONLY inside `automate_inbox.py`'s own script, never inside `router.py`'s `confirm_suggestion()`/`override_decision()`, so the real Inbox Dispatch page a person clicks through by hand is completely untouched and still requires a real human's own typed words. Forward and Schedule are deliberately excluded even with the flag on -- Forward would need a fabricated recipient address and Schedule a fabricated date, both actively wrong rather than merely unreviewed, unlike a drafted reply that's just typed and sent using the email's own real context.

  A real correctness bug was caught while wiring the outcome string through: the loop's `skipped` counter compared `result["outcome"] != "confirmed"` exactly, but a successful AI-drafted send reports the more specific `"confirmed (AI-drafted reply sent)"` -- an exact match would have wrongly treated that row as still-pending and skipped past whatever row replaced it next. Fixed to `not result["outcome"].startswith("confirmed")`, applied consistently in both the `skipped` counter and the final "N email(s) need a real reply" summary filter.

  Verified live: `automate_inbox.py --commit --auto-draft-reply --limit 3` against real pending mail produced two real AI-drafted-and-sent replies -- "Thank you for the submission. I will process the data entry for Mr. Delgado's new car insurance application as soon as possible." and "Sure, I can look into that. Please send over the invoice and let me know the specific details you need confirmed. Thanks!" -- both real, content-matching, both rows actually removed from the pending list, not just previewed. New tests: `tests/test_inbox_reply_llm.py` (3), `tests/test_automate_inbox.py::TestAutoDraftReply` (3: real send, fails-closed fallback, only "reply" ever triggers it).

  **Three more real bugs found running this live, repeatedly, at the user's insistence ("Run it yourself, I'm watching.")**:

  1. **Windows console encoding crash.** Printing an AI-drafted reply crashed `main()` with `UnicodeEncodeError` the moment this ran in a real (non-piped) console instead of a captured one -- the system codepage (cp1252) can't encode curly quotes/em dashes that a real LLM response routinely contains. Fixed with `sys.stdout.reconfigure(encoding="utf-8", errors="replace")` at the top of both `automate_inbox.py` and `automate_cold_email.py` -- only affects what gets printed, never the text that actually gets typed into the page.

  2. **`localhost` vs `127.0.0.1` navigation race.** `local_server.py`'s `HTTPServer` binds only IPv4 (`127.0.0.1`), but `SERVER_URL` used `"localhost"` -- Chromium's own resolution of that hostname can try IPv6 (`::1`) first depending on the environment, which nothing is listening on, so `page.goto()` hung for the full 30s timeout and crashed, even though the server was genuinely up and `curl` reached it instantly. Fixed by pointing `SERVER_URL` at the literal IP, removing the resolution step -- and therefore the ambiguity -- entirely.

  3. **Session-boundary root cause for "I never see the window."** Every run was provably correct server-side (screenshots, real state changes, real sends) but the user reported the browser window either never appeared or "appeared but did nothing," across many runs. Root-caused by direct comparison: a `PowerShell` `Start-Process` call earlier in the session DID visibly reach the user's real desktop (they interacted with that tab), while every `Bash`-launched Playwright browser did not -- strong evidence the two tools run in different Windows sessions in this environment. **Decision: use PowerShell, not Bash, for every live/visible automation run going forward** -- confirmed working the moment it was tried ("I did see the Window"). Not a code bug; a tooling-choice fix.

  **Fourth issue, a real content-quality bug, not a script bug**: one live AI-drafted reply came back with the local model's own meta-commentary leaked into the output, in Chinese, telling itself not to use Chinese. Since every reply in this project is English, any CJK character in a response is itself proof the generation is malformed. Fixed in `inbox_reply_llm.py`: `generate_reply()` now rejects (fails closed to `""`, same as every other failure mode) any response containing a CJK character, before it can ever be typed into the page. New regression test `test_generate_reply_fails_closed_on_a_malformed_cjk_response`.

  Full suite after this batch: 1618 passed, 9 skipped, 0 failed.

- [x] `scope3_real_gmail_deferred` — **2026-09-02, direct decision**: after everything above was proven working live and repeatedly, asked directly whether to wire up real Gmail (`RealGmailClient`) next. Direct answer: "No, just do it on the mock webpage, that would be better." Real Gmail requires the user's own Google OAuth login -- not something that can be done on their behalf -- and once live would put a real draft, addressed to a real person, in the user's own real Gmail account -- materially higher stakes than anything demonstrated so far against mock data (still never an actual send -- see the terminology fix below, `GmailClientBase` has no send() method for either client). **Decision: Scope #3 stays on `MockGmailClient` deliberately, not as a limitation.** The full pipeline (classify → decide → draft/generate → type → create draft) is proven end to end against it. `get_gmail_client()`'s mock/real swap is already built and ready the moment real credentials exist -- explicitly deferred, not blocked on any remaining code. Task Tree hub note updated from "In progress" to "Working (mock)" to reflect this.

- [x] `scope3_forward_auto_draft_and_terminology_fix` — **2026-09-02, direct request**: "I want to see it forward certain email to people" -- one of four concrete asks alongside reply, boss task list, and cold email. Forward previously always stayed pending (deliberately, since it needs a third-party recipient the email itself never supplies) even with `--auto-draft-reply` on. Fixed identically: `page.expect_response()` around the `setView()` call. New regression test `TestMainWaitsForSlowFirstPoll` (real slow-response handler), confirmed the same RED-before/GREEN-after discipline.

  **Terminology bug, found from direct user pushback**: "It says 'Reply sent' but I see no reply being created or actually being made, what is going on?" A completely fair question -- `automate_inbox.py`/`automate_cold_email.py`'s own output said "sent" when the real underlying action is `create_draft()`; `GmailClientBase` has no send()/send_message() method anywhere in this project, for either the mock or real client. Verified directly for the user: 79 real drafts existed in `mock_drafts.json` at the time, 55 of them real "Re:" replies with genuine distinct content -- the feature was never fake, the WORD was just wrong. Fixed every user-facing "sent"/"send" string in both scripts to "drafted"/"draft created" (`outcome` values, the mode-line banner, the final summary line, the confirmed-forward/reply outcome strings), including the ones tests asserted on. Same fix applied to `local_ui/app.js`'s own `SNACKBAR_TEXT` (the real toast a human using the real page sees) -- "Reply sent."/"Forwarded." became "Reply drafted."/"Forward drafted.", since a real person clicking those buttons hits the exact same `create_draft()`, never an actual send.

No real checkpoint exists yet in this project's live demo path (still cold-start, rule/LLM only), so this is a correctness guarantee for the future, not an active migration.

Across `test_inbox_router.py`, `test_local_server.py`, `test_inbox_agent.py`, `test_autonomous_watcher.py`, `test_recorder_bridge_inbox_router.py`, `test_inbox_features.py`, `test_llm_classifier.py`, `test_automate_inbox.py`: every real assertion checking a "flag" decision or fallback text updated to "leave_alone" (the no-LLM/no-signal fallback genuinely changed), three whole tests specific to `apply_flag_label()`'s real-action behavior deleted (`test_confirm_flag_applies_a_real_flag_label`, `test_override_to_flag_applies_a_real_flag_label`, `test_confirm_leave_alone_does_not_apply_flag_label`), one renamed and repurposed to keep covering its still-valid no-draft-needed-decision behavior (`test_confirm_flag_creates_no_draft` -> `test_confirm_leave_alone_creates_no_draft`).

- [x] `scope3_visible_typing_and_rationale_cjk_guard` — **2026-09-02, direct feedback watching a live run**: "I said I want to see it actually typing in a Reply, please." `page.fill()` sets a textarea's value in one instant DOM write -- nothing visible actually happens on screen, which is why the auto-drafted reply/forward demos never looked like anything was being typed even though a real draft really was created. Switched both to `locator.press_sequentially(text, delay=35)` in `automate_inbox.py`, which sends one real keystroke at a time -- a person watching the real browser window now genuinely sees it being typed.

  **Second real bug, found live in that same run**: the classifier's own rationale text ("because: ...") leaked CJK meta-commentary mid-sentence -- the same failure mode the CJK guard in `inbox_reply_llm.py` already catches, but that guard only covers reply/forward generation, not `llm_classifier.py`'s own `classify()` call. Added the identical guard to `classify()`'s rationale field specifically: unlike the reply/forward case (where the generated text IS the whole point, so a malformed response fails the call closed), a garbled rationale doesn't invalidate the real decision/confidence the same response carries -- so this replaces just the rationale text with a generic fallback rather than discarding the whole classification. New test `test_classify_sanitizes_a_malformed_cjk_rationale` confirms the real decision/confidence survive while the garbled text is replaced.

  Verified live immediately after: a fresh run showed 3 real replies visibly typed character-by-character and drafted, 1 leave_alone confirmed, 3 schedule items correctly left pending, and one reply correctly failed closed when LM Studio didn't respond usably (the existing fail-closed safety net, not a new bug). One transient test failure during that same run (`test_commit_processes_every_email_down_to_empty`, unrelated to either fix -- doesn't even reach the `auto_draft_reply` code path) confirmed as resource contention from running the live demo and the test suite at once, not a regression -- passes clean in isolation.

  Full suite: 1621 passed, 9 skipped, 0 failed.

  A crash in one phase must not silently skip the other -- "everything the boss needs done" means the rest still happens even if Cold Email (or the inbox) hits a real `PlaywrightError` mid-walk; each phase's own try/except is independent, matching the crash-resilience pattern already proven in both underlying scripts.

Fixed with `page.wait_for_function()` polling the real row count instead of guessing a longer fixed number.

The code was never broken; both effects are real but small/fast (~0.45-0.55s each) against a busy multi-step live demo with a lot else changing on screen at once -- not a code bug, a "which exact half-second to look at" problem. - [x] `scope3_more_cold_emails_and_checks` -- 2026-10-05, direct request: 'Add more tasks for the Scope #3, more cold emails and general checking.' The user chose all three kinds of checking (replies, inbox topic, schedule) plus more test data, and ~10 cold-email people in 3 sections.

(2) Answered by rules, not an LLM (new checker.py): reply = any inbox mail from that address; topic = every topic word (filler dropped) appears in subject+body; schedule = schedule_extract reads the time (needs a date AND a clock time, as the schedule flow already requires), then the calendar is read for a 30-minute slot. Every answer carries its evidence (the emails/events). Anything that cannot be read literally -- 'next Friday', a calendar that cannot be listed -- is 'unclear', never a guess. (3) Read-only by construction: checks never draft, send, label or schedule (a test compares the data files byte-for-byte before and after). (4) Same reach as Cold Email: a Checks list/detail view in the Inbox Dispatch page with no nav button (the user's earlier no-tab decision for Cold Email), served by GET /checks/api/list, which re-reads the task list and mailbox per request. (5) run_boss_task_list.py gained Phase 3, after the inbox, so a reply/schedule check sees the mailbox and calendar as the run left them -- e.g. Grace's new email proposes Oct 14 2pm; if the inbox phase schedules it, that check turns from free to busy in the same run. Needed: calendar clients gained a read-only list_events() (mock + real Google Calendar); the base method is not abstract so existing write-only fakes keep working.

  TEST DATA: mock_inbox.json gained replies from 2 of the new cold-email people (Hannah Cole, Grace Okafor) and an October invoice, so reply checks give yes AND no, topic checks find and miss.

  TESTS: tests/test_inbox_checks.py (13) -- unit (parser kinds/order/junk, no leakage into cold targets, the real task list's shape, every answer kind, newest-first evidence, overlap at a slot edge, unreadable calendar -> unclear, nothing written, filler words), integration (the server route on a real mock mailbox; 503 without a service), E2E (a real headless Chromium on the real page walks every check with automate_checks.process_one). tests/test_run_boss_task_list.py: checks run last, on the same page, into the run log. 364 Scope #3 tests pass.

Before: Launch opened the PRACTICE inbox (/practice/, the recording page) in the default browser, while Play's automate_inbox.py launched its own Chromium on the main page -- two windows, and the one Launch opened was never the one the agent used.

Isolated step by step: blank page attaches in 0.6 s; in-process server, separate-process server, trivial static page and a visible off-screen window all hang; the same page opened AFTER start attaches in ~1 s. DECISION: open_url starts Chromium on about:blank, opens the page as a new tab through DevTools (/json/new), closes the blank tab. Bonus: pressing Launch again after closing the tab reopens it without a second window.

  SAFETY FIX: with the user's real Scope #2 window open, a show-mode Scope #2 test attached to it and filled a row there. tests/conftest.py now sets INTERN_NO_WORKSPACE_ATTACH for every test; attach() refuses while it is set; only the attach tests clear it, on their own private headless windows.

  TESTS: tests/test_inbox_workspace.py (15: shared helpers, main-page vs practice vs other server, separate ports, blank-then-tab launch, Launch-again reopens the tab, command-line http never used, headless never attaches, Electron guard, every script goes through open_page, test-suite switch, and E2E -- a real Chromium with a decoy practice tab; Play picks the main page, walks a check, window survives). Scope #2 workspace tests updated for the new launch. Scope #2+#3: 638 passed.

DECISION: rows come from '#rowList .row-item' only (guard: test_play_only_picks_rows_from_the_inbox_list). - [x] `scope3_cold_email_signed_as_sender` -- 2026-10-05, direct request: 'Put [My Name] as Kevin'. Found in the saved drafts (mock_drafts.json): the cold-email LLM wrote '[Your Name]', '[Your Full Name]', '[Your Company]', or invented a sender ('Alex Thompson', 'GreenTech Solutions') -- it was never told who is writing.

  DECISIONS: (1) the sender's name is a setting, data/sender_profile.json {"name": "Kevin"}, not code; (2) the prompt says the email is written on behalf of that person, signed with that name, and forbids placeholders and invented names, companies, titles or facts; (3) safety net -- a name placeholder that still slips through ([Your Name], [My Name], [Your Full Name]) is filled with the real name; any other bracketed blank (e.g. [Your Company]) gets one retry, then the draft is refused and the person is left for a human, rather than saving a draft with a hole in it (same never-invent rule as the rest of Scope #3). ### Perception
Objective 1 (thesis): ≥95% detection accuracy across multiple GUI environments.

- [x] `perception_adapter_seam` — Observer base (capture→normalize→validate), injectable, canonical element schema (`observers/schema.py`), loud validation on mismatch. Excel perception swap proven.
- [ ] `perception_vision_big3_1` — **Big Three #1:** UIA → Vision. Today reads the UIA a11y tree (free, but only works on apps with a clean tree). General agent must see pixels: screenshot → VLM that localizes + semantically reads elements, hybrid UIA-for-grounding + VLM-for-understanding. A CV+OCR vision observer prototype exists (`components/observers/vlm/`) and has been recorded/trained/run against once — not yet proven at the 95% bar.
- [ ] `perception_detection_accuracy_95` — Measured detection accuracy (F1) ≥95% across multiple environments. Tooling exists (`scripts/perception_eval.py --log`) — no logged runs yet. **Concrete blocker found 2026-08-11**: Tesseract OCR isn't installed on this machine (`shutil.which("tesseract")` returns `None`) — the vision observer's OCR path can't run at all, not just "hasn't been run."

  **Blocker fixed, same day, step 1 of the Scope #1 roadmap.** Root cause was more specific than "not installed": `winget install UB-Mannheim.TesseractOCR` reported it already present (`C:\Program Files\Tesseract-OCR\tesseract.exe` exists, version 5.4.0.20240606) — the real gap was that its install directory was never added to PATH, so `cv_detector.py`'s `shutil.which("tesseract")` check (the only lookup mechanism it has, no hardcoded fallback path) always came back empty. Fixed by adding `C:\Program Files\Tesseract-OCR` to the persistent User `PATH` environment variable (`[Environment]::SetEnvironmentVariable`, not just the current shell session — verified it also needed a manual `export PATH=...` inside the already-running Bash tool session, since a process's environment is fixed at the time it starts and doesn't pick up registry-level PATH changes made after). Verified directly: `tesseract --version` now runs from a fresh shell, `shutil.which("tesseract")` resolves.

  **This retroactively explains two "pre-existing, unrelated" test failures that had shown up as noise in every single full-suite run this entire session**: `tests/test_cv_perception.py::test_detector_finds_interactive_elements` and `::test_ocr_reads_a_known_label`. Both now pass — full file: **10/10 passed**. Full suite re-run: **619 passed, 0 failed, 9 skipped** — the first completely clean full-suite run of the whole session, no failures of any kind, not even the usual 2 tesseract ones.

  **First `perception_eval.py --live` attempt, same day: mis-targeted, invalidated, not a real Objective 1 reading.** `--live` mode's 5-second countdown ("Click the form window NOW") exists specifically so a human can bring the target window to the foreground before the capture fires — it has no other way to know what to point at. Ran it without the user actually clicking the form during that window (nothing to click *for* me), and it captured whatever genuinely was in the foreground instead: the user's own browser. The resulting overlay image showed real personal content (open tabs, a real document name, browsing history) — deleted immediately on discovery, matching this session's already-established rule against blind/uncoordinated screen capture. The logged result (`detection_accuracy=1.2%`, matched 1/29) was measured against that browser window, not the form — meaningless as an Objective 1 reading, and removed from `perception_eval_log.jsonl` (the file's only entry, so deleted outright rather than edited) so it can't be mistaken for a real measurement later. **Real next step, not yet done**: this needs the user to actually run `python scripts/perception_eval.py --live` themselves (or be ready to click the form the instant the countdown starts if run from here) — it is not a command safe to fire blindly from this side.

### Representation
Objective 2 (thesis): <5% encoding-ambiguity error rate.

- [x] `representation_datasource_injection` — DataSource injection (partial): `data_source` is injectable, agent reads source I/O through the seam. Follow-up: extract `_refresh_record_cache` orchestration.
- [ ] `representation_encoding_ambiguity_5` — Encoding ambiguity <5% (duplicate type+label signatures making a click target un-identifiable). Measured 2026-08-06 on current dataset: **10.88%** — above target. `scripts/encoding_ambiguity.py --log`. **Re-measured 2026-08-11 directly on the 4 real clean sessions: ~14%.** Real, structural (Driver 2/3 label collisions, verified directly). A closely related fix (`disambiguate_attempted`) already regressed accuracy when tried — any fix here needs a real isolated A/B test, not an assumed win. See Scope #1's ROADMAP note above.

  **Promising, differentiated lead found 2026-08-11 — not yet implemented, deliberately.** `disambiguate_attempted`'s failure was diagnosed at the time as "rank-based disambiguation depends on accessibility-tree list order, which isn't as stable a signal as hoped." Checked whether a *structurally different, more stable* signal exists: `agent.py`'s `_detect_section()` already resolves which repeated section (e.g. "Driver 2") a focused element belongs to, live, at inference time, for value-lookup purposes — via real `panecontrol` elements' bbox geometry (`section_pattern=r"section_(driver|vehicle)_(\d+)$"` matched against pane labels), not list order. **Verified directly against real training data, not assumed**: `section_driver_2` and `section_driver_3` genuinely exist as distinct, stable pane elements in the actual captured trace files (`session_20260809_214316`, confirmed by direct inspection). This is a fundamentally different kind of signal than the one that already failed — bbox/geometry-based, the same category of information the model already trusts for its click-pointer decision, not fragile traversal-order rank.

  **Implemented and A/B-tested 2026-08-12 — result: negative, not adopted.** Important scoping correction found first: this whole mechanism (`_attempt_key`'s "attempted" bookkeeping) doesn't actually move THIS metric at all — `scripts/encoding_ambiguity.py`'s `ambiguity_rate` only measures raw `(type, label)` collisions in a captured state, and never reads `_attempt_key`/`disambiguate_attempted`. It affects a *related but distinct* problem (the "already filled" false-positive on Driver 2/3's still-empty fields), useful in its own right but not this objective. Built anyway as a real, tested capability: `disambiguate_attempted` is now a 3-way enum (`"none"`/`"rank"`/`"section"`, mirroring `rare_weight_basis`'s existing pattern) in `transformer.py`, `train.py`, and `BCTrainer`; `"section"` disambiguates via a new offline `_detect_section()` (mirrors `agent.py`'s live version — real `panecontrol` bbox geometry, not list order), with `section_pattern`/`section_prefix` injected the same way `agent.py` receives `ScopeConfig`, keeping `transformer.py` scope-agnostic. 17 new regression tests (`tests/test_section_disambiguation.py`, `tests/test_rare_weight_basis_toggle.py` updated for the new string API).

  **Isolated A/B on the current 5-session/3,987-step dataset, identical hyperparameters, only this flag changed**: `"section"` → `val_click_acc=81.9%` (epoch 78/80) vs. the `"none"` control's `83.0%` — a small regression, not the hoped-for win. Checkpoint saved to `tasks/form_filling/experiments/model_section_disambig_ab.pt`, NOT adopted into the live `model.pt`. Combined with `"rank"`'s much larger prior regression (46.9% vs 68.9%), this is now **0/2 real wins from disambiguation-signal experiments** on this dataset — a real, if small-sample, signal that this whole family of fix doesn't help at the current data volume, consistent with root cause #1 (data volume, not representation) being the actual bottleneck. **Decision: do not pursue the larger, riskier version of this idea (adding section as a real `ELEM_FEATURES` input dimension, which would require an `ELEM_FEATURES` size change synced across `agent.py`/`transformer.py` — the exact class of change that caused the stale-checkpoint crash incident) until there's a specific reason to expect data volume isn't the limiting factor.** The `"section"` option itself is kept (tested, documented, real capability) in case a future, much larger dataset changes this calculus — just not defaulted on.
- [ ] `representation_transition_mapping_90` — State-transition mapping correctness ≥90% (does each recorded action visibly do something?). Measured 2026-08-06: **81.7%**. `scripts/validate_transitions.py --log`. **Re-measured 2026-08-11 directly on the 4 real clean sessions: ~45-50%** (the 81.7% figure was on the since-archived, less-representative old dataset).

  **Bug found and fixed, same day, step 2 of the Scope #1 roadmap.** `components/recorder/recorder.py`'s `_derive_action_from()` (shared by the live recorder and `scripts/backfill_actions.py`) unconditionally returned `action_type="keyboard"` whenever any raw stroke existed, even when every stroke was ignore-listed or had a blank/malformed `key` value and produced zero real text. `validate_transitions.py`'s own docstring is explicit that a no-effect action "would mistrain the model on a transition that didn't really happen" — exactly this case.

  **A first draft of the fix was too broad and caught a real regression before shipping**: suppressing the keyboard classification for any stroke group with no typed text also turned a legitimate lone Tab press into `noop` — verified directly by calling `_derive_action_from({'key': 'tab'})` in isolation before committing. Tab genuinely moves focus on its own; `check_transition()` already correctly validates empty-text keyboard actions via a focus/value-changed check, so navigation keys (Tab, arrows, Escape, Page Up/Down, Home/End, Insert/Delete, F-keys) needed to keep working exactly as before. Narrowed the fix to only suppress when every stroke is either a blank/malformed `key` value or a bare, standalone-meaningless modifier (Shift/Ctrl/Alt/Win/Caps Lock) — verified all 7 relevant cases directly (blank key, lone Shift, lone Tab, lone arrow, normal typed char, Shift+letter combo, lone Backspace) before writing tests.

  **Real impact, far bigger than expected**: re-derived actions on all 2,974 clean steps with the fixed logic — **896 steps (30.1%) changed**, every single one matching the exact bug pattern (`keystrokes: ['']`, an empty/malformed key value, reclassified `keyboard`→`noop`). Re-measured transition-mapping accuracy on all 4 sessions: **63.6% / 70.9% / 65.5% / 61.8%** (aggregate 1,327/2,074 = **64.0%**), up from ~45-50% before the fix — still short of the 90% target, but this one fix closed most of the gap on its own.

  Regression tests: `tests/test_derive_action_empty_keystrokes.py` (16 new tests) — blank/malformed keys and bare modifiers become `noop`; every navigation key still produces a real keyboard action (the exact regression class caught pre-ship); normal typing, Shift+letter combos, Space, Enter, and Backspace are all unaffected; falls through correctly to a real click or clipboard paste when strokes are garbage but something else happened. Full suite: **635 passed** / 9 skipped / 0 failures — second fully clean run in a row this session.

### Learning
Objectives 3 & 5 (thesis): ≥90% action prediction accuracy (training + held-out eval).

- [x] `learning_fixation_solved` — Empty-field fixation solved (2026-06-12): `'attempted'` state-feature (ELEM_FEATURES 394→395) — once a field is acted on this session, the transformer stops re-targeting it.
- [x] `learning_double_type_fixed` — `'99'` double-type fixed: typing is idempotent (select-all before paste).
- [x] `learning_training_metrics_wired` — Per-epoch/total training time + throughput now logged (`transformer_training_log.jsonl`), 2026-08-06.
- [x] `learning_stale_checkpoint_fixed` — `model.pt` was stale (trained for 394 elem features; code encodes 395 since the `'attempted'` fix — drifted, never retrained, crashed every live run on Step 1). Retrained on GPU 2026-08-06 against the current 19 sessions: epoch 65/80, `val_acc=68.4%`, `val_click_acc=30.9%`.
- [ ] `learning_scroll_head_dead` — `TransformerAgentNetwork.scroll_head` is defined but never wired into `forward()` or the loss — scroll has no learnable target/magnitude, only a bare type-label. Found 2026-08-06 during the navigation investigation. *requires: `scope1_rerecord_50k`* (nothing to wire a loss against without real scroll examples — 0/11,062 prior steps had one).
- [ ] `learning_action_accuracy_90` — Action prediction accuracy ≥90% (train + held-out). **Cleared for the first time 2026-08-12** via the semantic action space port (see `learning_semantic_action_space_port` below): **91.6% val_click_acc**, isolated A/B, identical 3,987-step dataset that scored 83.0% under the legacy scheme earlier the same night. Not yet marked done — this is a TRAINING metric on held-out data, not a live-run confirmation, and the checkpoint lives in `tasks/form_filling/experiments/`, not deployed as `model.pt`, pending the user's decision on whether/when to promote it. Historical trail: 30.9% (`learning_stale_checkpoint_fixed`'s retrain, data-quality-limited) → 68.9-83.2% across this session's data-volume and bug-fix work (legacy scheme's practical ceiling, per `_merge()`'s own comments about the collapsed action-type head) → 91.6% (new representation, same data).
- [ ] `learning_semantic_action_space_port` — **Ported 2026-08-12, direct request** ("I need to fix the problem that supports generalization along with not bothering the Transformer through deterministic means" → "What does the verb-loop-rewrite fix?" → "Please do that"). Root question: tonight's own bug-fixing pattern (Texas dropdown, checkbox uncheck, leave-blank reclick, combobox give-up counter — all found live in one session) kept needing the SAME class of fix applied twice, to duplicate deterministic code paths in `agent.py` that never shared logic — a real, structural cost of hand-coded scaffolding compensating for a weak model rather than the model itself getting better. `origin/verb-loop-rewrite` (diverged from master 2026-07-10, never merged, 135 commits, real 16-day effort) built a genuinely different fix for the same underlying cause: a richer action vocabulary (`Verb.FOCUS/SET_VALUE/SELECT_OPTION/TOGGLE/INVOKE/SCROLL_TO/HOTKEY/WAIT/VERIFY/DONE` instead of the legacy `click/keyboard/hotkey/scroll`) with split pointer heads, reporting `click_acc 0.957` vs its own legacy baseline's `0.878` — on ITS OWN, unverified-on-ours dataset at the time of the claim.

  Ported the vocabulary/labeling layer first, additively (`semantic_action.py`'s `Verb`/`SemanticAction`, `action_labeler.py`'s offline demo-step translator) — confirmed against all 5 real `eight_Tabs` sessions before touching training at all (zero errors, full 1:1 step correspondence, sensible verb histograms). Then wired `action_space="legacy"|"semantic"` through `TrajectoryDataset`/`train()`/`predict()` as a NEW, opt-in option — the "legacy" default is byte-for-byte unchanged. Key finding while doing this: `TransformerAgentNetwork` needed **zero architecture changes** — `num_actions` was already a constructor parameter, and the `click_elem`/`source_elem` pointer heads are RE-PURPOSED by verb identity instead of by action-type identity (FOCUS/SET_VALUE reuse the `source_elem` head as a typing-target pointer), not duplicated — confirmed by reading the class before touching it, not assumed. `predict()`'s one seam back to legacy dict shape (`SemanticAction(verb=...).to_legacy_dict()`) means **`agent.py` needs zero changes for either mode** — it always receives the same `action_type`/`click_elem_idx`/`click_position` shape regardless of which scheme trained the model.

  Deliberately scoped OUT of this pass: `disambiguate_attempted`/`rare_weight_basis`-by-field are not yet made semantic-aware (raises `NotImplementedError` if combined with `class_weight_mode != "none"`) — a correct, simple baseline first, reconciling every one of tonight's other independent `transformer.py` features is real follow-up work once the baseline's worth pursuing further, not a same-night requirement. Also flagged, not fixed: semantic mode isn't fully LIVE-deployable yet — `agent.py`'s `self._history` stores `action_type` as a legacy string (since that's what `predict()` returns for compatibility), so a multi-step live run's history-reconstruction would feed the model's OWN action-type embedding legacy ids instead of verb ids, a real but separate gap from the TRAINING-time result below (which never goes through that round-trip — `TrajectoryDataset.__getitem__` builds `past_types` directly from correctly-encoded verb ids).

  **Isolated A/B, identical 3,987-step/5-session dataset and hyperparameters as tonight's legacy retrain (d_model=128, num_layers=4, dim_feedforward=256, epochs=80, GPU)**: **best val_click_acc = 91.6%** (epoch 62/80) vs. the legacy scheme's **83.0%** on the exact same data, same night. The first checkpoint all session to clear the thesis's own ≥90% target for Objectives 3/5. Saved to `tasks/form_filling/experiments/model_semantic_action_space_ab.pt`, deliberately NOT promoted to the live `model.pt` — that's a real decision (which model backs the next live run) left to the user, not made unilaterally. `predict()` round-trip verified directly against this real checkpoint (clean legacy-shaped output, `agent.py`-compatible). 11 new tests (`tests/test_semantic_action_space.py`).

  **Live-deployability gap closed, 2026-08-13, direct request** ("Fix that and lets do a live test run"). Root cause: `predict()`'s history-encoding loop converted each `self._history` entry's `action_type` through `_ACTION_IDS` — a legacy string→id table — regardless of which scheme trained the model. `agent.py`'s history always stores the legacy-collapsed string (`predict()` always returns a legacy-shaped dict via `to_legacy_dict()` for cross-mode compatibility), so a semantic model reading its own history back was fed LEGACY ids (0-6) into an embedding trained on VERB ids (0-9) — and the two schemes numerically collide (legacy `CLICK=1` == verb `SET_VALUE=1`, legacy `KEYBOARD=2` == verb `SELECT_OPTION=2`), so this wasn't just imprecise, it was silently *wrong*, feeding the model a plausible-looking but incorrect signal about its own recent actions.

  Fixed by having `predict()` additionally return the raw verb string (`result["verb"]`, already computed for the legacy-dict conversion, just not persisted) and having `agent.py`'s `self._history.append(...)` stamp it onto each entry (`"verb": prediction.get("verb")`, `None` for legacy-mode models — purely additive, no existing reader of `self._history` touches the new key). `predict()`'s history loop now uses `item["verb"]` directly via `VERB_TO_ID` when present; only falls back to the lossy `SemanticAction.from_legacy_dict(item).verb` reconstruction for older-shaped or mixed-mode history entries. Legacy mode's own encoding path is untouched (still `_ACTION_IDS`, still exercised in isolation by its own test). 2 new regression tests (`tests/test_semantic_action_space.py::TestPredictHistoryUsesVerbSpace`) pin the exact collision this fix prevents — one asserts the verb field wins over a deliberately-mismatched legacy `action_type` (would silently give the wrong id if the bug were still present), one confirms legacy-mode history encoding is bit-for-bit unaffected. Full suite: **737 passed, 9 skipped, 0 failed** (2 net-new tests; one `test_ghost_overlay.py` timing flake seen once in a prior full-suite run reproduced clean in isolation and did not recur on the next full run).

  Semantic mode is now genuinely live-deployable — a multi-step run reads its own history back in the same vocabulary it trained on. Still not promoted to `model.pt`; still not verified against a real live run.

  **Action-TYPE accuracy investigated 2026-08-13, live-run-driven** ("How do we fix the verb-type and wasted step call?"). This checkpoint's own action-type (verb) accuracy was only **51.8%** despite click_elem/source_elem pointer accuracy being 91.6%/90.9% — a real per-verb confusion breakdown against the trained checkpoint's held-out val split (not assumed) found `focus` (24.1% acc) and `set_value` (63.3% acc) — the two most important verbs for this task — both dominated by misclassification AS `hotkey`.

  First hypothesis (Tab/Shift+Tab getting mislabeled as `HOTKEY` instead of `FOCUS`) was implemented, then checked against real data BEFORE spending a retrain on it: **0 of 3987 real recorded steps ever used a literal Tab press** — every field-to-field move in this project's actual demos was a direct click, already correctly labeled. Kept the fix anyway (harmless, correct in principle for future recordings) but it was inert on this dataset.

  Second, verified hypothesis: `backspace` mid-edit correction was **455 of 470 real hotkey-labeled steps (97%)** across all 5 sessions — the actual driver. Reclassified lone `backspace`/`delete` from `HOTKEY` to `SET_VALUE` (target = the currently-focused field, empty value — a correction, not new text) in `action_labeler.py`. Verified against real data before retraining: dataset-wide histogram shifted `hotkey` 491→36, `set_value` 1182→1637, exactly the ~455 steps expected, confirming the relabel did what it was supposed to. Bumped `TrajectoryDataset`'s cache-version tag (`v10_semantic_action_space` → `v11_backspace_setvalue_fix`) so no stale pre-fix pickle could get silently reused. 13 new tests (`tests/test_action_labeler_tab_backspace.py`).

  **Isolated A/B, identical hyperparameters/dataset as the original semantic port's A/B — RESULT: NEGATIVE, not adopted.** Same metric, same click_acc-based checkpoint-selection criterion: type accuracy **51.8% → 46.1%**, click accuracy **91.6% → 90.5%** — both went down. The labeling change was correct by inspection and verified against real data, but didn't translate into better learned structure; plausible cause, not confirmed: collapsing "value got longer" (typed char) and "value got shorter, no new text" (backspace) into one `SET_VALUE` class may have made that class *noisier* rather than cleaner, since both now share the same near-zero aux signal for very different edits. Also can't fully rule out this project's own previously-documented finding that single-run comparisons on this ~4k-sample dataset carry real run-to-run variance (`nn.TransformerEncoderLayer` isn't bit-for-bit deterministic on CUDA even with a fixed seed) — a ~5-point swing is inside the range already seen from noise alone in earlier experiments this project ran. Saved to `tasks/form_filling/experiments/model_semantic_backspace_fix_ab.pt`, **not promoted anywhere**. The Tab/Shift+Tab and Backspace/Delete label fixes themselves are kept in the codebase (correct on their own terms, validated against real data, zero regression risk to legacy mode) — just not proven to help THIS specific metric on THIS dataset size.
- [x] `learning_embedding_hash_fallback_fixed` — **Root-caused why val_click_acc plateaued at ~29-31% across three differently-sized training runs in one session (2,947 / 6,401 / 13,609 samples) — size was never going to fix this.** `_embed_text()` (`transformer.py`) has two documented paths: a real sentence-transformer embedding, and a SHA256-hash fallback with zero semantic structure — but the function that primes the real embeddings, `_prime_embed_cache()`, was defined and never called anywhere in the pipeline. Every element label in every training run to date, ever, was encoded via the hash fallback: 384 of the model's 395 input features per element were content-addressed noise, not text semantics. Verified directly: `sim(embed("First Name"), embed("Last Name"))=0.79` vs `sim(embed("First Name"), embed("Submit"))=0.22` under the real model — meaningful structure the hash path cannot produce (hash similarity is ~uncorrelated regardless of label relatedness). This directly undercuts the "data-quality-limited, not architecture-limited" assumption baked into this task's own note above, and into `scope1_rerecord_50k`'s framing generally — a chunk of the plateau was architectural the whole time. Fixed: new `TrajectoryDataset._prime_all_embeddings()` collects every unique element label across the dataset and batch-embeds them via the real model before tensor encoding starts, wired into both the cache-hit and fresh-build code paths. Smoke-tested end-to-end on the 6-session batch (433 unique labels, ~157s one-time cost including model load) — confirmed the same real similarity structure flows through the actual dataset, not just the standalone check.

  **Validated 2026-08-07 with a real retrain, same dataset as the pre-fix small-batch run (2,947 samples) for a clean A/B**: val_click_acc **29.8% → 61.2%** (epoch 45/50, val_acc 84.5%), roughly double, the only variable changed being this fix. This is the largest single-change improvement this project has seen and directly falsifies the "data-quality-limited, not architecture-limited" framing this task and `scope1_rerecord_50k` both carried into today. Still short of the 90% target — next step is likely re-running the full combined dataset (13,609 samples) with the fix now active, since data volume may finally start helping now that the representation problem is fixed (not run yet, deliberately, given how many long retrains happened today already).
- [x] `learning_val_click_acc_inflated` — **The reported 61.2% val_click_acc (from `learning_embedding_hash_fallback_fixed`'s validated retrain) was not honest — found and fixed 2026-08-07.** Built `scripts/eval_click_by_tab.py` to break click accuracy down per-tab, and its first run came back 41.1% — a huge, suspicious gap from the training-reported 61.2% on what should've been the same checkpoint. Root-caused in two layers:
  1. **Eval-script bug (mine)**: it rebuilt the validation split from the *current* `data/demos/eight_Tabs` folder, which has grown to 7 sessions since the training run happened (the ongoing recording campaign keeps adding sessions) — same `seed=42` on a differently-sized dataset produces a completely different, meaningless split, not a reconstruction of the model's actual held-out set. Fixed by isolating (via directory junctions) the exact 5-session/2,947-sample snapshot the checkpoint trained on. Re-run: **48.1%**, not 41.1%.
  2. **Real bug in `train.py`/`transformer.py`**: `TrajectoryDataset` is a single shared instance across `train_ds`/`val_ds` (`random_split` only splits indices), and `__getitem__` applied `aug_drop_prob`'s element-dropout + shuffle unconditionally — so the "held-out" validation pass was scored on the same randomly-corrupted inputs as training, not clean ones. Randomly hiding ~10% of on-screen fields makes the click choice *easier* (fewer confusable candidates), which silently inflated every val_click_acc this project has ever reported. Fixed: new `TrajectoryDataset._eval_mode` toggle, set `True` only around the val_loader epoch pass in `train()`, so validation now sees the same clean state the model gets live. Regression test: `tests/test_val_augmentation_disabled.py`.

  **The model's true, deployment-relevant click accuracy is ~48%, not 61%.** Per-tab on the correct snapshot: Policy 67.9% (19/28), Policyholder 50.6% (40/79), Vehicle 45.2% (33/73), Coverage/Drivers/Claims/Payment 44.4% (63/142). Confusions are overwhelmingly between adjacent/similarly-named fields in the same tab ("DL Expiration"→"DL Issuing State", "Effective Date"→"Policy Term", "Comprehensive Deductible"→"Collision Deductible") — the model has the right neighborhood, not fine precision.

  **Decision**: retrain immediately on the full current `eight_Tabs` folder (7 sessions, ~2x the data used for the 48.1% checkpoint) with the val-augmentation fix now active, so the next reported number is both bigger-data and honest. Launched 2026-08-07, same hyperparameters as the original run (`d_model=64`, `epochs=50`, `batch_size=16`, `val_split=0.15`, `aug_drop=0.1`) — data volume is the only variable changed, to isolate its effect cleanly. Not yet verified live.

  **CORRECTION, same day, retrain finished**: the retrain's own clean val_click_acc came back 57.8% — an improvement over 48.1%, so at first glance it looked like the data-volume bet paid off. But cross-checking `eval_click_by_tab.py`'s per-tab number against `train.py`'s own reported metric for the *same checkpoint* gave 37.1% vs 57.8% — a huge, suspicious mismatch that shouldn't exist once the aug-bug was fixed on both sides. Root-caused to **two more bugs, both in `eval_click_by_tab.py` itself, not the model**: (1) it read the trace's raw top-level `"action"` field (string type like `"drag"`, pixel-space coordinates) instead of re-deriving history via `_decode_actions()` like training does — fed the model an action-type id (`drag=6`) it never saw during training, since `_decode_actions` always collapses drag/double_click into `CLICK=1` first; (2) far bigger — it never stamped the `attempted` feature (has this field already been acted on this session) onto any element, since that flag doesn't exist in raw trace JSON and is synthesized only inside `TrajectoryDataset` at load time. Every element in every evaluated state looked like a first-ever visit. This is the exact feature `learning_fixation_solved` added to stop empty-field re-fixation — silently absent from every eval this script had ever run. Fixed both (reusing `TrajectoryDataset`'s own precomputed `_attempted_by_file` map + calling `_decode_actions` directly) — the fixed script's number for the new checkpoint now matches `train.py`'s own report exactly (57.8% both ways), first time the two have agreed. Full details + fix: `execution` section, or see the commit fixing `scripts/eval_click_by_tab.py`.

  **Re-measured the ORIGINAL 2,947-sample checkpoint with the now-fixed script: 68.9%, not 48.1%.** So the honest comparison is **68.9% (5 sessions) vs. 57.8% (6 sessions) — the data-volume retrain made accuracy WORSE, not better.** Likely cause: the added data isn't evenly "more of the same" — one single session (`session_20260806_203555`, 3,135 of 6,079 total samples, over half the entire new training set) also has a `drag`-action rate of ~20% vs. near-0% in four of the other five sessions (one other session, `194340`, shares the same elevated rate) — a real, concrete composition lead, not yet confirmed as the root cause. **Action taken**: reverted `model.pt` to the original 68.9% checkpoint (`git checkout --`, safe — the worse checkpoint was never committed). `learning_state_encoder_split_projection` below is now queued against this restored 68.9% baseline, not the regressed one. Before trying "more data" again, the oversized/high-drag-rate session should be inspected for quality before blindly folding it back in.
- [x] `learning_state_encoder_split_projection` — **Tested 2026-08-07, RESULT: NEGATIVE — reverted.** Hypothesis: the confusion pattern in both checkpoints' per-tab breakdowns is overwhelmingly same-neighborhood, similar-label mixups ("DL Expiration"→"DL Issuing State", "Comprehensive Deductible"→"Collision Deductible") — text embeddings for these pairs are close by construction (they share words), while bbox position is what actually distinguishes them, and `StateEncoder`'s single shared `nn.Linear` over the full 395-dim vector (11 structured + 384-dim embedding) let the embedding dominate by sheer dimensionality. Tried: separate linear projections for structured features and the embedding, each a fixed share of `d_model`. **Trained in isolation against the exact 68.9%-baseline dataset, same hyperparameters otherwise: best val_click_acc 45.0%, never approached 68.9% through all 50 epochs.** The position-vs-embedding-dilution hypothesis wasn't the (or wasn't the whole) root cause. Reverted `StateEncoder` to the single-projection version; old tests deleted (code no longer exists to test).
- [x] `learning_rare_field_weighting_and_repeated_label_fix` — **Two fixes landed together 2026-08-07, then a live user report forced a closer look. RESULT SO FAR: negative when bundled, isolation test in progress.**
  1. Rare-action loss weighting only balanced by control TYPE (edit box vs button vs tab), not individual field identity — two edit-box fields of very different real-world frequency (e.g. a name field on every record vs. an admin field on few) got equal loss weight. Changed to weight by the clicked element's specific identity (`_attempt_key`), which subsumes type-level weighting.
  2. **Bigger, directly reported by the user live**: "Driver 1's First Name and Driver 2's First Name being mixed up and being indistinguishable along with similar input names." Confirmed in raw trace data — every driver block labels its name field just "First Name," no qualifier. `_attempt_key` used the label alone as identity, so filling Driver 1's First Name marked the shared key `"first name"` as attempted — Driver 2's First Name (different, still-empty element) then silently read as already-done and got skipped. Real functional bug in both `transformer.py` (train-time) and `agent.py` (live-inference, a second copy of the same logic that has to stay in sync) — not just an accuracy gap. Fixed by disambiguating same-labeled elements by rank among same-labeled elements in list order (stable under scroll, general fix for any repeated-section UI, not hardcoded to drivers). Regression tests: `tests/test_rare_field_weighting.py`, `tests/test_attempt_key_disambiguation.py`.

  **Trained both together against the 68.9% baseline dataset: 43.2% — worse, not better.** Bundling two changes in one test was a discipline mistake (violates "one variable at a time," which held for every other experiment tonight) — can't tell which one hurt, or whether both did. Prime suspect: field-level weighting creates much more extreme per-sample weight variance across dozens of distinct field labels in only ~2,947 samples than the old type-level scheme did, plausibly destabilizing training. Disambiguation alone (type-level weighting restored) was separately tested: **46.9%** — also worse than baseline, confirming the regression wasn't only about weighting.

  **True isolation, done 2026-08-08.** The two changes were structurally intertwined: both the "attempted" input feature and the loss-weighting key were computed through the same `_attempt_key(elem, elements=...)` call, so there was no way to change one without the other. Added two independent `TrajectoryDataset`/`train()` parameters: `disambiguate_attempted` (bool, default `False`) controls only the "attempted" feature; `rare_weight_basis` (`"none"|"type"|"field"`, default `"type"`) controls only the loss-weighting key. Defaults reproduce the pre-7999efc7 (historical, non-regressed) configuration exactly. Regression tests: `tests/test_rare_weight_basis_toggle.py`.

  **Before trusting any comparison, tried to reproduce the 68.9% baseline exactly as a sanity check — found two unrelated real bugs along the way, both fixed:**
  1. `TrajectoryDataset` stored file paths in its on-disk pickle cache exactly as passed to `data_dir`. A dataset built from one working directory, then its cache reloaded by a process running from a *different* working directory, silently resolved every cached relative path against the wrong cwd — `_load_trace()` swallows the resulting `FileNotFoundError` and returns `None`, which becomes an empty `{}` state. No crash, no error — an entire training run silently corrupted into empty-state noise (hit this directly: ~5,900 skipped files, essentially the whole dataset). Fixed by resolving `data_dir` to absolute immediately in `__init__`. Regression tests: `tests/test_dataset_absolute_paths.py`.
  2. `train()`'s checkpoint-saving criterion summed action-TYPE accuracy with click accuracy (`val_acc + click_acc`). ~95% of actions in this task are type "click", so `val_acc` saturates near-ceiling from epoch 1 just by always guessing "click" — after that it's noise, not signal, and it drowned out the genuinely-improving `click_acc` metric. A run saved epoch 1 (`val_acc=0.914, val_click_acc=0.171`) over epoch 44 (`val_acc=0.753, val_click_acc=0.298`) — the *worst* click-targeting epoch in the run beat one of the best. Fixed: select by `click_acc` alone, the metric actually deployed on — it can't be gamed by the dominant action type. Regression test: `tests/test_checkpoint_selection_by_click_acc.py`.

  3. **Third bug, bigger than the first two**: `transformer.train()`'s own Python-level defaults are `lr=1e-4, weight_decay=1e-2` — but every actual training entry point in this codebase (`train.py`, `BCTrainer`, `transformer.py`'s own CLI) overrides those to `lr=1e-3, weight_decay=1e-4`, and `train()`'s own docstring describes `1e-4` weight_decay as "the default" (it isn't, at the function-signature level — a drift between the docstring and the code). Every experiment tonight called `train()` directly without passing these explicitly, so all of them trained at 10x too-low a learning rate and 100x too-strong weight decay — severe, silent underfitting, capping val_click_acc around 28-30% regardless of what else was being tested. Fixed `train()`'s own defaults to match every real caller (`lr=1e-3, weight_decay=1e-4`), so calling it directly no longer silently underperforms the CLI.

  **With all three bugs fixed, re-ran the type-vs-field A/B one more time (same seed/architecture/data, only `rare_weight_basis` differs) — this is the number that actually counts:** `type` basis (control): **49.1%** val_click_acc (peak = saved checkpoint, epoch 42). `field` basis: **43.2%** (peak = saved checkpoint, epoch 44). Control wins by ~6 points — not noise-level this time. **RESULT CONFIRMED: field-level weighting is not an improvement; kept `rare_weight_basis` default at `"type"`.**

  **Where this leaves the bigger picture**: 49.1% is real progress from the 28.3% every earlier run in this investigation was stuck at (confirms the LR/weight_decay bug was a major, genuine underfitting cause, not just noise) — but it's still well short of the historical 68.9% figure, and of the 75%+ thesis target. The gap is no longer a mystery: train click_acc reached 62.9% on this same run while val click_acc topped at 49.1% — a widening train/val gap that's the classic signature of overfitting on a small dataset (~2,947 samples, ~176 distinct fields across 8 tabs, only 5 recording sessions' worth of diversity), not a remaining hyperparameter or architecture problem. More epochs or more tuning on the same data will not close this; more DATA is the next real lever. Also worth noting: this session could not reliably reproduce 68.9% under any tested configuration, which further undermines treating it as a solid target — it may itself have been a favorable, non-representative run (GPU training on `nn.TransformerEncoderLayer` isn't bit-for-bit deterministic even with a fixed seed). **Decision, discussed directly with the user**: don't jump to the originally-planned 50k-frame target (sized without a real scaling curve behind it) — record a moderate next batch first (~2-3x current volume, roughly 6,000-9,000 more samples), re-measure with tonight's now-fixed methodology (correct hyperparams, correct checkpoint selection, correct val-metric reporting), and use two honest data points to decide whether a much larger recording effort is actually justified before committing to one. `scope1_rerecord_50k`'s 50k figure should be treated as provisional pending this.

  **Before the LR/weight_decay bug (#3 above) was found, and matching the deployed checkpoint's exact saved architecture (`num_layers=2, dim_feedforward=128, dropout=0.2` — the `train()` CLI defaults are `4/256/0.1`, a mismatch that caused an earlier failed reproduction attempt), a fresh run could not reproduce anywhere near 68.9%** — best epoch peaked at 30.1% val_click_acc (corrected here: an earlier verbal report of "37.8%" during this same investigation mistakenly read that epoch's *train* click_acc off the same log line, not val — a mixup that also briefly affected two chat responses, both since corrected). Once bug #3 was found and fixed, val_click_acc reached 49.1% on the same data/architecture — most, but likely not all, of the historical 68.9% gap was this bug. **Conclusion: GPU training on this small a dataset (~2,947 samples) also has run-to-run variance too large for single-run point estimates to be tightly comparable** — `nn.TransformerEncoderLayer` isn't guaranteed bit-for-bit deterministic on CUDA even with a fixed seed, and this project has been treating 43.2%/46.9%/57.8%/68.9% as precise, comparable numbers when they may each just be one draw from a wide distribution. This is a new, generalizable methodology finding, not specific to this experiment — future single-variable comparisons should be read as directional, not exact, unless corroborated by multiple seeds.

  **First real recording session added, 2026-08-08 late night**: recorded a new session via the Tkinter GUI recorder (`app/main.py`, sharing the same `DemoRecorder` backend as the Electron one) after two false starts — two earlier "recordings" (`session_20260808_140418`, `session_20260808_142551`, ~2,100 frames combined) turned out to have **zero keyboard actions** and elevated drag-noise rates (16-26%), meaning they were leftover clicks from troubleshooting the recorder itself, not real form-filling demonstrations. Confirmed via direct inspection before trusting them (`grep`-style per-file audit of action-type composition), not assumed. `session_20260808_144216` (645 frames, 285 real keyboard actions, healthy action-type distribution, spans a real 15-minute pass) is the first genuinely usable new session. Retrained on the 5-session baseline + this one real session, same fixed hyperparameters as the 49.1% control: **50.8% val_click_acc** (saved checkpoint = peak, epoch 46) — a real, if modest, +1.7 point improvement. First positive evidence tonight that more (real, well-typed) data helps, after two batches of bad data that didn't.
