  **Re-tested `disambiguate_attempted=True` on this same 6-session/50.8% dataset**, since the original 46.9%-regression result for that flag was measured *before* bug #3 (the LR/weight_decay bug) was found — raising the question of whether that result was contaminated by unrelated underfitting rather than reflecting disambiguation's real effect. **Result: still regresses — 48.2% vs 50.8% (disambiguation off), a real -2.6 point cost under the corrected methodology.** Smaller than the original apparent regression (-22 points under the old, bug-confounded measurement) — confirms the LR/weight_decay bug was responsible for most, but not all, of that earlier damage — but the underlying instability theory (rank-based disambiguation depends on accessibility-tree list order, which isn't as stable a signal as hoped) still holds up under clean conditions. **`disambiguate_attempted` stays at its default `False`.**

  **Second real recording session added, same night**: `session_20260808_152310` (314 frames, 119 real keyboard actions, no drag actions at all, 1 stray empty frame — verified real before trusting it, same audit as the first). Retrained on 5-session baseline + both real sessions (`144216` + `152310`), same fixed hyperparameters throughout: **54.8% val_click_acc** (saved checkpoint = peak, epoch 50; `val_acc=0.779`) — up from 50.8% with one real session, a further **+4.0 point** improvement, larger than the first session's +1.7. Three real, honestly-measured data points now: 49.1% (baseline) → 50.8% (+1 session) → 54.8% (+2 sessions). The trend so far is holding or even accelerating slightly, not flattening — early evidence against "diminishing returns are already kicking in," though still only 3 points, not enough to fit a real curve yet. Consistent with the standing plan: keep recording real, complete passes and re-measure each time.

  **Data quality lesson worth generalizing**: frame *count* alone is not a proxy for data value — verify the action-type composition of any new recording (real typing present? drag/double-click rate reasonable?) before trusting it in a training run, the same way file/session provenance was already being checked. Two batches of high-frame-count, zero-value data happened here specifically because "record more" got interpreted as "produce more logged steps" rather than "produce more real task demonstrations."

  **While more recording continued, tested one independent, non-data lever in parallel: `dropout=0.3` (vs the current `0.2`)**, directly targeting the confirmed overfitting signature (train click_acc well above val click_acc). **Result: negative — 45.7% vs 54.8% control, on the same 7-session data.** Pushing regularization higher didn't reduce the generalization gap, it just starved the model of usable signal on an already-small dataset. Reverted; `dropout` stays at `0.2`.

  **Third real recording added**: `session_20260808_160819` (656 frames, 271 real keyboard actions, only 1 drag — verified real). Per the user, this one file actually contains **3 separate end-to-end form fills** recorded back-to-back without stopping in between, not one pass — so in terms of real *demonstrations* (not session-file count), this batch added roughly 3x a typical single pass, not 1x. Retrained on 5-session baseline + all real data so far: **60.4% val_click_acc** (saved checkpoint = peak, epoch 42; `val_acc=0.718`) — up from 54.8%, a further **+5.6 points**.

  Four honestly-measured data points now: 49.1% (baseline) → 50.8% (+1 real pass) → 54.8% (+1 more real pass) → 60.4% (+~3 more real passes in one recording). Read correctly — as real-*demonstration* count, not session-*file* count — the gain-per-pass looks roughly consistent (not dramatically accelerating as file-count framing suggested), which is the more sensible read anyway. Bottom line unchanged either way: real data keeps helping, no code-level lever found tonight beats it, and `dropout=0.3` (tested in parallel, see above) was a negative result — regularization isn't the bottleneck, volume is.

  **Given that, re-ran the weighting comparison as a same-conditions A/B instead of chasing the old absolute number**: `rare_weight_basis="type"` (control, historical-equivalent) peaked at 37.8% click_acc; `rare_weight_basis="field"` (experiment) peaked at 39.6% — a ~2-point gap, within noise, not a real signal. **RESULT: field-level weighting does not meaningfully help.** Kept `rare_weight_basis` default at `"type"` (unchanged); `"field"` remains available for future experimentation but was not adopted. Not pursuing Submit-specific extra weighting on top of this, since the premise (field-weighting helps) didn't hold up — logged as explicitly deprioritized, not abandoned, in case it's revisited with a larger dataset or multi-seed methodology where the signal-to-noise ratio might be better.
- [x] `learning_training_log_pollution_and_real_demo_proof` — **2026-08-18, direct request** ("I still don't understand how my actions and demonstration can affect the performance of the model... please" → "Fix both right now"). Started from a real, sharp question: is there actual proof demos improve the model, or just theory? Checked `data/output/transformer_training_log.jsonl` — 27 rows, **every single one identical**: `n_train=4, best_val_click_acc=0.396`, spanning three days.

  Root cause, verified not guessed: `transformer.train()`'s per-run metrics log path was hardcoded (anchored on the file's own location, not on `save_path`/`data_dir`) — every `pytest` run of `tests/test_checkpoint_selection_by_click_acc.py` (a real, legitimate regression test with its own tiny 4-example fixture) was silently appending to the SAME real production log. The log meant to prove real training progress had been entirely test noise for three days. Fixed with TDD: wrote `tests/test_train_log_path_isolation.py` first, watched it fail (`TypeError: unexpected keyword argument 'log_path'`), then added an optional `log_path` param to `transformer.train()` (default: unchanged real-log behavior, so `scripts/train.py`/`BCTrainer` need no changes) and pointed `test_checkpoint_selection_by_click_acc.py` at an isolated tmp-path log. Verified with a real before/after line-count check on the actual production log (27 → 27, untouched) across a full pytest run, not just reading the assertion.

  With the log now trustworthy, ran a REAL training pass — `scripts/train.py --trace_dir tasks/form_filling/traces` (all 19 real sessions, 11,064 real trace files) — to a separate checkpoint (`model_verify_run.pt`, deliberately not overwriting the live `model.pt`, so this stays a measurement, not a blind swap — the exact "don't swap without checking" gap this file already flags elsewhere). Real result, first honest number this project has had: **click accuracy climbed from 21.5% (epoch 1) to 52.5% (best, epoch 33)** on 6,401 real training examples / 1,129 real validation examples from 18 real sessions (1 of 19 sessions was too short to use). Real, clean log row appended (line 28) — first non-test entry the log has ever actually held.

  **Separately found and explained why "swap in a worse model" showed no visible difference in a live run**: read a real run log (`run_task_20260817_114439.log`) — zero `[TRANSFORMER]` lines, 235 fields filled via `[OPT2] batch fast-fill` (`"no transformer, no LLM, no click"`, added 2026-08-14 per direct request to make filling instant). The trained model only gets consulted for fields a direct cached lookup can't answer with certainty — for a form where most fields ARE certain, most of a run structurally bypasses the model regardless of its quality. Not a bug — a deliberate, already-shipped speed optimization (`execution_llm_fast_path_lookup`/`execution_opt2_fast_fill_known_values`) — but it means demo quality's visible effect is currently confined to the unfamiliar-field minority of any run, which reframes what "the model matters" actually means on this architecture today.

  **Refreshed the stale task-spec ruleset the same way**: `tasks/form_filling/ruleset.md` (loaded into the LLM's system prompt every run as "TASK SPECIFICATION (learned from demonstrations)") was dated 2026-05-26, built from exactly one session. Ran `RuleExtractor.correct()` across all 19 real sessions in chronological order via LM Studio (local, free). First attempt looked like 19 successes (816 chars each) but was actually 19 silent failures — root-caused with a direct raw-call diagnostic (`_call_lmstudio` returning `''`): LM Studio's server was up but had **no model actually loaded** (listed ≠ loaded), and `correct()`'s own honest fallback (`if not corrected: return existing_spec unchanged`) silently returned the same stale spec every time, which a naive "non-empty = success" check in the refresh script wrongly counted as a real update — corrected by checking the file's own mtime before/after each call, not just whether a string came back. Loaded the model for real (`lms load qwen2.5-7b-instruct`) and re-ran: **17 of 19 sessions genuinely updated the spec** (verified via real mtime changes each round); the last 2 (the most recent, largest real sessions) failed for a different, understood reason — 28k and 110k-token prompts against an 8,192-token local context window, not a mystery. Ruleset is now dated today, built from 17 real sessions instead of 1 stale one — still missing the 2 largest/newest sessions, a real, named follow-up (chunking or a larger-context provider for those specific sessions).

  **Built a 4-scenario "foreign situation" test harness**, one obstacle type per file so results are interpretable in isolation, none touching the real files they're copied from (verified via `git status`/`git diff --stat` showing zero changes to originals): `data_entry_intake_FOREIGN_TEST.txt` (Record 1's "Policy Number" label renamed to "Policy Reference #" — tests whether a lookup-miss actually falls through to real reasoning instead of silently failing); `data_entry_intake_UNFORMATTED_TEST.txt` (Record 1's Policy section rewritten as messy prose instead of clean Label:Value lines, same underlying data); `data_entry_intake_MISSING_VALUE_TEST.txt` (Record 1's Email Address left blank on purpose); `car_insurance_form_wx_MESSY_TEST.py` (a full copy — not an edit — of the real form app, with a deliberately clashing/ugly color theme and the Policy tab's field order shuffled; every field's internal control name and UIA label wiring left untouched so it's a genuine "different-looking, differently-ordered, same-meaning" test rather than a broken one; confirmed it launches without crashing via a real subprocess check, visual-only, no simulated input sent). Live testing against these is the user's call, same as every other live run in this project — these are the test material, not yet exercised.

  Also audited the Task Tree's UI/UX section for staleness ahead of the planned Intern UI overhaul (direct request: "fix the Task Tree so that we could know what we're missing"). Found `uiux_workflow_microchip_layout` claiming "not yet started" while two of its three original asks (microchip-shaped task cards, toggleable emoji) had actually shipped under a different node (`uiux_home_tasks_settings_pass`'s EXTENDED round, 2026-08-17) — narrowed its scope to the one piece still genuinely missing (a detail sidebar with session/frame/date info), rather than leaving a misleading duplicate-looking gap in the list. `execution_semantic_click_tier` was checked too and found correctly `done:false` — its own text says "NOT YET LIVE-TESTED against the real form," consistent with this project's standing rule that unit-tested-but-never-live-confirmed work stays open.

  Verified throughout, not claimed: real `node --check`/`ast.parse` after every edit, a real pytest run (5 passed) for the log-isolation fix, real file mtimes (not trusted return values) for the ruleset refresh, a real subprocess launch check for the messy-UI form, `git status`/`git diff --stat` confirming no existing file was touched by any of the new test material.
- [ ] `learning_reasoning_ladder_experiment` — **Started 2026-08-19, on `experiment/reasoning-ladder`, explicitly not merged to master yet — direct request to build and test this as a real experiment before deciding whether to keep it.** Direct question that started this: "when do we stop and think" / "when to switch between reasoning and fast execution." Confirmed via code first, not assumed: the agent had exactly two speeds — fast-fill (skips the Transformer and LLM entirely for fields whose value is already known for sure) and the normal pipeline (Transformer picks WHERE, LLM decides WHAT, every remaining step, always the same prompt regardless of confidence). No tier in between.

  **Design (TDD)**: `_MED_CONF = 0.50` already existed in `agent.py`'s per-step routing block, unused. Wired it up: `_ask_llm()` gained a `deep: bool = False` argument; when `True`, one explicit "take real care" line is added to the prompt. The one call site that matters (`elif self._llm_client and t_conf < _HIGH_CONF:`) computes `deep` from the Transformer's own confidence, already free every step. `deep=False` (the common case) produces a byte-identical prompt to before — proven by a dedicated test comparing the default call against an explicit `deep=False` call. Fast-fill and every confident step stay completely untouched. Full suite: 1223 passed at the time this landed.

  **Live-tested, found a real gap via log evidence, not assumed working.** First live run (standard form, standard 176-field record): zero `[TRANSFORMER]` lines at all — fast-fill absorbed the entire run, so the branch where `deep` lives was never even reached once. Second live run, deliberately using the messy-UI form + unformatted data test file (built earlier this session) to force genuine uncertainty: reached the Transformer 3 times, once at `conf=0.37` — genuinely below the 0.50 threshold — but `deep` still never fired. Traced why directly in the log: that low-confidence click landed on an empty combobox, and OPT2's own pre-existing specialized handler (`"[OPT2] CLICK on empty combobox → treat as FILL"`) resolved it via direct lookup and `continue`d the loop, never reaching the general LLM branch at all. Not a bug in what was built — a real gap between where the confidence check was placed and where low-confidence steps actually go, since OPT2 has several specialized shortcuts (comboboxes, scrolling, tab-switching) that resolve steps independently of the LLM branch.

  **Three options discussed directly with the user before touching more code**: (A) add a confidence check inside every one of OPT2's specialized shortcuts individually; (B) stop watching click confidence entirely and trigger reasoning on ambiguous *value* lookups instead of click position; (C) broaden the trigger at the existing LLM branch to also fire when the agent has already been struggling on nearby steps (reusing the pre-existing `_lowconf_fallback_streak`/`_reclick_streak` escalation counters), instead of adding new checks throughout OPT2. Recommended and chosen: **C** — fewest new insertion points, reuses machinery that already exists, and "the system has already been struggling" is a more meaningful trigger than one isolated number. Explicitly flagged to the user before implementing: C does NOT fix the exact combobox case found in the log (OPT2 still resolves that one directly, same as before) — that gap needs a piece of A on top if it turns out to matter later.

  **Implemented via TDD**: `_deep_reason = t_conf < _MED_CONF or _lowconf_fallback_streak > 0 or _reclick_streak > 0` — one line, reusing two counters that already existed for other escalations (Navigation Protocol takeover, reclick redirects), not new state. A new test (`test_llm_branch_also_considers_existing_streak_state`) written first, watched fail, then made to pass; the existing 5 tests in `test_ask_llm_deep_reasoning.py` still pass unchanged, confirming the `_MED_CONF`/`deep=False` guarantees weren't disturbed.

  **Status, honestly scoped**: on the branch, not master. `experiment/reasoning-ladder` was merged forward with master's Electron UI work so live testing has the current app, not a stale snapshot. Not yet confirmed live whether Option C's broadened trigger actually fires DEEP on a real run — that's the next real thing to try, same as the branch's original confidence-tier code before this round.

  **Two more live-run rounds, direct request: "check most recent logs, did it reason?" (twice), then "find a way to make it fire please."** Neither run fired DEEP, but each failure was concrete and different, not a repeat:

  Round 1 (standard form, standard record): zero `[TRANSFORMER]` lines at all — fast-fill absorbed every field, so nothing ever reached the step loop.

  Round 2 (messy-UI form + the `UNFORMATTED_TEST` data file, meant to force genuine value-uncertainty): reached the Transformer 3 times, once at `conf=0.46` — below `_MED_CONF`, and this time the streak-broadened Option C trigger genuinely should have applied. Still didn't fire. Traced why: `_ask_llm()` had *two* early-return fast paths that run before `deep` is ever read at all — one for "value confidently found," one for "confirmed blank" (three independent lookup attempts, including a `_peek_notepad` raw-text fallback, all agreeing there's nothing). The `_peek_notepad` fallback turned out more robust than assumed — it successfully parsed the intentionally-unformatted prose-style intake text anyway, so even the fields meant to be ambiguous resolved with total confidence. For virtually any field with a real label, one of those two fast paths fires before the deep-reasoning code is ever reached — a much narrower gate than "OPT2 intercepts some clicks" found in round 1.

  **Root cause explained plainly to the user, with the real tension named before touching code**: the "confirmed blank" fast path isn't just a speed optimization — it exists because asking the LLM repeatedly about a blank field used to make it hallucinate (a real, previously-fixed bug, `execution_llm_value_errors`). Loosening it risks reopening that exact bug. Resolution agreed with the user: `deep=True` skips *only* the confirmed-blank shortcut, not the confident-value shortcut above it (a known value never needs reasoning, deep or not) — and this is safe specifically because Option C already made `deep=True` rare (only low confidence or an active struggle streak), unlike the original bug's every-single-visit asking pattern.

  **Implemented via TDD**: `if _fn != "?" and not deep:` — one added condition. Three new tests in `test_ask_llm_deep_reasoning.py`: `deep=True` now genuinely reaches the real LLM call (with the low-confidence banner) instead of the blank fast path; `deep=False` (the default) is byte-for-byte unchanged from before — the exact scenario that caused the original hallucination bug stays protected; and the confident-value fast path is confirmed untouched by `deep` either way. All 9 tests in the file pass; the pre-existing blank-fast-path regression test in `test_ask_llm_fast_path_lookup.py` (guarding the original bug fix) still passes unchanged.

  **Third live round, same request repeated ("did it reason?"), still no fire — but for the already-known reason.** This run's one genuinely low-confidence step (`conf=0.37`, same `'Policy Type'` combobox as round 2's original finding) hit the exact same OPT2 shortcut identified back then — never touched by either the streak fix or the blank-shortcut fix, since both only change what happens *inside* `_ask_llm()`, and this shortcut never calls it at all. The two steps that *did* reach `_ask_llm()` this run were both confident (0.60, above threshold) and correctly skipped reasoning. Nothing new broken — exactly the gap already flagged as out of scope for Option C.

  **Closed that gap, direct request ("let's try that")**: the empty-combobox click-to-fill shortcut sourced its value from a plain `self._lookup_field()` call — no escalation, no confidence awareness, so it bypassed `_ask_llm()` (and `deep`) entirely regardless of how unsure the Transformer was about the click. Fixed surgically: only the *value source* changes when `t_conf < _MED_CONF` or either streak counter is active — call `_ask_llm(state, deep=True)` first and use its answer, falling back to the plain lookup only if the LLM didn't return a usable value. Every other mechanic in this branch (the dropdown-open click, `_open_dropdown_items`, `_mark_attempted`, the `_leave_blank_keys` tracking, the repeat-guard fingerprinting/blacklisting) — each with its own hard-won fix history documented inline — stays completely untouched; this only changes where the value comes from, not how the click/fill itself happens.

  **TDD**: one new test asserting the combobox-fill code references `_ask_llm`, `deep=True`, and the same confidence/streak signal Option C already established — not a new, separate condition. All 10 tests in `test_ask_llm_deep_reasoning.py` pass; the directly-related suites (`test_ask_llm_fast_path_lookup.py`, `test_opt2_fast_fill.py`, `test_payment_tab_oscillation_fix.py`, `test_combobox_keyboard_fallback.py`, `test_opt2_settle_wait_batch1.py` — 111 tests covering this exact branch's neighborhood) all still pass unchanged. This is now the last of the three real gaps found across three live-testing rounds — not yet confirmed live whether this specific combobox case now actually produces a `DEEP` log line.

  **Real bug caught immediately on the very next live attempt, direct report ("Emergency button is not working, what the hell happened?" investigated first, then "Just find a way to make it fire") — the combobox deferral above filled 'Policy Type' with 'YES (check)', a value that belongs to a completely different field ('E-Signature Obtained') and isn't even a valid option for Policy Type's dropdown.** Traced directly in the log, not guessed: `_cbox` (the combobox this OPT2 branch is handling) is matched by the Transformer's click *coordinates* against `state["elements"]`, entirely independent of `state["focused_element_id"]` — the click on `_cbox` hasn't happened yet at the point this code runs, so the OS can (and did) still report a different field as focused. `_ask_llm()` derives "the focused field" purely from `state["focused_element_id"]`, so the deferred call from the round above — which passed `state` straight through unmodified — asked about whatever was *actually* focused, not the combobox actually being filled. Fixed: build a shallow copy of `state` with `focused_element_id` overridden to `_cbox.get("element_id")` before calling `_ask_llm`, so it reasons about the right field. Two new tests (one for the override itself, one confirming a copy is used, never a mutation of the shared `state` dict other code in the same step still reads). All 11 tests in `test_ask_llm_deep_reasoning.py` pass; the same 111-test related-suite check still passes.

  **Separately, the actual "Emergency button" investigation — a real, serious, now-fixed safety gap, not the reasoning-ladder's own scope but found while chasing the "why didn't Stop respond" report during this same session.** `capsule_activity.log` showed zero `"Stop requested"` lines anywhere in the run, yet `agent.py`'s own log showed it repeatedly detected the Electron window holding focus for several consecutive steps, its own message even reading *"treating this as deliberate (e.g. the user reaching for Stop)"* — the agent knew, and kept running anyway. Root cause: the existing streak-breaker fix (`_reassert_form_window()`, from a previous, similarly severe incident documented in `emergency_stop.py`'s own docstring) only ever stopped ONE action — re-stealing OS window foreground — once it detected this. The main per-step loop kept observing and acting regardless, still physically moving the mouse and typing on the form the whole time, still fighting the user for the OS mouse cursor even though window *focus* was correctly left alone. Window focus and cursor position turned out to be two different things, and only the first one had ever been protected.

  Fixed with a new `self._user_reaching_for_stop` flag, set `True` by `_reassert_form_window()` at the exact point it already decides to back off, reset `False` once the form regains real foreground. The main loop now checks this flag immediately after calling `_reassert_form_window()`, before `_observe()` — if set, it sleeps briefly and skips the rest of that step entirely (no observe, no predict, no click/type), re-checking every step until the user either succeeds (the process exits) or returns focus to the form on their own. TDD: 4 new tests in `test_reassert_foreground_streak_breaker.py` (flag starts `False`; set `True` on backing off; resets `False` on regaining focus; a source-level test confirming the main loop's check sits directly between the `_reassert_form_window()` call and `_observe()`, not after). All 12 tests in that file pass, plus the same related-suite check (`test_ghost_overlay.py`, `test_modal_dialog_dismiss.py`, `test_emergency_stop_hotkey.py` — 41 tests total covering this exact neighborhood).

  **Fourth live round surfaced a real data-correctness bug, not another reasoning-tier gap — found directly from following the user's own correction ("Don't lower the apple just to get a reaction, create an actual situation that would warrant the Agent to actually reason").** Rather than lowering `_MED_CONF` to force a trigger (tried once, explicitly reverted — confirmed via `git diff` showing zero change), used the real `FOREIGN_TEST` intake file, where record 1's `Policy Number` is genuinely relabeled `Policy Reference #`. Live log showed `Policy Number` silently filled with `PAI-2026-00442` — record **2's** policy number, not record 1's. Root cause, traced directly: `_peek_notepad`'s raw-text fallback searched the ENTIRE multi-record file with no record-boundary awareness, so a lookup miss on record 1 fell through to the next matching line anywhere in the file — record 2's. No error logged anywhere; this was silent cross-record contamination, not a missing-reasoning case. Fixed with a new `_record_body_and_line_offset(raw_text, record_num)` in `notepad_source.py` (reuses `_parse_records`'s own record-boundary regex, so the two can never disagree), and `_peek_notepad` now scopes its search to `self._record_num`'s own text before falling back to whole-file search for single-record sources. TDD: 3 new tests in `test_notepad_source.py` against the real `FOREIGN_TEST` file (record-scoped search finds the right value; the mislabeled field is confirmed NOT found via a different record's data; a nonexistent record returns `None`), plus 2 source-level tests in a new `test_peek_notepad_record_scoping.py` confirming the fix is actually wired in and the single-record fallback still works.

  **Re-testing with the now-fixed intake surfaced one more shortcut still bypassing reasoning: batch fast-fill's own separate "confirmed blank" path.** `Email Address` (a field deliberately left blank in an earlier test file) got marked blank via batch fast-fill's own lookup-miss check, before the Transformer or `_ask_llm()` ever engaged — a second, independent place where "lookup found nothing" and "genuinely blank" were being treated as the same thing, this time upstream of everything fixed so far. Fixed the same way as the earlier confirmed-blank fix: before either the editcontrol or comboboxcontrol batch branch commits a field as blank, it now calls `_ask_llm(state, deep=True)` once (with `focused_element_id` overridden to the real field being resolved, same override pattern as the combobox-deferral fix) — if the LLM also finds nothing, blank is committed exactly as before with the same `leave_blank_keys`/`mark_attempted`/Tab-only bookkeeping, never re-paid on a later visit. TDD: 4 new tests in `test_opt2_fast_fill.py` (`TestConfirmedBlankGetsOneDeepLLMCheckFirst`) confirming both branches get the check, the check happens before the blank commit, the `focused_element_id` override is present, and the existing blank-bookkeeping guarantee is unchanged. Full suite green, 0 regressions.

  **Live-confirmed the same night**: two subsequent live runs against `FOREIGN_TEST` both correctly resolved `Policy Number` end-to-end — record-scoped search correctly found nothing under that exact label (no cross-record contamination), deferred to a real `deep=True` LLM call, got a real response back in a few seconds, and filled the correct value (`PAI-2026-00441`, matching record 1's own `Policy Reference #`, not record 2's number). Confirmed directly in `run_task_20260819_193906.log`. Both runs were later cut short by the emergency-stop hotkey partway into record 2 — unrelated to this fix, the run itself was healthy up to that point.

  **Separate, real bug found the same night while checking why Task Completion Rate looked low**: direct report ("it did not fill all it was supposed to fill"). Counted record 1's actual field coverage against the source data field-by-field rather than trusting the completion-rate number alone — found 7 fields (Driver 2's First Name, Last Name, Date of Birth, Gender, DL Number, DL Issuing State, DL Expiration) silently never touched at all: no fill log, no "confirmed blank" log, nothing. Driver 3, right next to Driver 2 on the same tab, got all 12 of its equivalent fields filled correctly.

  First hypothesis (a bare-label collision with the Policyholder tab, which shares these same 7 field labels) was directly disproved by reproducing the actual `_attempt_key`/`find_all_visible_empty_targets` matching logic locally with a constructed elements list matching the real scenario — rank-based disambiguation handled it correctly, no collision, in either plausible ordering tested. Good thing this was checked rather than assumed, since a fix built on that theory would have fixed nothing.

  Traced further to the real suspect: `_resolve_field_control`'s caller in the batch loop had `if not _bf_hwnd: continue` with only a `logger.debug()` line on the SetFocus()/NativeWindowHandle exception path, and **no log at all** when `_bf_ctrl` resolved to `None` cleanly (the normal, non-exceptional "couldn't find a matching UIA control" case) — invisible at the INFO level every run log has been read at. This matches the observed pattern exactly: only the 7 fields genuinely ambiguous on screen (shared with the Policyholder tab, forcing the slower position-disambiguation search path) were affected; Driver 2's other 5 fields and all of Driver 3's 12 (fields NOT shared with any other tab) filled fine.

  **Not yet root-caused past this point, and said so directly rather than guessing a behavioral fix**: WHY the control resolution actually fails for these specific fields (a tab-switch timing race, a `WindowFromPoint` hit on the wrong window near the tab strip, a UIA search quirk) still isn't known — the only way to find out was hidden behind that missing log line. Direct request ("There's still a problem with Driver 2 and Driver 3, please change") confirmed doing exactly that first: promoted both the clean-`None` case and the exception case to `logger.warning()`, with the field label and the real value that was ready to write but couldn't be delivered — so the next live run gives a definitive answer instead of another guess. TDD: 4 new tests in `test_opt2_fast_fill.py` (`TestBatchFastFillControlResolutionFailureIsVisible`) confirming both branches log at a visible level (not debug), the exception path is promoted too, the log includes the lost value, and control flow (`continue`) is unchanged. Full suite green.

  **Two more real, smaller bugs found the same night while investigating the Driver 2 gap, not yet fixed**: (1) a genuinely-blank field ("Third Party Name," no third party involved) got the literal text `"leave blank"` typed into it — the LLM answered in English instead of truly returning nothing, and the batch-fast-fill deep-check fix built earlier this session took that English answer as a real value to write. (2) "Third Party Policy" got filled with the customer's own policy number, which doesn't make sense when there's no third party. Both flagged directly to the user, not yet investigated or fixed — logged here so they aren't lost.

  **The new diagnostic logging paid off immediately, live, the same night**: two more real runs against `FOREIGN_TEST`. The first showed 12 fields on the Policyholder tab all failing to resolve their control in a row (Last Name, Date of Birth, Gender, SSN, Marital Status, Occupation, Education Level, Credit Score, Years Continuously Insured, Email Address, Home Phone, Cell Phone) — the very next run showed zero failures anywhere, including both Driver 2 and Driver 3 filling completely. Direct user correction on the pattern read from those two runs: **Driver 2 does not intermittently fail — it is consistently empty**, unlike the Policyholder-tab failures, which really were run-to-run inconsistent. That distinction matters: two different failure modes were being conflated.

  Direct request: **"fix either way also add a way to distinguish similar bare label names."** Implemented section-aware control disambiguation, reusing real on-screen geometry instead of adding a new mechanism: `_detect_section`'s own pane-scanning logic was extracted into a shared `_sorted_section_panes()` helper (so `_detect_section` and a new `_section_bounds()` can never disagree about where a section starts), and `_section_bounds(state, "Driver 2")` returns the (top, bottom) y-range that section's own on-screen pane owns. `_find_uia_control_by_name()` gained an optional `section_bounds` parameter — among several same-named UIA matches (e.g. "First Name" shared by the Policyholder tab and both drivers), it now prefers whichever candidate's own position genuinely falls inside the target section's range, and only falls back to the pre-existing plain nearest-distance ranking when nothing qualifies (so this can only ever narrow the choice toward the right section, never make resolution worse than before). `_resolve_field_control()` threads its caller's already-computed section through at zero extra cost — the batch loop was already calling `_detect_section` once per field for the value lookup; the exact same result now also drives control resolution, instead of two independent signals that could disagree.

  **TDD, in dependency order**: `_section_bounds` (6 new tests in `test_detect_section.py`, alongside `_detect_section`'s own, sharing the same pane/field/state fixture helpers) → `_find_uia_control_by_name`'s new parameter (3 new tests in `test_focus_element_via_uia.py`, including one specifically constructing a stale-bbox scenario where raw distance alone would pick the wrong driver and confirming `section_bounds` doesn't) → `_resolve_field_control`'s threading (3 new tests in `test_resolve_field_control.py`) → the batch-loop call sites (2 new tests in `test_opt2_fast_fill.py`, one confirming both branches pass the already-computed section, one confirming correct ordering). 124 new/existing tests across the four files, all passing; full suite green.

  **Honestly scoped**: this directly addresses the geometry-based disambiguation gap the user asked for, and is expected to fix Driver 2's consistent (not intermittent) empty result specifically, since that pattern matches exactly what section-bounds targets — a same-named control being resolved to the wrong section's instance. The Policyholder-tab *intermittent* failures (a different-looking symptom, still not fully root-caused past "the visible diagnostic logging catches it now") are a separate question this change doesn't claim to resolve on its own; the next live run is what actually tells us whether the Driver 2 gap specifically is closed.

  **Live-tested immediately, direct report: "Driver 2 was still not filled."** The section-disambiguation fix above never even got the chance to run — the new `logger.warning()` from the earlier round (control-resolution failure) never fired either, meaning Driver 2's fields weren't reaching the control-resolution step at all this time. Read the run's own Drivers-tab log lines directly: Driver 2's Relationship/Accidents/Violations/both checkboxes filled correctly, then the batch jumped straight to Driver 3's First Name/Last Name/Date of Birth/Gender — Driver 2's own First Name, Last Name, Date of Birth, Gender, DL Number, DL Issuing State, DL Expiration (its 7 topmost fields) never appeared as targets anywhere in the log — not filled, not confirmed-blank, not warned-about. Zero scroll events occurred anywhere during that tab visit (confirmed via a direct grep, compared against an earlier successful `Scroll-form: UIA SetScrollPercent` event on a different tab in the same run).

  **Real root cause, found by reading `_try_advance_tab`'s own code, not another guess**: `_scroll_form_to_top()` already existed in this file, with exactly the right stated purpose ("so that `_focus_first_empty_field` always starts from the first visible field, not mid-page") — but it was only ever wired into `_try_advance_tab`'s rare "no tabs found at all" fallback branch. The ORDINARY path — clicking the next tab in the tab strip, the thing that happens on every single tab switch — never called it. A long tab (Coverage, scrolled deep down before this switch) left the newly-active Drivers tab's own view starting mid-page instead of its own top, silently scrolling Driver 2's topmost fields out of the initial viewport, never revisited before the tab was judged exhausted.

  **Fixed, tested (5 new tests in `test_advance_tab_scrolls_to_top.py`), full suite green — then REVERTED the same night, direct report: "it got slower, revert whatever the hell you were doing / Driver 2 still not get filled / so many wrong things got filled."** The fix didn't just fail to help — it made things actively worse. Root cause of the regression, not guessed: `_scroll_form_to_top()` performs a REAL physical mouse click (to give the panel keyboard focus), then Ctrl+Home, then several scroll-wheel events — safe as a rare, near-never-hit fallback (its only prior use), but now running on every ordinary tab switch (8 times per record). A stray click landing in the wrong place, or a scroll-wheel event catching a combobox despite the type-exclusion logic meant to prevent that, explains both new symptoms directly: real wall-clock time added on every tab switch ("slower"), and unrelated fields changing value ("wrong things got filled"). Reverted cleanly via `git revert` (commit `c5959b96`, non-destructive — keeps the failed attempt in history rather than erasing it), confirmed zero diff against the pre-fix state, full suite green again.

  **Three real fix attempts for the Driver 2 gap now tried, in order: (1) making the silent control-resolution failure visible via logging — real, useful, kept; (2) section-aware control disambiguation — real, useful, kept, but never actually the mechanism causing Driver 2's gap; (3) resetting scroll position on tab switch — the correct mechanism in theory, reverted because its implementation caused a worse regression than the bug it targeted.** Per this project's own systematic-debugging standard (3+ failed attempts at the same bug means stop and question the approach, not attempt a 4th blind guess): paused, explained plainly to the user (not in code jargon) why a fourth blind fix wasn't the right next move, and proposed a read-only logging step instead — no new mouse/keyboard action, purely reporting what the system sees. Direct approval: "Add it, sure."

  **Added, tested, shipped — then REVERTED again the same night, direct report: "Again, it became slower because of wasted steps... I think were going backwards with this feature."** Checked the real log before responding, not assumed: the diagnostic fired 2,417 times across a 42-step run — far more than expected, because the label-repetition check it relied on also matched unrelated noise (Notepad's own "File"/"Edit"/"View" menu bar items appearing duplicated across multiple accessibility roles in the SAME `elements` list the form's own fields live in — a real, previously-hidden perception detail the diagnostic incidentally surfaced, itself worth a future look). That volume of per-step logging was real, measurable overhead — an estimate this file got wrong; "just a log line" assumed roughly one line per tab visit, not dozens per step. Confirmed the Third-Party-hallucination and Driver-2-empty symptoms in the same log are unchanged, pre-existing issues (Third Party Name filled with the policyholder's own name, "James Arthur Delgado," despite the adjacent "Third Party Involved" checkbox correctly left unchecked — the same class of bug flagged earlier this session, not new), not caused by the diagnostic itself — only the slowness was. Reverted cleanly via `git revert` (commit `ced8b4d9`), confirmed zero diff, tests back to the pre-diagnostic count.

  **Four attempts now, net effect on the Driver 2 gap: two real, kept improvements to a different problem (control-resolution visibility, section-aware disambiguation) and two reverted regressions (scroll-to-top, the field-scan diagnostic) — the gap itself is still exactly as open as when this investigation started.** Explicitly not proposing a fifth automated attempt without the user's direction — this is the point to stop and let the user decide whether to keep investigating this specific gap, change approach entirely, or deprioritize it, rather than keep guessing.

  **Direct instruction to proceed anyway, with all three constraints explicit at once: "fix Driver 2, and not fill things where they're not supposed to be filled, while maintaining the same speed we had earlier."** Attempt five, but built specifically to avoid the exact two failure modes that sank attempts three and four:

  **Driver 2, fifth attempt — same root cause as attempt three (ordinary tab switch never resets scroll position), fundamentally different mechanism.** `_scroll_form_to_top` (attempt three's tool) does a REAL physical mouse click, `Ctrl+Home`, and several scroll-wheel events — the exact thing that caused the regression when it started running on every tab switch instead of a rare fallback. Built `_scroll_form_to_top_uia_percent` instead: reuses the SAME `ScrollPattern.SetScrollPercent()` mechanism already proven live for the downward-scroll case (`_scroll_form_down_uia_percent`), just targeting 0% — ONE native UIA call, no mouse, no keyboard, no scroll-wheel simulation, so it structurally cannot land a stray click or nudge a combobox's value. Wired into `_try_advance_tab`'s ordinary tab-click path in place of the old function (the rare "no tabs found" fallback still uses the original `_scroll_form_to_top` — untouched, unaffected). No artificial settle delay added this time either — a UIA property read/call doesn't need one, and the failure mode of running a beat too early is just "no reset happens for that one switch" (self-correcting, matches the pre-fix baseline), never a wrong action. TDD: 7 new tests in `test_scroll_form_to_top_uia.py` (jumps to 0% when not already there, no-op when already at top, no pyautogui/click/hotkey/scroll usage anywhere in its source, clean failure when no scrollable pane or UIA is unavailable, never raises) + 7 rewritten tests in `test_advance_tab_scrolls_to_top.py` (re-observes and calls the SAFE route with the fresh state, never calls the old risky one, click-before-scroll ordering, a failed reset doesn't fail the whole tab advance, the two unrelated existing branches — last-tab-exhausted, no-tabs-found — stay on their original paths unaffected).

  **Wrong fills — the confirmed, reproducible half of the bug, fixed; the harder half, named honestly as still open.** Live evidence had shown two different bad outcomes for a genuinely-blank field like "Third Party Name": one run typed the literal text `"leave blank"` into the box; a later run invented an actual-looking but wrong value (the policyholder's own name). Only the first is fixed here — reused `_is_leave_blank_prediction` (already proven at another call site, `_merge()`'s click-vs-keyboard override guard) at both batch-fast-fill branches' deep=True LLM-answer acceptance point: a genuine keyboard-action prediction whose text means "leave this blank" (empty, `none`, `n/a`, or starts with `leave blank`) is now rejected before being accepted as a value, falling through to the existing confirmed-blank commit path unchanged (same `leave_blank_keys`/`mark_attempted`/Tab-only bookkeeping). The combobox-click-fill call site (a separate, third acceptance point) was deliberately left alone — a literal "leave blank" phrase can't match a real dropdown option anyway, so it already fails safely there without this filter. TDD: 3 new tests in `test_opt2_fast_fill.py` confirming both branches reject a leave-blank prediction before accepting it, that the reject check is combined with (not a replacement for) the existing action-type gate, and that rejecting falls through to the pre-existing confirmed-blank path. The SECOND failure mode (a plausible but wrong hallucinated value) is a genuinely different, harder problem — semantic correctness, not phrase-matching — and is not solved by this change; said so directly rather than overclaiming.

  **Speed**: neither change adds a blocking sleep or a per-field cost. The scroll-reset is one UIA call per tab switch (8 per record, not per field); the leave-blank check is one already-existing, already-cheap function call added to a branch that already only runs when a field's value couldn't be resolved any other way. 154 tests across the directly-related suites pass; full suite run before commit.

  **Not yet live-confirmed**: whether this actually closes the Driver 2 gap and the literal-phrase false-fill — that's the next real thing to try, honestly, after four prior rounds where log-evidence reasoning turned out to need real live confirmation regardless of how strong the reasoning looked on paper.

  **Live-tested, direct report: "Still could not fill the Driver 2 First Name, Last Name, Date of Birth, etc."** Checked the log directly: identical field pattern to every prior round (Driver 2's Relationship/Accidents/Violations/checkboxes fill correctly, then the batch jumps straight to Driver 3's full 12 fields — Driver 2's own 7 topmost fields never appear anywhere), and — critically — zero `"Scroll-form: UIA SetScrollPercent reset"` lines anywhere in the log, meaning attempt five's scroll-reset either never fired or scroll position was never the actual cause. Five attempts now, same exact symptom every time, regardless of which theory drove the fix — strong evidence the root-cause theory itself (not just each implementation) has been wrong or incomplete.

  **Direct instruction to keep going anyway ("just find a way"), but not with a sixth blind guess.** Added a second, much NARROWER diagnostic than the one reverted earlier for volume: instead of matching ANY repeated label (which incidentally matched unrelated UI noise and produced 2,417 log lines in one run), this checks ONLY the 7 specific field labels already confirmed missing (Driver 2's First Name, Last Name, Date of Birth, Gender, DL Number, DL Issuing State, DL Expiration) — structurally bounded to a handful of lines per tab visit, the exact volume problem that sank the prior attempt cannot recur with a fixed 7-label set. Logs label, type, visible flag, current value, bbox, and whether it actually became a fill target, right when the batch loop's target list is computed — same pure-logging shape as before (no new mouse/keyboard action). TDD: 4 new tests in `test_opt2_fast_fill.py` (`TestBatchFastFillDriverFieldScanDiagnostic`) confirming the exact 7-label set, correct placement (after targets computed, before the fill loop), no new executor/pyautogui calls, and that visible/value/is_target are all reported. 78 tests in the file pass; full suite green.

  **Not yet live-confirmed**: this is the next real thing to try — whether Driver 2's fields even appear in `state["elements"]` at all when the Drivers tab loads, which will finally tell us whether the problem is upstream (perception never sees them) or downstream (sees them, rejects them for some other reason) of everything investigated so far.

  **Live-tested, direct report: "The Driver 2 respective things were still not filled."** The narrow diagnostic finally answered the open question directly, real data, not another guess: Driver 2's First Name, Last Name, Date of Birth, and Gender were genuinely `visible=True`, genuinely `value=''`, yet consistently `is_target=False` — while Driver 3's identical fields, same observe(), were `is_target=True`. That combination can only mean one thing: `self._attempted_keys` already contained Driver 2's computed key, despite those fields never having been touched. A real, confirmed bug, not a theory — `run_task_20260820_223224.log` has the exact evidence.

  **Root cause, found by reading the real UIA data the diagnostic surfaced, not guessed:** every repeated-section field label is exposed by UIA as MULTIPLE elements sharing the identical accessible name — a decorative `textcontrol` label-duplicate right alongside the real `editcontrol`/`comboboxcontrol` (confirmed directly in the scan output: two "first name"-labeled elements per driver, one `textcontrol`, one `editcontrol`; "gender" had four, including an invisible duplicate and the dropdown's own button). `_attempt_key`'s existing disambiguation (added months ago for the original Driver 1/Driver 2 mix-up, `learning_rare_field_weighting_and_repeated_label_fix`) ranks same-labeled elements purely by their POSITION in the elements list. With three sections sharing a label (Policyholder + Driver 2 + Driver 3) and this doubled-up UIA duplication inflating the count per section, that rank position is fragile enough that marking the Policyholder's real "first name" field attempted (after it was correctly filled, several fields earlier in the same run) computed the SAME key as Driver 2's real "first name" field — two completely different, unrelated fields silently sharing one bookkeeping slot. This is a genuinely different, more specific mechanism than any of the five prior fix attempts targeted (label-collision reproduction disproved a simpler 2-element version of this exact idea back at attempt two — the missing piece was the real UIA duplicate-role structure, which no synthetic reproduction had modeled until real data was available).

  **The fix reuses machinery already built, doesn't add anything new:** `_attempt_key`/`_mark_attempted` (agent.py's live-inference copies) gained the optional `section` parameter transformer.py's own copy has already had all along (mirrored exactly, previously just unused here) — when a section is known (via `_detect_section`, the same real-geometry mechanism already used for control resolution), it dominates rank entirely, so two fields in genuinely different sections can never collide regardless of list position or duplicate-label noise. Rank-based disambiguation still applies exactly as before for the vast majority of fields that have no section (non-sectioned tabs) — this only adds a stronger signal on top, never removes the existing one.

  Wired into BOTH `_no_autohandlers` paths that matter (batch fast-fill and single-field fast-fill, confirmed the only two paths ever exercised in every log this whole investigation): `_bf_sec`/`_ff_sec` computation moved earlier so the key itself can be section-aware from the start instead of computed blind and never corrected; every `_mark_attempted` call site (5 in batch, 4 in single-field) now passes the same section; and — the piece that would have been silently missed otherwise — `find_all_visible_empty_targets`'s own exclusion filter (the check that decides whether a field becomes a candidate AT ALL, upstream of the later per-field key) now uses a small section-aware closure instead of the bare rank-based `_attempt_key`, so a field can't be dropped from the target list before ever reaching the later, already-fixed check.

  **TDD**: a genuine reproduction test first (`test_attempt_key_disambiguation.py`, `TestRealShapeCollisionAcrossRepeatedSections`) modeling the REAL element shape confirmed live (duplicate textcontrol+editcontrol pairs across three sections) — proves the failure mode is real, then proves section-awareness closes it; 11 tests total in that file, all passing including the pre-existing rank-based ones (untouched, still pass exactly as before for the no-section case). Plus 7 new source-level tests across both call sites in `test_opt2_fast_fill.py` confirming correct ordering, section threading through every `_mark_attempted` call, and the target-exclusion filter's own section-awareness. 228 tests across all directly-related suites pass; full suite run before commit.

  **Not yet live-confirmed** — the next real thing to try, but this is the first fix in six attempts backed by a REAL, reproduced collision using the actual UIA element shape from a real run, not a theory built on log inference alone.

  **LIVE-CONFIRMED, direct report: "Congrats, you did it. It filled."** Driver 2's fields — First Name, Last Name, Date of Birth, Gender, DL Number, DL Issuing State, DL Expiration — filled correctly for the first time across seven live rounds of this investigation, verified directly in `run_task_20260821_001922.log` (real values matching the intake data: DOB `03/22/1980`, DL `F8821047`, etc.). The Driver 2 gap this whole sub-investigation chased is closed.

  **Two follow-ups from the same report, handled the same night.** First, direct objection: *"why do we have a hard-coded task-specific protocol for the DRIVER itself named DRIVER_PROTOCOL or some shit, we can't have that."* Correctly identifying the `[DRIVER-FIELD-SCAN]` diagnostic (a hardcoded 7-label set — `"first name"`, `"dl number"`, etc. — literally naming this form's own field names) as exactly the kind of form-specific hardcode this project's architecture rule forbids. It had already served its purpose (finding the real root cause above) — removed entirely, cleanly, including its 4 now-obsolete tests. Confirmed nothing else depended on it; the section-aware `_attempt_key` fix it led to stays, since that's a real, generic mechanism, not a hardcode.

  **Second, a genuinely new, separate bug** — direct report in the same message: *"it halted at Drivers and it's not moving."* Because Driver 2 had never filled correctly before, the run had never reached far enough into the Drivers tab's checkbox-heavy tail to hit this. Traced directly in the log: `"Checkbox 'Driver 2 — SR-22 Required' already checked — redirecting instead of blind Tab."` fired repeatedly, even once the step had moved on to a *different* checkbox (`'Driver 2 — Excluded Driver'`) — the redirect logic kept sending the run back to the same field.

  Root cause, found by reading the actual code, not guessed: the "type into a checkbox" intercept has two branches — "should check" and "leave unchecked." The "should check" branch correctly records itself in `self._checked_fields` once handled; the "leave unchecked" branch (added 2026-08-12 for a different bug, `learning_rare_field_weighting...` — see `execution_checkbox_reset_default`) never did, so the exact same "leave it unchecked" decision re-ran every single time that field was re-offered as a redirect target, forever. A second, compounding bug in the SAME code: the "already handled" check on both branches compared against the *bare* label, while the one branch that did bookkeeping stored the *section-qualified* label — a key-format mismatch that alone would have broken recognition even with the missing `add()` fixed. Fixed both: `_flabel_full` (section-qualified) used consistently for the check and the store, in both branches; the "leave unchecked" branch now also adds itself to `self._checked_fields`. TDD: 5 new source-level tests in `test_checkbox_type_intercept_marks_handled.py` confirming both branches use the same key, the missing `add()` is now present, and neither branch's own check/uncheck mechanism was touched. 101 tests across directly-related suites pass; full suite run before commit.

  **Not yet live-confirmed**: whether this closes the halt — next real thing to try.

  **REGRESSION, same night, direct report: "It did not advance to the History tab, I don't know what you did but its not good."** The "compounding key-format mismatch" half of the fix above was wrong, and made the underlying symptom worse, not better. Checked the real log before touching anything again: batch fast-fill correctly handled `'Driver 2 — SR-22 Required'` (`leave unchecked`), then a few steps later `[OPT2] dead-spot rescue` re-focused that SAME, already-handled checkbox, and the run got stuck in the identical "already checked — redirecting" loop as before — this time reached through a different door.

  Root cause of the regression: every OTHER `self._checked_fields` call site in this file (batch fast-fill's own checkbox branch, the single-field OPT2 checkbox fast-fill) reads and writes the *bare* label — correct for this form, whose checkbox labels already bake the driver number in at the form level (`"Driver 2 — SR-22 Required"` is the control's own raw accessible name, not something agent-side code adds). The `_flabel_full` (section-qualified) change applied only to the type-intercept path broke cross-path recognition: batch fast-fill stores the bare key, the type-intercept path then checked the section-qualified one — so a field batch fast-fill had *already* correctly handled looked unhandled here again, the exact same infinite-loop shape as the original bug, not a fix for it.

  **Reverted the key-format change, kept the actual fix.** Both branches now check and store the bare `_flabel`, matching every other site in the file — the missing `self._checked_fields.add(...)` call in the "leave unchecked" branch (the real, necessary fix from the round above) stays untouched. TDD: rewrote the 3 tests that asserted the wrong (`_flabel_full`) behavior to assert the corrected one (bare `_flabel`, matching every other site); the 2 tests confirming neither branch's own check/uncheck mechanism was touched needed no changes. 96 tests across directly-related suites pass; full suite run before commit.

  **Not yet live-confirmed**: whether this actually closes the halt this time — that's the next real thing to try. Separately, `[OPT2] dead-spot rescue` re-focusing a field batch fast-fill had already correctly finished is itself worth a look eventually (it shouldn't need to re-visit something already resolved) — not fixed here, since the immediate regression is what needed fixing first; noted so it isn't lost.

  **Live-tested, direct report: "Did not navigate away from Drivers tab goddamni[t]."** Same symptom, same field (`'Driver 2 — SR-22 Required'`), even after the revert above. This time traced the FULL, unabridged code path (not fragments) from `_merge()`'s call site through to the exact log line firing, line by line, rather than guessing which block was responsible — the earlier two rounds had both assumed the wrong block. The real culprit: `_cb_next = self._navproto.find_visible_empty_target(state, ..., attempted_keys=self._attempted_keys, attempt_key_fn=self._attempt_key)`, an UNCONDITIONAL redirect-target search that runs every time this whole code region is reached, sibling to (not nested inside) the type-intercept branches already fixed — so it runs even when a field is already correctly recorded as handled.

  Root cause: this search excludes already-attempted elements via a *plain*, non-section-aware `self._attempt_key` call — matching every other checkbox-tracking site in the file. But the earlier "Fix real cross-section attempted-key collision" commit's `replace_all` had applied `section=_bf_sec` to *all five* of batch fast-fill's `_mark_attempted(_bf_el, ...)` calls uniformly, including the checkbox branch — even though checkbox labels on this form are already globally unique (the form itself bakes the driver number into the raw label, e.g. `"Driver 2 — SR-22 Required"`) and never needed section-qualification the way bare labels like "First Name" genuinely do. That one over-broad line meant `_attempted_keys` held a section-qualified entry for the checkbox that `_cb_next`'s own bare-key search could never match — so it kept re-offering the same, already-handled checkbox as a redirect target forever, regardless of `self._checked_fields` (a separate, correctly bare-keyed set) already knowing it was done.

  **Fixed narrowly**: reverted section-awareness on just the one checkbox `_mark_attempted` call, back to the bare form — the 4 editcontrol/comboboxcontrol calls (which genuinely need it, proven by the original Driver 2 fix) are untouched. TDD: split the old "all 5 calls get the section" test into two — 4 editcontrol/comboboxcontrol calls confirmed section-aware, 1 checkbox call confirmed NOT. 45 tests across directly-related suites pass; full suite run before commit.

  **Three real bugs found and fixed across this one investigation, each caught by reading the ACTUAL executing code path instead of guessing from symptoms a second or third time**: (1) the type-intercept's missing bookkeeping (kept), (2) a wrong "fix" for a second bug that turned out not to exist, reverted same night, (3) this one — an unrelated, over-broad side effect of the *original* Driver 2 fix, only surfacing once Driver 2 started filling far enough to reach it.

  **LIVE-CONFIRMED, real full completion, `run_task_20260821_134129.log`**: for the first time in this entire multi-round investigation, record 1 went start to finish with no manual intervention — Policy → Policyholder → Vehicle → Coverage → **Drivers (both Driver 2 and Driver 3, every field, no loop)** → History → Claims → Payment → a real Submit click → correctly advanced to record 2 with its own distinct data (`PAI-2026-00442`, not record 1's contaminated value). Driver 2's fields specifically verified correct in this run: `Maria Delgado, DOB 03/22/1980, DL F8821047` — matching the real intake data exactly, filled in one batch pass, no redirect loop, no re-visit. Both the reasoning-ladder's original goal (confidence-gated deep reasoning firing correctly on relabeled fields, confirmed earlier the same investigation) and everything found and fixed in this Driver 2/checkbox sub-thread are now proven working together in one real end-to-end run.

  **Separate, real gap found the same session, direct question ("Btw, are you recording the damn metrics?")**: checked `data/output/run_metrics.jsonl` directly rather than assuming — 66 rows, every single one `task_completion_rate: 0.0` / `action_prediction_accuracy: 0.0` / `execution_success_rate: 0.0`, and **the run that just succeeded above has no row in the file at all**. `run_task.py`'s metrics computation (`eval_metrics.evaluate_run`) lives in a `finally:` block wrapping `agent.run()`, meant to run "always, even on early stop or crash" per its own comment — but that guarantee only holds for a graceful Python-level exit (normal return, `KeyboardInterrupt`, an unhandled exception), and something about how this particular run actually ended skipped it entirely. Checked the obvious suspect first rather than asserting it: `app/recorder_bridge.py`'s `stop_capsule()` already sends the graceful `CTRL_BREAK_EVENT` (not a hard kill), with its own comment explicitly documenting that choice for exactly this reason — so that theory is ruled out, not confirmed. The real cause (which stop path was actually used for this run, and why `finally` didn't fire) is still unknown. Not yet investigated further or fixed — flagged honestly, logged here so it isn't lost.

- [x] `learning_eight_tabs_data_audit_and_83pct_click_acc` — **User recorded 4 new end-to-end passes via the Tkinter GUI recorder (`app/main.py`) 2026-08-09 late night and asked for a retrain. First attempt trained on the entire `data/demos/eight_Tabs` folder unfiltered (11,207 files, 17 sessions) — result was flat-to-negative: `val_click_acc` 0.613 vs the deployed 0.612 baseline (noise-level), `val_acc` actually dropped 0.845→0.784. Did not accept this as a real result and audited session composition before writing it down (per this file's own repeated lesson above about frame-count ≠ data value).**

  **Audit found 13 of the 17 session folders have ZERO real keystroke events** (`keyboard.actions` empty in every file) — including two large ones (`session_20260808_140418`, `_142551`, ~2,100 files) this file already flagged as troubleshooting artifacts back on 2026-08-08 (see `learning_rare_field_weighting_and_repeated_label_fix` above) that never actually got removed from the folder, plus six older 2026-08-06 sessions and four 3-6-file test recordings, none of which were ever real human demonstrations. Only 4 folders are real: `session_20260808_144216` (285 real keyboard events, matches this file's earlier count exactly), `_152310` (119), `_160819` (271), and the new `session_20260809_214316` (600) — 2,974 files total, closely matching this project's historic "~2,947 clean baseline" figure, suggesting that number was always effectively this same small real set. **Moved all 13 no-typing folders to `data/demos/eight_Tabs_pre_typing_archive/` (not deleted) so `eight_Tabs` can never silently re-include them in a future training call.**

  **Retrained on only the 4 verified-real sessions (2,958 samples): `val_click_acc` = 83.2%, the best this project has ever recorded** (previous best confirmed-reproducible figure was 60.4%; the historic "68.9%" outlier was already flagged elsewhere in this file as possibly non-representative). `val_acc` (action-TYPE accuracy: click/keyboard/other) dropped to 54.0%/train 64.6% — investigated rather than dismissed: built a type-confusion matrix (fixed a stale 7-vs-8-tuple unpack bug in `scripts/diagnose_click_predictions.py`, `_sample_weight` had been added to `TrajectoryDataset.__getitem__`'s return tuple since this script was last run) and found the drop is not random noise but a specific, lopsided confusion — real clicks get misclassified as keyboard 79/156 times (49.4% recall), while keyboard is recalled correctly 84.5% of the time. Root cause: the OLD dataset's ~95%-click imbalance (all 13 junk sessions are click/drag/scroll only) let `val_acc` saturate near-ceiling via the same "always guess click" shortcut this file's own earlier LR/weight_decay investigation already named — the clean data's genuinely balanced 47% click / 43% keyboard / 10% other mix removes that shortcut, so 54% is a harder, more honest number, not a regression in real capability.

  **Live-verified 2026-08-10, `run_task_20260810_003743.log`**: real Submit reached (`Stuck guard: last tab exhausted — clicking 'Submit'`), no crashes, and the run's own metrics confirmed the paper number translated to reality — **Action Prediction Accuracy 100% (12/12 clicks on-target)**, Transformer Dependency up to 56.1% (from ~48% the prior run — the model needed the low-confidence fallback less, consistent with genuinely higher confidence, not just a higher number). Checkpoint kept deployed; backup of the pre-retrain 61.2%/84.5% checkpoint preserved at `tasks/form_filling/model_pre_20260809_2200_backup.pt` in case of regression.

  **Decision, not yet executed**: record ~4 more real end-to-end sessions (doubling the clean set from 4 to 8) to test whether the click-vs-keyboard confusion shrinks with more data, the same volume-helps pattern already confirmed for click_acc earlier in this file. Not urgent — flagged as the next real lever, not a same-night task.

### Adaptability
Objective 6 (thesis): ≥75% success rate on unseen GUI environments (layout/position/visual variation).

- [ ] `adaptability_unseen_success_75` — **Proxy instrumented and measured 2026-08-12** (direct user request to find a way to implement this without waiting on a live held-out environment). `scripts/adaptation_proxy_eval.py` takes real recorded states and synthetically perturbs their *presentation* (shuffles `elements` list order; translates every bbox by a random offset, simulating a moved/resized window) without changing the underlying task, then checks whether the model still names the same field it would click on the unperturbed state. Explicitly NOT the literal thesis test — can't probe genuinely novel labels/controls/tab structure, only robustness to superficial layout drift on the known form. Result on all 5 real sessions (1,781 steps): unperturbed baseline **65.9%**, shuffle-only **58.2%**, translate-only **63.3%**, combined **56.7%** — **FAIL vs. the 75% target**. Note the baseline itself (65.9%) is well below training's reported `val_click_acc` (83.0%) because this script scores against ALL recorded clicks (not just the held-out val split) using `(type,label)` identity match rather than index match — a different, stricter measurement, not a contradiction. Logged to `data/output/adaptation_proxy_log.jsonl`; wired into `scripts/objectives_report.py`'s Objective 6 row, clearly labeled PROXY. A genuine held-out-environment live run (sequenced last in the Scope #1 roadmap — only worth building after the model works reliably on the known environment) is still needed for an authoritative reading; that part is on the user per the live-run rule.
- [ ] `adaptability_adaptive_mechanism` — **Added 2026-08-13, direct request, not yet scoped in detail.** `adaptability_unseen_success_75` above only *measures* robustness to layout drift (the proxy eval) — it doesn't give the agent any actual mechanism to cope when the measurement fails, which it currently does (56.7% vs. the 75% target). This task is the real fix: some runtime capability that lets the agent detect its prediction confidence has dropped on an unfamiliar layout and adapt (e.g., fall back to the LLM more aggressively, re-observe before committing, or another concrete strategy — not yet decided). Needs a follow-up conversation to pin down the actual mechanism before implementation starts; logged here so it isn't lost, not started.
- [ ] `adaptability_scroll_gap` — Scroll navigation is a three-layer gap, found 2026-08-06: (1) **data** — 0/11,062 recorded steps ever contained a scroll action (demos relied on Tab-to-reveal only); (2) **model** — `scroll_head` is dead code (`learning_scroll_head_dead`); (3) **agent** — `agent.py`'s scroll-reveal/drought-guard/`_try_advance_tab` block (`agent.py:1589-1630`) runs **unconditionally, "in every mode"** — not gated by `disable_auto_handlers` — fully preempting the transformer's own decision every time, which is the opposite of the thesis's transformer=WHERE division. Fix order: record real scroll demos → wire the loss → fix `executor.py`'s scroll (no foreground-focus assert, likely why `pyautogui.scroll` doesn't move the wx `ScrolledPanel`) → strip the hardcoded agent.py block one change at a time. *requires: `scope1_rerecord_50k`*

  **Reframed 2026-08-07 per explicit user direction, and layer (3) partly addressed — see `execution_navigation_protocol` below.** Rather than wait indefinitely for enough real scroll demonstrations to clone scroll *behavior* (layer 1/2 — genuinely blocked on `scope1_rerecord_50k`), navigation was split out as a system-level responsibility instead: the system's only job is keeping an actionable empty target visible; the Transformer never needs to learn WHEN/HOW FAR to scroll at all. Layers 1/2 (real data, a learned scroll head) remain open for when/if scroll needs to be a genuine model decision rather than a system one — not required for the current fix to hold.

### Execution and Integration
Objectives 8, 9 & 10 (thesis): ≥85% end-to-end completion without manual
intervention; ≤10% execution error rate; ≤20% wasted steps; unified coordinated
framework.

- [x] `execution_max_steps_ceiling` — **Found and fixed 2026-08-07, while pushing toward a real end-to-end attempt after tonight's four live loop-bug fixes.** `run_task.py`'s `MAX_STEPS` was 200 — but the form has 176 fields, and even the best live run tonight needed ~5-6 raw steps per field (typing + navigating + occasional retries) to make progress. Full completion needs on the order of 800-1000+ steps, not 200 — the run was mathematically guaranteed to stop short regardless of how many more correctness bugs got fixed, independent of the transformer's own accuracy. Very likely the single biggest reason no run has reached Submit yet. Raised to 1000 with headroom, not tightly tuned to a computed minimum — the first real attempt at this ceiling will show the actual number needed. Not yet verified live.

  **CORRECTION, same conversation: user correctly pushed back — an earlier version of this project fit a full record into ~200-250 steps, so ~5-6 steps/field tonight was a real regression, not just "the form is big."** Raising the ceiling was treating the symptom. Investigated instead: counted field-lookup frequency in the big live run and found individual fields visited far more than others — "Education Level" 11 times, "Number of Doors" 8, "Occupation" and "Years Continuously Insured" 6 each. Traced "Education Level": `_merge()` overrode a "type" decision into a "click" (comboboxes need a click to open — deliberate, not itself a bug), executed via the generic execution path with NO toggle-awareness. Step 41 clicked to open (6 new elements appeared); step 42's transformer, still confidently (0.97) targeting the same spot, clicked AGAIN — closing what it had just opened (`state changed: +0/-9 elements`) — and a naive close-tracking heuristic wrongly logged it as "filled." Repeated 11 times before accidentally recovering through a different, already-fixed handler. Confirmed the identical pattern on "Occupation" too.

  This is the EXACT toggle bug fixed twice already tonight (`_open_dropdown_items`), but in a THIRD, previously-untouched place: the two dedicated combobox handlers both guard against it; this was the one spot `_merge()`'s own click override reaches the generic execution path, bypassing both. Fixed the same way — check for already-open dropdown items before letting the click execute, select the match directly (or Escape cleanly) instead of blindly re-clicking. 2 new tests, reusing the already-tested `_open_dropdown_items`/`_option_matches` building blocks. This is very likely the dominant cause of the efficiency regression — a handful of comboboxes eating 8-11 steps each adds up fast across 176 fields. `MAX_STEPS` deliberately left at 1000 rather than reverted, since this fix hasn't been live-verified yet and cutting the budget back down prematurely risks truncating the NEXT diagnostic run too early to see the real, now-hopefully-much-lower steps/field number. Right-size it once there's real evidence, not another guess.

- [x] `execution_drift_solved` — Drift solved (2026-06-12): form-window LOCK (capture hwnd at GO, re-assert every step) + in-form click guard. No more typing into PowerShell / clicking Notepad.
- [x] `execution_belowfold_solved` — Below-fold reach solved: the drift guard's Tab doubles as scroll — wx `ScrolledPanel` auto-scrolls the focused field into view.
- [ ] `execution_action_space_big3_2` — **Big Three #2:** Action space, form-fields → universal. Today: click/type/select(+combobox). Need click, double_click, type, select, drag, scroll, hotkey, wait, verify, menu, file-dialog. Enumerable engineering, not research.
- [ ] `execution_control_flow_big3_3` — **Big Three #3:** Workflow, linear → control flow. Demos are one linear path; real tasks branch/loop/skip/error-recover. Pragmatic path: LLM induces the workflow (if/for) from multiple traces → execute → DAgger-correct.
- [ ] `execution_agentic_integration_85` — End-to-end task completion ≥85% without manual intervention. `eval_metrics.py` now tracks `manual_interventions` (DAgger corrections) as a direct signal. *requires: `scope1_e2e_metric`* **Root-caused 2026-08-11**: 14 of the last 15 live runs ended at step 0, manually interrupted — zero real recent data, not a regression from the 2026-08-07 furthest-E2E baseline (30.7% completion, still the real high-water mark). See Scope #1's ROADMAP note above.

  **First real number since that baseline, 2026-08-13**: `run_task_20260813_005301.log`, 29.5% completion, 0 manual interventions (the run was stopped by the user, not by the agent needing help) — below the 30.7% historical high-water mark but the first live data point this session's semantic-action-space/checkbox-fix work has actually produced. See the "four questions" note in Scope #1's roadmap section.

  **FURTHEST E2E PROGRESS TO DATE, recorded 2026-08-07 per direct user instruction:** *"Good, we reached Payment tab, but there was a loop at the end. Take record of this as our furthest E2E."* Run `logs/run_task_20260807_205021.log` reached **all 8 tabs in correct forward order** — Policy → Policyholder → Vehicle → Coverage → Drivers → History → Claims → Payment — with **zero backward tab cycling**, the first time this session that's held all the way through (confirmed via `grep "Stuck guard: advancing to tab"`, one clean forward-only sequence, no repeats). 46/176 fields filled (26.1%), 354 actionable steps, Value Accuracy 76.8% (43/56 typed values correct), Wasted Step Rate 53.4% (still well above the ≤20% target). The run ended in an 18+ step oscillation at the very end between `'Auto-Pay Enrolled'` and `'Last Payment Amount'` on the Payment tab — see `execution_payment_tab_oscillation_fix` immediately below for the diagnosis and fix. This is a baseline to beat, not a finish line — still far from the 85% completion target and the 20% waste ceiling — but the first clean run of all 8 tabs in order, with the accumulating tab-blacklist and checkbox-recognition fixes both holding under real conditions, is genuine, verifiable progress worth anchoring future comparisons to.
- [x] `execution_payment_tab_oscillation_fix` — **Confirmed live 2026-08-07 on the furthest-E2E run above.** An 18+ step oscillation between `'Auto-Pay Enrolled'` (checkbox, already correctly checked) and `'Last Payment Amount'` (editcontrol, legitimately blank — no record value) at the very end of the run. Two independent gaps combined:

  1. OPT2's fill-branch entry condition (`_t_is_type`: "is the focused thing a fillable, empty field?") never checked `attempted_keys`. Fatal specifically for checkboxes: `ui_observer.py` never reports a real checked state via `ValuePattern` (`wx.CheckBox` doesn't expose it), so a checkbox's `value` reads empty **forever**, checked or not — `_t_is_type` was unconditionally `True` for any focused checkbox regardless of attempted state, forcing the full `ask_llm` → `merge` → click dance every single time, relying entirely on a downstream Win32 `BM_GETCHECK` guard to catch the redundant click and convert it to Tab. Wasteful, but not destructive — that guard did correctly stop it from actually unchecking (unlike `execution_filled_combobox_reclick_guard`'s data-corruption risk).
  2. `_merge()` overrides *any* `"type"` decision into a `"click"` whenever the transformer's (collapsed, near-constant-confidence) action-type head says click — regardless of whether the LLM's intended text was real or empty. `_is_leave_blank_prediction` is deliberately gated on `action_type=="keyboard"` (see `execution_combobox_leave_blank_infinite_loop`) so a legitimate combobox click-override is never mistaken for leave-blank — but that same gate meant a genuinely empty answer for `'Last Payment Amount'` never got recognized once merge had already turned it into a click, and the resulting click used the transformer's own ~69%-accurate pointer position, not this field's — landing almost anywhere, apparently drifting back near Auto-Pay Enrolled.
