Found every combobox exposes its actual dropdown toggle as a SEPARATE `ButtonControl` with a real `InvokePattern`, flush against the combobox's own right edge (~19px wide, measured directly) — the combobox control itself only exposes `ValuePattern`, no `ExpandCollapsePattern`. The click-open mechanism has always clicked dead center of the combobox's own bbox, gambling on the whole control responding like a button.

  **Could not find a confirmed root cause for why `'Primary Use'` specifically fails while `'Fuel Type'` (checked side by side, live) doesn't** — both are structurally identical in every property checked: same UIA patterns, same bbox structure, same choices-list length, same widget-creation source code (`self._choice()`, no per-field special-casing). Said so directly rather than force a guessed fix — the same discipline already burned twice earlier tonight (the Tab and dead-class-verb theories, both wrong, both checked before being acted on).

  **What WAS implemented is a real, generalizing hardening, not a targeted fix for the unconfirmed root cause**: target the combobox's own dropdown-arrow position (right-edge offset from its bbox, geometry only, not tied to any field) instead of its center, at both combobox-open call sites (the click-fill branch and the type-into-combobox branch — the same duplicate-code-path shape this file has hit before). Same "use semantic, not precise" principle already shipped for buttons (`executor.py`'s `BM_CLICK` tier) — the existing click pipeline (`_try_uia_invoke`, already tried before any raw coordinate click) now has a real chance to invoke the actual toggle control instead of hoping a center-click lands on something responsive. 8 new tests (`tests/test_combobox_dropdown_arrow_click.py`), including a direct source-level check that neither call site regresses to a bare center-click and that no field-name equality check exists near the new code. Full suite: 786 passed, 9 skipped, 0 failed. Not yet re-verified live — and given the root cause for `'Primary Use'` specifically remains unconfirmed, this should be read as a real improvement worth having regardless, not a guarantee this exact field is fixed.
- [ ] `execution_plan_then_replay` — **Built 2026-08-14, direct request: "unify whatever advantage OUR Intern has with the speed of my coworker's [separately ported Scope #2] work."** The obvious framing — train a model to predict WHAT to type from broad demonstration data, matching the coworker's decide-once-then-replay shape — didn't survive contact with the actual code, checked directly rather than assumed: the transformer (`components/intelligence/model/transformer.py`) has no text-generation head, only pointer/classification heads (`predict()`, ~L1844), so it structurally can't emit arbitrary values the way the coworker's system predicts a column mapping. More importantly, `FormFillerPlugin.handle_step()` (`components/agent/task_plugins/form_filler_plugin.py:105`) already resolves most fields with **zero transformer or LLM calls** — WHERE via UIA `SetFocus`-by-Name, WHAT via `_lookup_field`'s deterministic cache — which is exactly why `execution_llm_call_cost_reduction` (below) was superseded before being built: LLM calls were never the real cost for most fields. What actually costs time is re-deriving the same ~15-branch guard cascade (focus-check, tab-switch, stuck-guard, pane-escape, button-escape, tab-complete-verify, auto-skip, auto-fill+peek+dup-label, auto-check, combobox-fix, drought-guard) from scratch on every single step, one field at a time, even for fields whose answer was already knowable from one glance at the screen.

  **Built instead**: a new pure module, `components/agent/field_planner.py` (styled like the existing `navigation_protocol.py` — state dict in, decision out, no side effects, no pyautogui, fully unit-testable without a live GUI). `plan_visible_fields()` scans every visible, empty, fillable element on the current tab **once** and resolves each to a value via the exact same two-step lookup-then-peek protocol already in `handle_step()` (lines 419-454 pre-change) — only marking a field `NEEDS_LLM` if both attempts genuinely miss, so nothing the current reactive code already resolves for free regresses. `stable_field_key()` mirrors `LLMAgent._attempt_key` exactly — `(label, rank-among-same-labeled-elements)` — since `element_id` is documented position-unstable across separate observations (`state_validator.py:134-150`); this is now a third independent copy of that scheme, cross-checked by a dedicated drift-guard test against the `agent.py` original. `check_divergence()` re-resolves that stable key against the *current* observation before every planned action executes — never trusts the plan-time snapshot blindly.

  `FormFillerPlugin` gained an opt-in `plan_replay` flag (default `False`) and one new early branch in `handle_step()`, inserted at the exact seam confirmed by direct read of the file (after the button-focus-escape block, before Auto-skip). With the flag off, the branch's guard is always false and every existing branch is reached exactly as before — proven by a dedicated regression test asserting `plan_visible_fields` is never even called. When on: a `LOOKUP_HIT`/`LOOKUP_BLANK` `editcontrol` field is focused (via the real `_focus_element_via_uia`, wired onto the plugin through the same `hasattr`-gated pattern already used for `_executor`/`_observe_fn`, `agent.py` ~L1193) and filled via the existing, unmodified `_paste_and_verify` + settled via the existing `_adaptive_settle_wait` — no re-derivation of the cascade at all. A `NEEDS_LLM` field, or any combobox/checkbox (**deliberately out of v1 scope** — `_combobox_needs_fix`'s dropdown state machine has already been through ten documented bugfix rounds, `execution_filled_combobox_reclick_guard`; not worth re-deriving for an unproven speed gain), gets a best-effort focus and defers to the existing, completely unmodified reactive `_predict`/`_ask_llm`/`_merge` path for just that one field. A diverged field (vanished, or already holds something unexpected) is consumed and the step falls through to the untouched legacy cascade. `notify_tab_click` clears the plan so a stale tab-A plan can never replay against tab B; plan exhaustion auto-rebuilds (catches fields that only appear after another field's value changes, without a dedicated detector).

  **One deliberate deviation from bbox as a click target, worth stating plainly**: `PlannedField.bbox` is documented as a plan-time *hint* only. When the real UIA focus-by-name fails, the new branch does **not** fall back to a raw coordinate click — it treats the field as diverged and lets the existing, already-tested reactive cascade (which has its own focus mechanisms) take that step instead. So unlike every other click site hardened this session, no new `_find_destructive_button_at` call site was needed here — there's no new raw click for it to guard.

  22 new tests (`tests/test_field_planner.py`) plus 22 more (`tests/test_plan_replay_mode.py`, constructing `FormFillerPlugin` directly — its first direct unit tests; none existed before this change) covering the off-by-default guarantee, plan build/consumption/rebuild, all three resolutions, all three divergence states, the fallback-helper wiring, `notify_tab_click`'s reset, and the `LLMAgent.__init__` wiring itself. Full suite: 855 passed, 9 skipped, 0 failed — zero regressions. **Not yet live-verified, and deliberately not claimed as a speed win yet**: this removes a real, identified cost (cascade re-derivation, redundant re-observation) — not the cost originally assumed (per-field LLM calls, which mostly weren't happening anyway) — so per this project's own "verify from real logs, don't assert 'fixed' before a live run" rule, a live comparison (`plan_replay=False` vs `True`, same record) is needed before any speedup number goes here. Per project rule, only the user runs live tasks.
- [ ] `execution_direct_fill_wm_settext` — **Built 2026-08-14, direct request following the plan-then-replay work above: "I want it to fill like my coworker's — one-shot and go, no click."** Two dead ends ruled out empirically before building anything, not assumed: UIA's own `ValuePattern.SetValue()` fails outright (`COMError`) on this form's controls when tested directly against the real running practice form; and a subtler trap caught before it could cause real damage — `win32gui.SendMessage`'s own `GetWindowText()` wrapper was independently found to be **buggy** for reading this exact control back (disagreed with two other, correct readbacks), which would have made a genuinely broken write mechanism look like it was working if trusted as the verification tool.

  **What actually works, live-tested against the real running form (read-only/reversible tests, not guessed):** sending a raw Win32 `WM_SETTEXT` message directly to a text field's native window handle, via `ctypes.windll.user32.SendMessageW` (not the buggy wrapper), sets an `EditControl`'s value in one call — no click, no simulated keystrokes. Confirmed correct two independent ways: a raw `ctypes` `WM_GETTEXTLENGTH`/`WM_GETTEXT` readback, and UIA's own `ValuePattern` readback, which agree with each other. Also live-tested against a `ComboBoxControl` and found to be a silent no-op (value unchanged, no error) — comboboxes are excluded and keep their existing click-based handling entirely untouched. Checkboxes already have their own separate, working equivalent (`BM_SETCHECK`) — also untouched.

  **Deliberately not built as a new top-level `action_type`.** A full audit of `agent.py`'s fill path found **seven** separate `== "keyboard"` gates between decision and execution (TextResolver enrichment, the combobox type→click guard, the pre-type focus/clear block, the foreground guard, the settle-poll, and the verify-at-fill retry loop) — not just the two a narrower read would suggest. Introducing a second literal action-type string would mean finding and correctly widening all seven, with a real risk of silently missing one. Instead, two new *optional* keys ride on the existing `"keyboard"` prediction dict: `direct_fill_eligible` (decided cheaply in `_merge()`'s `l_type == "type"` branch, reusing `_tp_fel_ty` — already computed a few lines above for the pre-existing click-override check — strictly `== "editcontrol"`, the one type actually live-tested, not a broader membership check) and `direct_fill_hwnd` (resolved live, only when eligible, at the pre-type focus/clear block: `_find_uia_control_by_name` is called directly instead of going through `_focus_element_via_uia`'s bool-only wrapper, so the same lookup that already focuses the field also hands back its `NativeWindowHandle` — nothing resolved twice).

  New `ActionExecutor._keyboard_direct()` (`components/agent/executor.py`, patterned directly on the existing `_try_semantic_click`/`BM_CLICK` helper — same injectable-`user32` shape, same "return `False` on any failure, never raise" contract): checks the resolved HWND's class name against `_EDIT_CLASSES` (hoisted from `ui_observer.py`, previously a locally-scoped literal on its own read-side `WM_GETTEXT` fallback, to module scope so the write side and read side share one set instead of two independent copies), sends `WM_SETTEXT`, then immediately reads the value back via raw `ctypes` — any mismatch, wrong class, or exception returns `False`. `_keyboard()` tries this first when a handle is present and falls through to the existing, completely unmodified clipboard-paste path on any failure, transparently, in the same call — the caller never sees a difference, `ExecutionResult` shape is unchanged. `dry_run` gates the new path exactly like every other action type (the opposite of a real, separately-found gap: the checkbox `BM_SETCHECK` sites elsewhere in this file call real `win32api.SendMessage` with **no** `dry_run` check at all — noted, not fixed here, since checkboxes are out of scope for this change).

  27 new tests: `tests/test_executor_direct_fill.py` (following `tests/test_executor_ghost_cursor.py`'s established `monkeypatch.setattr(mod, "_user32", ...)` convention — successful direct-fill skips the paste path entirely, class-mismatch/readback-mismatch/exception all fall back to paste, `dry_run=True` never touches real `SendMessageW`, and the no-`direct_fill_hwnd` case is byte-for-byte identical to before this feature existed), `tests/test_merge_direct_fill_eligible.py` (following `tests/test_merge_type_click_override_needs_click_first.py`'s established pattern — `editcontrol` eligible, `comboboxcontrol`/`checkboxcontrol` never eligible, `"input"` deliberately NOT silently included alongside `"editcontrol"`), plus one addition to the existing `tests/test_executor.py` confirming a `dry_run=True` executor's result shape is unchanged with the new key present. One pre-existing test (`tests/test_type_path_focus_via_uia.py`) needed its source-scan window widened (400→1800 chars) after this change legitimately added code between its anchor and the `_focus_element_via_uia` call it checks for — the underlying guarantee it tests was confirmed still true, just further from the anchor. Full suite: 891 passed, 9 skipped, 0 failed.

  **Honestly unverified**: only "Policy Number" (plain text) and one combobox were live-tested this session — the 8-tab form's masked/numeric/date fields and repeated-section fields (Driver 1/2/3) are unconfirmed. Whether a wx validator could reformat or revert a `WM_SETTEXT`-set value on blur (after focus moves away) is untested — this session's readback happened immediately, while the field still had focus. `_EDIT_CLASSES` is inherited from a read-side precedent built for a different app's edge cases (Windows 11 Notepad, some PDF viewers), not independently confirmed complete for every field on this form. Per project rule, only the user runs live tasks — not yet verified live.
- [ ] `execution_opt2_fast_fill_known_values` — **Built 2026-08-14, direct request ahead of a 3-day demo deadline: "don't stop unless there's something to reason about."** The ambitious version — get a whole-page fill order from one model call, informed by demonstration order — was investigated first and does NOT hold up: read `transformer.py`'s actual training code (`TrajectoryDataset`) and confirmed the WHERE-model was only ever trained to predict "what's next given everything that already happened" (via the per-element `attempted` feature, derived from real accumulated history at both train and inference time). A single forward pass's rank-2+ scores are NOT a valid multi-step plan — they're alternate guesses at the FIRST click, since the model has no way to imagine "assume the first field already got filled" without that actually having happened. Real future work (a genuine demonstration-order plan already has a valid, unused foundation: `scripts/bc_fidelity.py`'s `build_behavior_reference()`/`_extract_sequence_from_traces()` directly aggregates the literal field-visit order across many real recorded sessions, no model inference needed — currently only used for offline scoring, not wired into execution) — correctly not attempted under this deadline.

  **What was built instead, scoped to what's both valid and buildable from already-tested parts**: one new early-exit branch in OPT2, inserted immediately *before* `t_pred = self._predict(state)` (`agent.py`, the real live path's per-step loop) — skipping the transformer call itself, not just the LLM call. Fires only when the focused element is a plain, empty, not-yet-attempted `editcontrol` AND its value is already sitting in the intake data under an exact key match via `self._lookup_field()` — the exact same deterministic lookup `_ask_llm()`'s own fast path already trusts, not a new mechanism. Mirrors the exact exclusion logic the existing `_t_is_type` check already uses a few lines below (`_attempt_key`, `self._leave_blank_keys`, `self._typed_keys`) so it can never disagree with the reactive path about what's already handled or deliberately left blank.

  On a hit: resolves the live control via `_find_uia_control_by_name()` + a manual `.SetFocus()` (no click — the same mechanism the pre-type focus block already uses), fills it via the `direct_fill_hwnd` mechanism built earlier the same night (no click, no paste), marks it attempted, Tabs, settles via `_adaptive_settle_wait`, and `continue`s — skipping the transformer call, the LLM call, and any click entirely for that field. On ANY miss along the way (no label, no known value, HWND resolution fails) — nothing special happens; the branch simply doesn't `continue`, and today's existing, completely unmodified code runs exactly as it already does. Reasoning only costs anything on genuine absence, never on a guess about the future — the same "absence vs. known" rule already agreed on earlier tonight, applied here for the first time to WHERE, not just WHAT.

  **Deliberately, explicitly out of scope** — stated plainly so it isn't mistaken for an oversight under deadline pressure: comboboxes (confirmed this session the direct-write mechanism is a silent no-op on them) and checkboxes (already have their own separate, working `BM_SETCHECK` mechanism) both keep going through today's existing reactive path untouched. Deciding WHERE to navigate next (moving toward a field that isn't focused yet) is also untouched — still calls `predict()` exactly as today, since the valid no-model alternative (`bc_fidelity.py`'s demonstration-order aggregation) isn't wired into live execution yet.

  Unconditional, not behind a flag — like the settle-wait fixes earlier the same night, this sits directly in the real path and only ever activates for a case where the existing code would reach the identical decision anyway; when it doesn't apply, behavior is provably identical to today, since it's the same code path, untouched.

  19 new tests (`tests/test_opt2_fast_fill.py`) — following this project's own established pattern for logic embedded in the un-extracted `run()` method (mirror-function tests for the gating condition, plus direct source-level checks confirming the real code wires in the real, already-tested mechanisms — `_lookup_field`, `_find_uia_control_by_name`/`SetFocus()`, `direct_fill_hwnd`, `_mark_attempted`, `_adaptive_settle_wait` — rather than a parallel reimplementation), including an explicit check that the branch sits textually before the `predict()` call (the actual point of the feature) and a check that no literal field name appears anywhere in it. Full suite: 910 passed, 9 skipped, 0 failed.

This is now a third independent place gating on `_attempt_key`/`_leave_blank_keys`/`_typed_keys` semantics (alongside the existing `_t_is_type` block and `_ask_llm`'s own checks) — a known risk pattern this project has hit before; a dedicated drift-guard test (like `field_planner.py`'s own) would be worth adding if time allows, not blocking for the demo if it doesn't. Navigation between fields is unchanged, so a live run will still show real "thinking" pauses between fields — this speeds up filling known values, not the whole walk; worth setting that expectation before the demo, not after.

Direct user pushback ("we're still using the mouse click") was correct and led to live-testing the combobox equivalent before building it, same discipline as the text-field trick: `CB_SETCURSEL`, a direct Win32 message that selects a real dropdown option with no click and no dropdown ever opened, tested against a real combobox on the running practice form and confirmed correct two independent ways (`CB_GETCURSEL`'s own readback and UIA's `ValuePattern`, which agreed), then restored.

  New `ActionExecutor._combobox_direct()` (`executor.py`, same injectable-`user32`/"return `False` on any failure" shape as `_keyboard_direct`/`_try_semantic_click`): reads the combobox's real options directly via `CB_GETCOUNT`/`CB_GETLBTEXT` (no dropdown opened just to enumerate them), matches the known value case/whitespace-insensitively, sends `CB_SETCURSEL`, and verifies via `CB_GETCURSEL`'s own readback. Wired in as a genuinely new `action_type` (`"combobox_select"`, `combobox_hwnd` key) — unlike the text-field case, today's click-based combobox filling never sets `action_type == "keyboard"` at all (it's two separate `"click"` actions in `agent.py`), so there was no existing gate to widen or risk missing, making a clean new action type the more honest choice here. `dry_run` gates it exactly like every other action type.

Critically, a combobox's result is checked (`_ff_cb_result.success`) before committing — if the known value doesn't match any of the combobox's real options, nothing is marked attempted and the branch does **not** `continue`, falling through to today's existing, completely unmodified click-based open+select path exactly as before.   **Two changes, both reusing only already-tested/already-used mechanisms**: (1) the fast-fill success paths' settle-wait tightened from `step_delay * 0.4` to `step_delay * 0.2` — still fully adaptive (returns early once state stabilizes), just a lower worst-case ceiling, justified because fast-fill already has hard synchronous proof the value landed (the `WM_SETTEXT`/`CB_SETCURSEL` readback matched) before this line is ever reached, unlike the general-purpose fill path this same helper also guards. (2) A new **dead-spot rescue** branch, gated on the focused element's type being outside the known-fillable set (`editcontrol`/`comboboxcontrol`/`checkboxcontrol`/`checkbox` — i.e. genuinely nothing to act on, matching the real `panecontrol` case) inserted as an `elif` alongside the existing fast-fill check, still entirely before `t_pred = self._predict(state)`. Tries `self._navproto.find_visible_empty_target()` — the exact same deterministic, model-free "find the next visible empty unattempted field" function this file already calls elsewhere for the identical purpose (the redirect-stuck sites) — and if it finds one, jumps there via `_find_uia_control_by_name()` + `.SetFocus()` (no click, same mechanism fast-fill itself uses) and `continue`s, letting the next loop iteration re-check fast-fill eligibility on the newly-focused field directly. Falls straight through to today's unmodified transformer call on any miss (nothing found, no label, `SetFocus()` fails) — never worse than today, only sometimes faster.

  **`run_task.py`'s `STEP_DELAY` cut from `1.0` to `0.5`** — direct request ("amp it up... something much faster") after this arithmetic was laid out plainly. This is the single constant every settle-wait budget in `agent.py` scales from (fast-fill's `*0.2`, the main loop's own `*1.0`, every other OPT2 settle-wait site), so cutting it once here proportionally speeds up all of them simultaneously, without touching a dozen separate call sites individually. **Real, stated risk, not hidden**: `_adaptive_settle_wait` already returns early once state stabilizes, so this ceiling mainly matters for the genuinely-slow cases — and some of those are exactly the focus-transition races this project already found and fixed once this session (`execution_type_path_focus_via_uia`, specific fields that needed real settle time to land correctly). Cutting the ceiling risks reintroducing that class of bug if pushed too far; `0.5` (a moderate cut, not zeroed out) was chosen deliberately over a more aggressive value for this reason. Full suite re-run: 934 passed, 9 skipped, 0 failed (unaffected, as expected — the unit suite exercises timing behavior via a fake clock, not real wall-clock duration).

The bottleneck had moved: every step — fast-filled or not — still pays for one full `_observe()` (a fresh UIA snapshot of 100-180 elements) plus one keyboard action. Reasoning was never the remaining cost; the fixed one-field-per-observe-cycle walk was.

  (Separately noted, not a fill-quality bug: that same log's tail fidelity report scored record 2's actual filled values against record 1's expected intake data — e.g. "expected `PAI-2026-00441`, got `PAI-2026-00442`", where `00442` is record 2's own correct policy number, read straight from that record's own cache sample earlier in the same log.   Fix: a new early-exit block, textually *before* the existing single-field OPT2 fast-fill (which still runs unchanged whenever this one finds nothing to do). Instead of acting on only the currently-focused field, it asks "what's every actionable, empty, not-yet-attempted field visible on screen *right now*, from the one `state` we already have?" and fills all of them in a single pass — one `_observe()`, N direct writes, no Tab-to-navigate and no re-observe between fields (only a cheap Tab-to-blur after each write, same as the single-field path). One settle-wait for the whole batch, one `continue` — the next step's single `_observe()` picks up whatever's left.

  Built as a genuine refactor, not a parallel scan: `navigation_protocol.py`'s `find_visible_empty_target()` (the exact function Navigation Protocol itself already calls just above this block, and the same one the dead-spot rescue reuses) is now a thin wrapper over a new `find_all_visible_empty_targets()` — same eligibility rule (fillable type, empty value, not yet attempted, on-screen geometry + UIA's own visible flag), one implementation instead of two that could drift apart. Each returned target is filled via the exact same already-tested, already-shipped mechanisms as the single-field path — `WM_SETTEXT`/`direct_fill_hwnd` for `editcontrol`, `CB_SETCURSEL`/`combobox_select` for `comboboxcontrol` (falls through on no matching option, exactly like the single-field path), `BM_SETCHECK`/`WindowFromPoint` for checkboxes. Checkboxes needed one small extraction first: `self._auto_check()` was hardwired to `state["focused_element_id"]`, but batch needs to evaluate checkboxes that *aren't* focused — so its lookup+parsing logic was pulled out into `self._lookup_checkbox_should_check(field_name)`, called by both `_auto_check` (unchanged behavior, regression-tested) and the new batch block.

  Any single field the batch loop can't resolve (no label, lookup miss, HWND resolution fails, combobox option doesn't match) is just skipped, not retried — it stays visible and empty, so it's picked up by the single-field fast-fill / dead-spot rescue / transformer fallback on a later step, exactly as if batch fast-fill had never run for that one field. `filled_count` starts at 0; only a nonzero count short-circuits the rest of the step.

  21 new tests: 7 in `tests/test_navigation_protocol.py` (`TestFindAllVisibleEmptyTargets`, including one proving `find_visible_empty_target` still agrees with the first element of the "all" version) and 14 in `tests/test_opt2_fast_fill.py` (`TestBatchFastFill`) — source-level checks confirming the real block reuses the real mechanisms (not a reimplementation), fills all three known types, calls `_lookup_checkbox_should_check` rather than `_auto_check` (which would silently only ever see the focused checkbox), contains no `self._observe()` call inside the per-field loop body, and settles/continues exactly once per batch rather than once per field. Full suite: 961 passed, 9 skipped, 0 failed.

  **CONFIRMED-BLANK SKIP added same night**, direct follow-up ("I need it a bit more faster") after tracing one specific gap in that same log: a single genuinely-blank field (`'Custom Equipment Value ($)'`) cost **two full transformer calls** (steps 23-24) before the reactive path's own three-attempt escalation (plain lookup → cache refresh + retry → Notepad peek + retry) finally confirmed there was nothing to fill, then a third step advanced the tab.   Fix: extracted `_ask_llm`'s own three-attempt escalation (found live 2026-08-08 — see `execution_opt2_fast_fill_known_values` history and `tests/test_ask_llm_fast_path_lookup.py` — a naive single-lookup miss isn't trustworthy enough to conclude "blank"; a real field got hallucinated by the LLM three times before this same escalation finally got it right on the fourth attempt) into `self._resolve_field_value_with_escalation(state, field_name, section)`, called by both the unchanged `_ask_llm` (regression-tested, identical behavior) and the batch fast-fill loop's `editcontrol`/`comboboxcontrol` branches. When escalation still comes back empty after all three attempts, batch fast-fill now Tabs past the field directly — no write, no click, and now no transformer call either — records it in `self._leave_blank_keys` (the same tracker Navigation Protocol and the reactive path already trust, not a batch-only concept of "blank"), marks it attempted, and counts it toward the batch so the step still `continue`s without paying for a model call. A confirmed-blank result costs no more than what the reactive path already paid for the same field today — this only removes the transformer call sitting in front of it, never adds new cost.

  **`_resolve_field_control` added same night**, direct follow-up ("still too slow, at least <60s") after timing this exact log: a 13-field batch took 7 seconds with **no `observe()` between fields** — the thing batching exists to eliminate — meaning something else was costing ~0.5s/field. Ruled out one theory first (the "ghost caret" visual overlay adding a UIA call per keystroke) by reading the code: it's already disabled on the real live path (`ghost_cursor=False`), so `_show_ghost_caret()` returns immediately every time. Traced the real cost into `_find_uia_control_by_name` (`agent.py`): every caller (batch fast-fill, single-field fast-fill, dead-spot rescue, the direct-fill-eligible site) always passed `expected_bbox`, forcing its position-based disambiguation search — a `foundIndex=1..10` loop where each "does a second match exist" check that comes back negative costs a real UIA timeout (up to 0.3s) — even for the overwhelming majority of fields whose label is completely unique on screen. Worse for non-`editcontrol` types: the function tries `EditControl`/`ComboBoxControl`/`CheckBoxControl` in that fixed order, so a checkbox field paid for **two** wrong-type timeouts before ever reaching the right finder.

  Disambiguation itself is correct and necessary — it exists specifically because some labels genuinely repeat (`"First Name"` across Driver 1/2/3 sections, added 2026-08-09, `execution_attempted_combobox_position_drift`'s sibling fix) and removing it would reintroduce that exact bug. Fix: a new `self._resolve_field_control(state, label, bbox)` that checks — from the elements *already in the current observed state*, no new UIA call — whether the label is genuinely ambiguous (appears more than once). Only ambiguous labels still get `expected_bbox` passed through to `_find_uia_control_by_name` unchanged; a unique label gets `expected_bbox=None`, taking that function's own fast path (one `Exists()` check per finder, returns as soon as found, no timeout-driven probing). `_find_uia_control_by_name` itself is untouched — this only changes which argument reaches it. Provably never slower than before (same result either way, just sometimes via the faster path), and it generalizes: routed through all five in-`run()` call sites that had `state` in scope (batch fast-fill ×2, single-field fast-fill, dead-spot rescue, the direct-fill-eligible click-path site), not just the one profiled path. Two remaining call sites (`_focus_element_via_uia`, `_scroll_into_view_via_uia`) are thin wrappers without direct access to the elements list — left unchanged rather than widening their signatures across every caller for an unproven marginal gain.

Full suite: 981 passed, 9 skipped, 0 failed.

The search itself, not the disambiguation loop wrapped around it, was always the dominant cost; removing the loop only ever removed a secondary cost sitting on top of it.

  **`_resolve_control_via_point` added same night**, direct follow-up ("any more improvements we could do"). Extends the checkbox mechanism to `editcontrol`/`comboboxcontrol` fields: resolve the live control directly from its own bbox center via `WindowFromPoint` (one plain, cheap Win32 call — no accessibility-tree walk, no search timeout at all), then wrap the resulting handle via UIA's `ControlFromHandle` (already used inside `_find_uia_control_by_name` itself, on a handle FOUND by searching — here, on a handle already in hand from screen position, no search involved either way). `_resolve_field_control` now tries this FIRST, before ever falling back to the proven name-search path.

  Because a wrong text write is a worse failure mode than checkboxes ever risked (a wrongly-checked box is visibly wrong and correctable; wrong text can look plausible and go unnoticed), this checks more than the checkbox path does before trusting a point-based hit: the resolved control's own native window class (a coarse "is this really an edit/combobox control" gate) AND its accessible Name (case/whitespace-insensitive) must both match what was expected — a stale bbox or `WindowFromPoint` landing on a parent panel instead of the actual field returns `None` and falls straight back to `_find_uia_control_by_name`, unchanged. Never worse than before this fix, only faster when the point-based hit is trustworthy.

  Given a live, C-backed, process-wide `ctypes.windll.user32` can't be safely patched in place inside a test (the same constraint executor.py's own `_try_semantic_click`/`_keyboard_direct`/`_combobox_direct` already solved), `_resolve_control_via_point` gained the identical injectable-dependency shape (`uia_mod`/`win32gui_mod`/`user32`, all optional, defaulting to the real modules) rather than faking `sys.modules` for a third dependency.

  26 new tests: 11 in a new `tests/test_resolve_control_via_point.py` (matching edit/combobox controls, wrong-class rejection, Name-mismatch rejection, case/whitespace-insensitive matching, no-window/missing-bbox/empty-label short-circuits with zero Win32 calls, exceptions swallowed not raised, confirms the bbox CENTER is used not the corner) and 15 in `tests/test_opt2_fast_fill.py`/`tests/test_resolve_field_control.py` (updated to explicitly mock the new point-based step so existing routing tests stay deterministic, plus new tests proving a point-based hit short-circuits the name-search entirely). Full suite: 993 passed, 9 skipped, 0 failed.

  **Honestly unverified**: not yet live-tested. Given the direct, same-batch evidence (checkboxes fast via this exact mechanism, text/combobox fields slow via the search this replaces), this is the strongest lever tried so far toward the 60-second target — but two prior "the evidence strongly suggests this fix will help" calls this same night (confirmed-blank skip, disambiguation-skip) had real but smaller-than-expected impact, so the honest expectation is "should help meaningfully," not "will hit 60 seconds."

  **Record-cache dedup guard added same night**, second half of "any more improvements we could do." Investigated a separate finding surfaced while chasing the WindowFromPoint fix: a single-record run logged **31** `"Record cache refreshed"` lines, every single one showing the IDENTICAL 176-field sample already sitting in `self._cached_record`. Traced all 14 call sites of `_refresh_record_cache` in `run()` — 13 of them are tab-advance handlers (7 tab-advances/record), each paying for a real Win32 Notepad text read plus a full regex parse on every tab switch, even though the intake record is a static per-record snapshot, never live-edited mid-record.

  Checked history before touching anything (`scope1_record_advance_5`/`scope1_per_record_refresh`): `_refresh_record_cache` already has real, load-bearing reset logic (clearing `_attempted_keys`/`_typed_keys`/`_advance_blacklist_pos`/`_leave_blank_keys`/`_checked_fields`, resetting `_current_tab_idx`) gated behind `self._record_num != self._attempted_record_num` — meaning that reset only ever fires on a genuine record boundary already, never on a same-record tab-advance. The 13 tab-advance calls were never triggering it; they were purely redundant re-reads.

  Fix: reuse that same existing invariant as a fast-path guard at the top of the function — if the cache is already non-empty AND `self._record_num == self._attempted_record_num` (a real, structured parse already succeeded for this exact record), return immediately, skipping the read and parse entirely. A record advance changes `self._record_num` *before* this is called for the new record, so the guard naturally doesn't match then and the reset logic still runs exactly as it always has — nothing about record-to-record correctness changes. `visual_reader` mode is deliberately excluded (its own branch never touches `self._attempted_record_num`, so the "confirmed populated" guarantee this guard relies on was never established for it — and no real live run uses that mode; every log this session shows "VLM pre-scan skipped").

  6 new tests (`tests/test_refresh_record_cache_dedup.py`): confirms 7 tab-advance-shaped calls in a row produce only 1 real read, a genuine record change still forces a real re-read AND still runs the reset logic, the very first call never skips despite `_attempted_record_num` starting equal to `_record_num` at construction, and `visual_reader` mode is never affected. Full suite: 999 passed, 9 skipped, 0 failed.

  **Deliberately NOT fixed, flagged honestly**: 2 of the 14 call sites (`self._refresh_record_cache(self._observe())`) force a *fresh full-screen `_observe()`* — one of the more expensive operations in this file — purely to have a `state` argument to pass in, even though this guard now makes the call itself a near-instant no-op most of the time. The `self._observe()` call happens as a Python argument evaluation, before `_refresh_record_cache` is ever entered, so this guard can't prevent it. Simplifying those 2 sites to reuse the already-current `state` variable (matching the other 11 call sites' own pattern) looks safe but wasn't independently verified this session — scoped out rather than risked without checking whether anything else at those 2 specific sites depends on a genuinely fresh observation.

  **Honestly unverified**: not yet live-tested, same as the WindowFromPoint fix it ships alongside. Real question: does removing ~30 redundant Notepad reads/parses per record show up as a measurable wall-clock difference, or was the actual cost of each individual read/parse small enough that this mostly matters for the 2 sites that also carry a wasted `_observe()` call.

- [x] `execution_attempted_combobox_position_drift` — **Confirmed live 2026-08-07, immediately after the verify-at-fill give-up fix.** That fix worked — no more 87-step infinite loop on one field — but the user's very next report was *"Stuck again and so many wasted steps"*, then *"We should stop wasting steps like that, the work is getting dirty"*, backed by the run's own metrics: 55.6% wasted-step rate, only 15/176 fields filled in 45 total steps.

  Traced the log: `'Suffix'` (an optional combobox, correctly left blank per the record's own data) got the **full** click-open → check-value → escape → mark-attempted → Tab treatment **4 separate times** across a single Policyholder-tab pass — roughly 10 steps spent re-confirming a field the agent had already correctly figured out was blank.

  Root cause: the existing dead-end defense (`self._nochange_click_pos`, built for `execution_dead_end_click_blacklist`) only blacklists an *exact, 10px-bucketed pixel position* after 3 identical clicks in a row. But the transformer's own click-position *estimate* for the same field isn't pixel-identical across separate approaches — it drifts a few pixels each time it circles back to Suffix minutes apart (not consecutively) — enough to dodge the existing bucket and restart a fresh 3-strikes-then-blacklist cycle instead of staying skipped for good. The `"treat as FILL"` branch (`components/agent/agent.py`, ~L2073) never checked `self._attempted_keys` — the label-based, pixel-drift-immune bookkeeping Navigation Protocol and verify-at-fill's own retry loop already rely on — before clicking at all.

  Fixed: check `attempted_keys` **first**. If this exact combobox (by its `_attempt_key`, i.e. its label) was already confirmed blank earlier this record, skip straight to Tab — no click, nothing to open, nothing to verify. 4 new tests (`tests/test_attempted_combobox_skip.py`), including one that deliberately changes the element's `bbox` and `element_id` between two simulated visits to prove the label-based key isn't fooled by drift the way the position-based blacklist was. Full suite: 206 passed, 9 skipped, same 2 pre-existing unrelated tesseract failures.

  **Separately noted but deliberately NOT patched**: the same log also shows long bursts of low pointer-confidence `"Tab fallback"` steps (6+ in a row, confidence clustered 0.32-0.48, below the 0.30/0.50 floor). That's the already-documented ~68.9% click-accuracy ceiling this project has tracked all session (`scope1_tab_order` and others) — a model-quality limitation, not a control-flow bug. Per `CLAUDE.md` ("the real fix is more training data not another patch") and this session's own established precedent on the same class of issue, no new confidence-gate patch was added for it. Not yet re-verified live.
- [x] `execution_navprotocol_checkbox_gap` — **Confirmed live 2026-08-07 — a fresh run, started AFTER every one of that night's fixes, still cycled Policyholder → Vehicle → Coverage → back to Policyholder for 10+ minutes, never reaching Drivers.** Traced one cycle in the log (`grep "Tab-click → navigating to\|Stuck guard: advancing to tab"`) and found a 23-consecutive-step pure-scrolling stretch on Coverage (steps 405–427): element count flat at 152, zero field interaction the entire stretch, just `"Navigation Protocol: scroll moved the view — new fields revealed"` repeating without ever stopping to act.

  Per the discipline earned from two misdiagnoses the same night ("4-Door Sedan" and poll-timing — both wrong, both caught only by re-checking real evidence instead of trusting a guess), checked the actual form source before touching any code: `car_insurance_entry/car_insurance_form_wx.py`'s `_build_coverage_tab` has 18 checkboxes (`_check_grid`) across "Additional Coverages" (10) and "Discounts Applied" (8), and nothing else in that stretch of the tab.

  Root cause: `navigation_protocol.py`'s `_FILLABLE_TYPES` (built earlier that same session, for `execution_navigation_protocol`) never included any checkbox type — `has_visible_empty_target()` could never return `True` for a screen made only of checkboxes, so `decide()` kept returning `SCROLL`. Meanwhile the view kept *genuinely* changing anyway (other comboboxes drifting in/out at the viewport margins as the page scrolled), so the dead-scroll cap never tripped to force a tab advance either — worst of both worlds: never waits, never gives up either.

  Second, related bug found in the same pass: `_SIG_TYPES` said `"checkboxcontrol"` only, but `ui_observer.py`'s `_CTRL_TYPE_MAP` maps real `wx.CheckBox` controls to `"checkbox"` (no suffix) — the exact same mapped-vs-raw split that caused `execution_listitem_type_mismatch_loop` earlier the same night, just resurfacing on a different field type. So checkboxes weren't even registering in the scroll-signature check either.

  Fixed: added both `"checkbox"` and `"checkboxcontrol"` to `_FILLABLE_TYPES` and `_SIG_TYPES`. Checked for the opposite failure mode before committing — does this now force a click on every checkbox, including ones meant to legitimately stay unchecked? No: `NavAction.WAIT` only stops the blind-scroll and hands the step back to the transformer's own normal per-step judgment, it never forces a specific action. And `_record_attempt()` already marks whatever element a click lands on as attempted — generic, not combobox-specific — so once the transformer does act on a checkbox it stops being offered as a target on the next `decide()` call. 6 new tests (`tests/test_navigation_protocol.py`). Full suite: 196 passed, 9 skipped, 2 pre-existing unrelated failures (`tests/test_cv_perception.py`, tesseract OCR not installed on this machine — a separate CV-based perception path, untouched by this fix). Not yet re-verified live.
- [x] `execution_checkbox_reset_default` — **Confirmed 2026-08-08, reported directly:** *"Submit button doesnt reset Checkboxes."* This is a bug in the practice-form fixture itself (`car_insurance_entry/car_insurance_form_wx.py`), not the agent.

  `_clear_all_fields()` (called by `_on_submit()` on every Submit, to reset for the next record) set **every** checkbox to `False` unconditionally, ignoring each checkbox's own declared `default`. Grepping for `default=True` only found 3 hits due to formatting differences in the source; instantiating the actual form headlessly (`wx.App` + `CarInsuranceFrame`, confirmed to work fine without a visible window) and reading `_checkbox_defaults` directly found the real count: **six** checkboxes default to checked on first load — `paperless` (Policy: "Paperless / e-Delivery"), `v_airbags`/`v_abs`/`v_daytime_lights` (Vehicle), `cov_um_uim` (Coverage: "Uninsured/Underinsured Motorist"), `pay_auto_pay` (Payment: "Auto-Pay Enrolled") — and every one of them answers "checked" in every record's own ground-truth data too, so this wasn't an edge case: record 2 onward opened with all six wrongly unchecked, every single run, requiring the agent (or a human) to re-check all six that a fresh form load never had to touch.

  Fixed (first attempt): `_check()` recorded each checkbox's declared default in a new `self._checkbox_defaults` dict at construction time; `_clear_all_fields()` reset each checkbox to its own recorded default instead of a hardcoded `False`. 7 tests, full suite green.

  **CORRECTED same day, direct user spec:** *"When I press Submit I want all the checked checkboxes to become unchecked."* The restore-to-default behavior above was the wrong call — not what was actually wanted. Reverted `_checkbox_defaults` entirely (dead code once nothing reads it) and simplified `_clear_all_fields()` back to unconditionally unchecking every checkbox, matching the direct spec exactly: Submit always clears every checkbox, regardless of its construction-time default.

  While investigating, also found a second, real reason even the *original* plain `ctrl.SetValue(False)` reportedly wasn't visibly working in practice: `agent.py` checks boxes via a raw Win32 `BM_SETCHECK` message sent straight to the native control (deliberately — its own comment says *"pyautogui clicks don't toggle wx checkboxes"*), bypassing wx's click/event pipeline entirely. wx's own Python-level `CheckBox` object never observes that native-level change, so its cached internal value can silently disagree with the real, visible, native state — meaning `ctrl.SetValue(False)` alone isn't guaranteed to re-sync a checkbox that got checked this way. Fixed by also sending `BM_SETCHECK` (unchecked) directly to the native control on reset, via `ctrl.GetHandle()` — the exact same mechanism the agent already uses to check boxes, so the reset can't be defeated by that same desync. A dedicated test (`TestNativeWin32StateIsForcedInSync`) simulates this precisely: checks a box at the *native* level only (bypassing `ctrl.SetValue()` entirely, exactly like the agent does), then confirms `_clear_all_fields()` still forces it unchecked.

  5 new tests (`tests/test_checkbox_reset_to_unchecked.py`, replacing the deleted `tests/test_checkbox_reset_to_default.py`), using a real headless `wx.App`/`CarInsuranceFrame` instance rather than mocking `wx` — this is form-widget behavior, nothing meaningful to fake. Full suite: 255 passed, 9 skipped, same 2 pre-existing unrelated tesseract failures. Not yet re-verified live.

- [x] `execution_uncaught_crash_not_logged` — **Found and fixed 2026-08-08, live: a test run of the newest checkpoint crashed, and `logs/latest.log` cut off after just 5 lines — right after `"Perception: UIA (accessibility tree)"`, with nothing explaining why.** Directly undermines this whole project's standing diagnose-from-logs workflow (`CLAUDE.md`: "live-test → diagnose from logs → fix root cause"), since a log that can silently stop mid-run is useless for exactly the situations it's most needed. Root cause: `LLMAgent(...)` construction in `run_task.py` sits entirely outside the only `try/except` in the file (which wraps `agent.run()`, further down) — any exception raised while building the agent (e.g. loading a checkpoint) had no exception handler anywhere in scope, so Python's default behavior kicked in: print the traceback to the console only, nothing to the log file. Also, the existing `except Exception as exc:` around `agent.run()` itself only logged `str(exc)` (the message), not the traceback — even a *caught* crash couldn't tell you *where* in the code it happened.

  Fixed both, with a structural fix rather than another narrow `try/except`: installed a custom `sys.excepthook` right after the logger is configured. Python calls `sys.excepthook` for *any* exception that reaches the top of the script uncaught, regardless of where it originates — this guarantees the log always has the real traceback no matter what changes in the script around it later, instead of relying on remembering to wrap every new block. Also changed the existing `agent.run()` catch to log with `exc_info=True` (full traceback) instead of just the exception's message. Regression tests: `tests/test_run_task_uncaught_exception_logging.py` (confirms the hook is installed and that calling it logs a full traceback, not just a message). Full suite 302 passed / 9 skipped / same 2 pre-existing unrelated tesseract failures. Not yet re-verified against the original live crash — the actual cause of that crash still needs a from-terminal repro now that it'll be logged.

  **Immediately paid off**: the fix above surfaced the real cause of that original crash on the very next attempt — `FileNotFoundError: CAPSULE NOT FOUND`, because the checkpoint file being tested had been deleted during routine post-experiment cleanup before the user got a chance to run it live. Not a code bug; just needed to regenerate the checkpoint and stop deleting scratch checkpoints the user hasn't tested yet.

- [x] `execution_verify_at_fill_timing_race` — **Found and fixed 2026-08-08, live: user reported wasted steps on "Years Continuously Insured."** Log showed the field visited 4 separate times over the run, each time: type `'9'` → verify-at-fill checks immediately → reads `''` (empty) → retry (re-click, ctrl+a, retype) → checks again → still reads `''` → retry again → still `''` → gives up, moves on. Every single check across all 4 visits and both retries read exactly empty — never partial, never a different wrong value — yet the run's final summary showed the field correctly holding `'9'`. That pattern (always exactly empty, but correct by the end) is the signature of a race condition, not a real typing failure: the pasted value hadn't finished landing in the control's text buffer yet when the verification snapshot was taken, because `state_after = self._observe()` fired with **zero delay** immediately after the paste executed.

  A single fixed sleep isn't a reliable fix either — the existing retry path already sleeps `step_delay * 0.3` (~300ms) before its own re-check, and that *still* read empty on both retries in the live log, so the real settle time varies and can exceed a guessed constant. Fixed with a short poll instead of one gamble: right before verify-at-fill's own check, poll up to `step_delay * 0.4` in `0.1`s steps, breaking the instant the value actually appears — fields that update instantly aren't slowed down, and slower ones get however long they actually need instead of one fixed guess. Scoped to keyboard/type actions only (click actions' "focus moved" check doesn't read typed content, so it isn't exposed to this race). Regression tests: `tests/test_verify_at_fill.py::TestVerifyAtFillSettlePoll` (3 new tests — breaks early on a match, exhausts the budget correctly when the value never appears, skips polling entirely when correct on the first check). Full suite 305 passed / 9 skipped / same 2 pre-existing unrelated tesseract failures.

  **Re-verified live same night — real regression found and fixed.** User reported the whole run got noticeably slower. Root cause: the fix as first shipped slept-then-checked *unconditionally* on every keyboard action, then the caller still took a separate `state_after = self._observe()` right after regardless — meaning every single typed field (the ~175 that never had this problem, not just the one that did) now paid at least one extra `sleep(0.1)` + `_observe()`, plus a redundant second observe on top. Fixed by checking **immediately first** (zero extra cost when already correct, which is the common case) and reusing the poll loop's own last observation instead of observing again afterward — the already-correct case now costs exactly one `_observe()` call, identical to before this fix existed at all; only a field that's genuinely still settling pays the poll cost. Tests updated to match (`test_returns_true_immediately_with_no_polling_when_already_correct` added).
- [x] `execution_dead_scroll_counter_reset_too_loosely` — **Found and fixed 2026-08-08, live, same session as the report above: "Navigation Protocol is not activating... when there are no more targets on screen."** Log showed 11 *consecutive* pure-scroll steps right after a run of successful fills (Email Address → Home Phone → Cell Phone → Work Phone), each one logging "scroll moved the view — new fields revealed," none of them ever stopping to fill anything. Root cause: the reset condition for `_tab_scroll_count` (agent.py's own counter, passed to `navigation_protocol.decide()` as `dead_scroll_count`, meant to accumulate consecutive *unproductive* scrolls and eventually hand off to `ADVANCE_TAB` after `max_dead_scrolls=2`) only checked whether `visible_field_signature(after) != visible_field_signature(before)`. But `visible_field_signature`'s own docstring says its job is proving the view *physically moved* ("not that nothing is left to fill") — scrolling shifts nearly every visible element's y-position, so this condition was satisfied by almost any scroll, whether or not anything fillable was actually revealed. Resetting the counter on that alone meant it could never reach `max_dead_scrolls` — the agent kept scrolling past non-actionable content (already-filled fields, section content re-entering the viewport) indefinitely instead of ever concluding the tab was exhausted.

  Fixed by only resetting the counter when `has_visible_empty_target()` — an already-existing, fully generic function (its own docstring: "no field names, coordinates, or app names hardcoded," just widget type + empty value + on-screen geometry) — confirms the new view actually has something actionable. A scroll that reveals new-but-non-actionable content now correctly still counts toward "still nothing to do here." Zero field/task-specific logic added; both this fix and the timing-race fix above route entirely through pre-existing generic mechanisms. Regression tests: `tests/test_dead_scroll_counter_requires_actionable_target.py` (4 new tests — a signature change from an already-filled field shifting position must NOT reset the counter; a genuine empty field revealed must reset it; repeated non-actionable scrolls must accumulate rather than stay pinned at 0; a scroll that doesn't move the view at all also increments). Full suite 309 passed / 9 skipped / same 2 pre-existing unrelated tesseract failures.

  **Re-verified live SAME night, even WITH the fix above already in place: a run still logged 7 straight "actionable field revealed" scrolls in a row, nothing ever getting filled between them, this time right near the end of the run (17,000-element-count-unchanged steps 51-58).** Traced it: the confirming `has_visible_empty_target()` check runs on `_state_after` — but the branch then just `continue`s back to the top of the loop, which takes a totally FRESH `self._observe()` before `navigation_protocol.decide()` gets called again. Two separate snapshots of the UI, moments apart — and evidently not perfectly frame-stable, since the fresh one kept disagreeing with the one that had JUST confirmed something actionable was there, re-triggering `SCROLL` instead of ever handing off to the transformer. Rather than chase the exact source of that frame-to-frame instability, fixed it the way the Drought Guard (right next to this code) already solves the identical problem: don't gamble that a later, separately-observed recheck will agree — act on the just-confirmed state immediately. Now calls `self._focus_first_empty_field(_state_after, after_scroll=True)` directly in the scroll branch the moment `has_visible_empty_target` confirms a target, the exact same helper/pattern the Drought Guard already uses successfully a few lines away. Regression tests: `tests/test_dead_scroll_counter_requires_actionable_target.py::TestScrollBranchActsImmediatelyOnConfirmedTarget` (2 new tests). Full suite 311 passed / 9 skipped / same 2 pre-existing unrelated tesseract failures.
- [x] `execution_click_before_type_focus_race` — **Re-verified live SAME night: "Years Continuously Insured" looped back a THIRD time, even with the value-settle-poll fix (above) already in place.** This time the gap between paste and the verify-at-fill failure was multiple real seconds — too long to explain as "the UI hadn't caught up yet." But the *retry* path (which re-clicks the field before retyping) still recovered it every time, same as the two prior sessions. That's the key clue: a race in a different place. UIA already reported the field as focused (the preceding navigate-click's own validator said "ok: Focus moved after click"), but real OS-level keyboard focus apparently hadn't actually landed there yet when the paste fired — a **focus-transition race**, distinct from the value-settle race fixed earlier. Since re-clicking is already proven (by the retry path, every single time) to fix it, the direct fix is doing that same click before the *first* attempt, not just the retries that come after already-wasted steps.

  Added a click on the focused field's own bbox (reusing this project's own established principle from the click-anchoring fix: "we already know exactly where the focused field is, no reason to trust a second, independent guess") right before every type action, alongside the existing pre-type-clear logic. Universal — applies to any typed field, not hardcoded to this one. **Known trade-off, stated plainly rather than discovered by the user again**: unlike the value-settle fix (which was tightened same-day to cost nothing in the common case), this one *does* add a small, unconditional cost to every typed field — one click execution + a 0.1s sleep, maybe ~150-250ms — because there's no cheap way to know in advance which fields will hit the race. Estimated ~15-25s added across a full ~150-field run, weighed against this bug's actual cost (several wasted seconds and 2 retries *every time it hits*, unpredictably, not just on one field). If this alone measurably slows a run down the way the first version of the settle-poll fix did, that's the next thing to revisit — but it wasn't safely avoidable this time the way it was there. Regression tests: `tests/test_click_before_type_focus_race.py` (5 new tests). Full suite 316 passed / 9 skipped / same 2 pre-existing unrelated tesseract failures.
- [x] `execution_attempted_keys_not_checked_at_fill_gate` — **Re-verified live SAME night: even with the click-before-type fix above in place, the user reported "Nope. Goddamnit. There are loops again as well."** Log showed genuine progress (scroll fixes filled Street Address 1, Work Phone after revealing them) but `'Years Continuously Insured'` — already typed and verify-at-fill-CONFIRMED once, several steps earlier — got navigated back to, read as empty by a later live snapshot (the same stale-read symptom the click-before-type fix was built around, just surviving longer than one retry), and re-entered the fill branch fully: a real LLM lookup, a real paste, a full 2-retry verify-at-fill cycle, 4 times in a row (`ptr_conf=0.98` each time).   **First attempt** added a straight `_fe2_key in self._attempted_keys` check for any type. Caught before it shipped: `self._attempted_keys` deliberately includes fields merely *clicked* for navigation with no value ever typed (`self._record_attempt` marks attempted on both keyboard actions and plain clicks) — exactly the distinction `execution_payment_tab_oscillation_fix`'s round-3 regression already found and fixed once (checkbox-only scoping, specifically because a bare click on an editcontrol/combobox is navigation, not a fill decision). Using the general attempted set here would have silently reintroduced that same regression: one stray low-confidence-escalation click onto a genuinely-empty field would permanently exclude it from ever being filled.

  **Fixed properly** with a new, narrower `self._typed_keys` set — populated only inside `_record_attempt` when the action was `keyboard` *and* carried real (non-empty, stripped) text, never on a bare click, never on a housekeeping `tab`/`ctrl+a` keystroke. The fill-gate's new `_fe2_already_attempted` check and the NAVIGATE-branch's `_reclick_edit_filled`/`_reclick_combobox_filled` reclick guards (which had the identical exposure — both required a non-empty *live* value read, the same unreliable signal, to treat a field as "already filled") now check `self._typed_keys` instead of `self._attempted_keys`. Cleared alongside `self._attempted_keys` at the new-record boundary. Checkboxes untouched — for them, click *is* the fill action, so the general attempted set was already the correct signal. Regression tests: `tests/test_attempted_vs_typed_keys.py` (5 new tests — genuine typed text is recorded, bare clicks and housekeeping keystrokes are not, a stale-empty re-read doesn't trigger a refill, a genuinely-empty merely-clicked field still gets filled). Full suite 328 passed / 9 skipped / same 2 pre-existing unrelated tesseract failures. Not yet re-verified live.
- [x] `execution_scroll_branch_two_uia_systems_disagreeing` — **Re-verified live SAME night: the scroll-branch fix above (`execution_dead_scroll_counter_reset_too_loosely`'s `_focus_first_empty_field` call) still looped — 3+ consecutive "actionable field revealed" scrolls with nothing filled, every single time.** Rather than guess at a fourth theory, added diagnostic logging to `_uia_focus_first_field`'s previously-silent "zero candidates" return path first (committed alone, logging-only, no behavior change) and asked for one more live run before touching anything else.

  **That run gave a clean, direct answer.** The diagnostic showed: pane found correctly (`active pane = 'tab_policyholder'`), but the raw UIA tree walk saw **zero** Edit/ComboBox controls in it at all — not filtered out, never seen — at the exact moment `navigation_protocol.has_visible_empty_target()` (a *completely different* code path, reading `ui_observer.py`'s own already-settled `state["elements"]` snapshot) had just confirmed one was there. Two independent systems walking the same UI tree, disagreeing — most plausibly because the raw `uiautomation` tree genuinely hadn't caught up yet immediately after a scroll, while `_state_after` (observed with its own settle delay) already had.

  Fixed by not routing through the second, disagreeing system at all: call `find_visible_empty_target()` — the sibling of `has_visible_empty_target()`, returning the actual matching element instead of just a bool — and click its own bbox directly. Same data source powers both the check and the action now, so there's nothing left for a second system to disagree with. Regression tests updated (`test_dead_scroll_counter_requires_actionable_target.py::TestScrollBranchActsImmediatelyOnConfirmedTarget`, rewritten to assert a direct click on the target's bbox instead of a `_focus_first_empty_field` call). Full suite 316 passed / 9 skipped / same 2 pre-existing unrelated tesseract failures. Not yet re-verified live.

  **Pattern worth naming across all four rounds tonight** (verify-at-fill's value-settle race, the dead-scroll counter, the focus-transition race, and this one): every one of them was two different subsystems each holding their own view of "what's true right now" on the same live UI, disagreeing by a small margin in a way that only shows up under real timing. The fix each time was the same shape — stop trusting a second, independently-observed source to agree with the first; act on the one that already proved itself right there, or make the disagreement the thing measured (poll) instead of assumed away (a single fixed wait).
- [x] `execution_scroll_reveals_only_one_field` — **Re-verified live SAME night: the fixes above genuinely reduced the looping, but the user's next report was that each scroll still only "revealed one damn input field" — not the "optimal view" (as many actionable targets visible at once as possible) `navigation_protocol.py` was originally built to maximize.** Root cause: `_scroll_form_down` always did a fixed, small `pyautogui.scroll(-5)` regardless of how much unfilled content remained below the viewport — one small nudge, stop as soon as anything new appeared, repeat.

  Explicitly told by the user that the mouse-wheel scroll mechanism isn't required — any means of reaching a better view is fine. **First attempt** scaled scroll magnitude by a count-based heuristic (more unfilled fields remaining below → bigger scroll) — a reasonable-sounding guess, but still a guess, not a computed position.

  **Corrected same conversation, directly told by the user this could be done precisely instead — and it can, without knowing the scroll-unit-to-pixel ratio in advance.** Self-calibrating approach: find the topmost still-unfilled fillable field currently below the viewport (the actual next target, using the exact same filtering as `has_visible_empty_target`); scroll a small known amount (`-5` units, the original safe default) as a calibration nudge; re-observe and find that *same* field again (matched via `_attempt_key`, the project's existing stable cross-snapshot identity); measure how many pixels it actually moved; divide to get a real, measured pixels-per-unit ratio for *this* run, on *this* machine; then scroll exactly the additional distance needed to land that field ~40px from the top of the viewport (capped at 60 more units as a safety ceiling against a bad measurement). No hardcoded pixel/unit constant, no re-tuning needed if the OS/app/display scaling changes — it measures itself fresh every call. Generic, no field/task-specific logic. Regression tests rewritten to match (`tests/test_adaptive_scroll_distance.py`, 6 tests — computes correct additional units from measured movement, caps at 60, stops cleanly if the target isn't found after calibration or the view didn't move, picks the topmost off-screen target). Full suite 322 passed / 9 skipped / same 2 pre-existing unrelated tesseract failures.

Fixed: one move-to-center at the start, one move-back at the very end — both scroll actions (calibration and, when needed, the calibrated follow-up) now happen from that single cursor position instead of round-tripping in between. Same underlying scroll amounts and math, half the cursor movement. Full suite 322 passed / 9 skipped / same 2 pre-existing unrelated tesseract failures. Not yet re-verified live.

  **User also asked directly: "Can we just move the form if we're not utilizing the scroll bar?"** — i.e., scroll via a direct Windows/UIA message instead of simulated cursor+wheel input, which would avoid visible cursor movement entirely. Real option (`win32gui.SendMessage(hwnd, WM_VSCROLL, ...)` or UIA's `ScrollPattern`), but not implemented tonight — whether this specific wx `ScrolledPanel` actually honors either mechanism can't be verified without live testing (only the user runs live GUI actions), and getting it wrong would mean scrolling stops working entirely, worse than "chaotic but functional." Fixed the immediate, evidence-confirmed problem (redundant cursor round-trips) first. Worth trying as a follow-up if the reduced-movement version still isn't good enough.

  **Re-checked the SAME log immediately after, at the user's direct redirect ("I don't care mainly about the cursor, I care about the goddamn Navigation Protocol") — found a real, self-inflicted architectural regression underneath the cursor symptom.** The precise self-calibrating scroll (added a few entries above) did its own internal `self._observe()` to measure movement — but the OUTER scroll branch in `run()` *also* does its own separate `time.sleep` + `self._observe()` + find-target-and-click right after `_scroll_form_down` returns. Two independent observation cycles stacked on each other, exactly the "two systems disagreeing" bug class already found and fixed three times earlier the same night (verify-at-fill's value-settle race, the dead-scroll counter, the focus-transition race) — reproduced by making the inner function smarter without touching the outer one that assumed a dumb, single-action scroll. Confirmed in the log: 6 repeated "scrolled — view barely moved" / "actionable field revealed" cycles in a row with nothing ever filled in between.

  **Simplified rather than adding a third observation layer to patch a bug caused by adding a second one.** Reverted `_scroll_form_down` to the earlier count-based scroll-magnitude estimate (one decisive scroll, no internal re-observation), and left the single observe-find-click cycle entirely to the caller — which was already correct and already fixed for this exact bug class once. Tests reverted to match (`tests/test_adaptive_scroll_distance.py`, back to the 7 count-based tests). Full suite 323 passed / 9 skipped / same 2 pre-existing unrelated tesseract failures. Noted for future work on this module: don't over-index on making scroll precise at the expense of the other navigation actions it's responsible for.
