Not a total freeze (it did eventually recover, unlike the earlier 11-scroll dead loop), but real, repeated waste, and it matches the user's complaint that a bigger requested scroll magnitude isn't translating into an actually bigger *revealed* view.

  **No fix shipped yet — added diagnostics instead of guessing a 5th theory.** Two guesses were plausible (pyautogui's scroll delta not translating proportionally into this wx `ScrolledPanel`'s actual scroll distance; or `_form_viewport_bottom`'s window-bottom estimate including a non-scrolling footer/button strip, so a "visible" target's computed bbox doesn't correspond to anything actually clickable at that screen position) but neither is confirmed by the log — this project's whole point is verifying instead of guessing, and the last two guess-first attempts on this exact scroll mechanism (self-calibrating precision, then the cursor-chaos regression it caused) are the reason to not repeat that pattern a third time. Added: (1) `[SCROLL-DIAG]` logging that tracks one specific off-screen field across a single scroll call (matched via `_attempt_key`, no second observation cycle — reuses the existing post-scroll `_state_after` this branch already takes) and logs its measured real pixel movement, and (2) logs the revealed target's own type/label/bbox at click time, so a repeat click at the same screen position will show up in the log as the *same* field name rather than an anonymous coordinate. Regression-covered by existing scroll/navigation tests (no new test needed — this is logging-only, no behavior change). Full suite 328 passed / 9 skipped / same 2 pre-existing unrelated tesseract failures. Needs one more live run before the real fix can be identified.

  **Re-tested live immediately after: "It's not finding the optimal view, its going one-by-one."** Checked the newest log — and it contained **zero** `Scroll-form` lines at all; the scroll branch never fired once in that entire run, even though fields as far down as y=794 got filled successfully. Asked the user directly whether this was the same scroll/reveal complaint or the separate 'Suffix' pointer-drift pattern also visible in that log — confirmed: scroll/reveal.

  **Root cause found, no guessing needed this time.** `components/observers/ui_observer/ui_observer.py` builds every element's `"visible"` field as a **hardcoded `True`**, unconditionally — it never actually reads whether UIA considers the element on-screen. Nothing downstream ever consumed `"visible"` either; `navigation_protocol.py`'s `has_visible_empty_target`/`find_visible_empty_target`/`visible_field_signature` only ever had a *geometric estimate* (`_form_viewport_bottom`, the window's own rect bottom) to decide what's "visible" — an estimate, not a fact. When that estimate is generous (e.g. a tall window where a field's raw y-coordinate falls within the window's bottom edge even though it isn't genuinely on-screen without scrolling), `decide()` never calls SCROLL at all — it believes everything is already visible and lets the transformer click directly at far-below coordinates. The target wx form then does its **own** native "scroll a newly-focused control into view" behavior, one field at a time, completely outside this project's Navigation Protocol — which is exactly what "going one-by-one" looks like from the user's seat, and exactly why two rounds of tuning the scroll *magnitude* code never touched it: the scroll magnitude code was never running.

  **Fixed at the actual source of the estimate, not by tuning it further.** Windows' UI Automation already exposes the real, authoritative answer directly — `IsOffscreen`, a base property on every UIA element, no pattern lookup needed. `ui_observer.py` now reads `ctrl.IsOffscreen` (defensively wrapped — a read failure defaults to "not offscreen," preserving the old always-visible fallback rather than wrongly hiding a field the system couldn't ask) and sets `"visible": not is_offscreen`. `navigation_protocol.py`'s three visibility functions now also require `e.get("visible", True)` — default `True` so any existing state/test that never sets the key is completely unaffected, but a real UIA-sourced element now carries the true answer instead of a second, independent geometric guess. This replaces guessing with reading a fact that was available the whole time. Regression tests: `tests/test_navigation_protocol.py::TestVisibleFlagTrustsUiaOverGeometry` (5 new tests) and `tests/test_ui_observer_visible_flag.py` (3 new tests, mirroring the defensive `IsOffscreen` read). Full suite 336 passed / 9 skipped / same 2 pre-existing unrelated tesseract failures. Not yet re-verified live.

  **Re-tested live immediately after: "Still did not do it."** Checked the newest log — genuinely mixed news. The fix IS working as designed: `[SCROLL-DIAG] tracked target 'Prior Liability Limits': bbox_top 869 → 869 (moved 0 px up)` confirms, with a real measured number, that the view is genuinely at the bottom of its scrollable content — Navigation Protocol now correctly recognizes "tab exhausted" and calls `ADVANCE_TAB`, something it essentially never did before (masked by the old always-visible bug). That part is fixed.

  **But it then exposed a second, previously-hidden bug: the tab-advance click doesn't reliably stick.** The log showed `_try_advance_tab` cycling through all 8 tabs repeatedly (0→1→2→...→7→0→1→2...) for the rest of the run, filling nothing further. Traced two separate contributing issues:

  1. `_detect_active_tab_idx_raw`'s own pane-detection reported `detected=0` (Policy) on literally every single check, regardless of which tab was actually showing — `_try_advance_tab`'s existing drift-guard correctly distrusted this and fell back to its own tracker (by design), so this alone wasn't fatal, but it meant detection could never confirm a switch actually worked either. Root cause: this form's tab strip is horizontal (confirmed live — 7 consecutive tab clicks in the same run all landed at the same y=136), so every tab header shares the same `tab_cy`, and the function's own per-tab check is geometrically identical across all of them — it can only ever answer "is anything genuinely visible" (a yes/no), not "which tab." Added the same `e.get("visible", True)` check used in the scroll fix above, which at least stops a covered-over, hidden panel's stale geometry from ever being mistaken for proof its tab is active — a real but partial improvement (documented as such, not oversold): it still can't discriminate between multiple simultaneously-visible candidates when tabs share one strip. Regression tests: `tests/test_startup_tab_verification.py::TestDetectActiveTabIdxRaw` (2 new tests).

  2. **The more likely actual cause of zero progress**: `_reassert_form_window` (called at the top of every step) logged `Foreign window from the form's own process is blocking it — likely a modal dialog. Dismissing with Escape.` on **every single tab-advance click**, no exceptions, for the rest of the run — and the field `_scroll_form_down`'s own diagnostic kept tracking (`'Prior Liability Limits'`) stayed identical across supposedly-different tabs, suggesting the actual on-screen tab may never have genuinely changed. Two competing, unconfirmed explanations: either this really is a modal dialog and something else is separately blocking the switch from sticking, or wx's `Notebook` gives each tab panel its own child HWND — meaning a plain, successful tab switch legitimately changes `GetForegroundWindow()` to a normal sibling window (same process, not a dialog at all), and this code's own Escape-then-force-focus-back-to-the-original-hwnd is what's undoing the switch, every time. Added diagnostic logging (window class name + title of the "foreign" window) rather than guessing which — `"#32770"` is a real Windows dialog box class; anything else points to the second theory. No test added (logging-only, no behavior change). Full suite 338 passed / 9 skipped / same 2 pre-existing unrelated tesseract failures. Needs one more live run to confirm which theory is right before the actual fix can be written.

  **Re-tested live immediately after: the diagnostic gave a definitive, alarming answer.** `class='#32770', title='Clear All'` — a genuine Windows dialog box (`#32770` is the real system dialog class, not a false positive), titled **"Clear All"**. Traced the trigger: the transformer's own low-confidence navigate-pointer (`ptr_conf=0.53` — entirely unremarkable, no different from dozens of other navigation clicks that step) happened to land on a real "Clear All" button, popping this confirmation dialog, which then got silently Escaped every subsequent step for the rest of the run — never causing actual data loss (Escape = cancel), but permanently blocking all further progress since the underlying stuck-pointer problem was never actually addressed.

  **Root cause: there is an existing guard for this exact class of mistake, but it doesn't cover this case.** `_SUBMIT_KW`/`_find_submit_button` already blocks accidentally clicking a Submit-like button early — but (a) it only guards against finishing early, not destroying data, and (b) it's gated behind `not self._no_autohandlers`, which is **disabled** in the exact run mode (OPT2, `self._no_autohandlers=True`) actually being used — confirmed via `grep`, zero hits for `[MERGE] TRANSFORMER overrides` anywhere in any recent log, so the legacy click-merge path this guard was written for isn't even the one executing.

  **Fixed with a new, unconditional guard, not a reuse of the disabled one.** Added `_DESTRUCTIVE_KW = {"clear", "reset", "delete", "erase", "discard", "cancel"}` and `_find_destructive_button_at()` (mirrors `_find_submit_button`'s "don't filter by `window_role`" reasoning — a Clear/Reset button lives on the main window too), wired directly into the OPT2 navigate branch right before its click gets queued: if the transformer's pointer target lands on a destructive button, Tab instead of clicking, unconditionally, in every mode. Generic — no form-specific button text assumed, matching the existing `_SUBMIT_KW` convention. Regression tests: `tests/test_destructive_button_guard.py` (7 new tests). Full suite 345 passed / 9 skipped / same 2 pre-existing unrelated tesseract failures. Not yet re-verified live.

  **Direct pushback, same conversation: "It's not just the Clear All."** Right call — the keyword list was already the whack-a-mole trap this project's own conventions warn against. Read `car_insurance_form_wx.py`'s own footer button list directly instead of guessing at more keywords: five buttons share one row — Submit, Clear All, Print Preview, Load Record, Save Record. `_on_load` opens a file picker and, if a file is chosen, **silently overwrites every field with a different record's data**; `_on_print_preview` opens its own separate modal dialog. Neither matches any destructive keyword, and both are exactly as capable of derailing a run as Clear All was.

  **Regeneralized rather than extending the keyword list again.** `_find_destructive_button_at()` no longer checks keywords at all — it matches ANY `buttoncontrol`/`button`-type element at the click position. The reasoning: the navigate branch's entire job is moving the pointer between *fields*; it has no legitimate reason to ever click an action button while doing that, regardless of its label. The one place a button should be clicked deliberately — Submit, once a record is genuinely finished — already goes through its own separate, dedicated path (`_find_submit_button`, called directly by the stuck-guard's "last tab exhausted" branch), which never flows through the uncertain `click_position` this check guards. So instead of naming every button that must not be clicked, this refuses all of them here; the one that legitimately should get clicked already bypasses the check entirely. `_DESTRUCTIVE_KW` is kept (now unused by this function) purely as a record of which specific buttons the original incident involved. Regression tests rewritten (`tests/test_destructive_button_guard.py`, 9 tests — explicitly proves Print Preview/Load Record/Save Record are caught despite matching no destructive keyword, that Submit is caught too when hit incidentally, and that tab-strip clicks are unaffected since they're a different element type).
