The group-header click handler goes back to calling `loadCapsuleIntoSlot()` directly followed by `flashPlaySlot()` (the same calm border/background pulse kept from the version before any of this, which was never the part anyone objected to). This is the version now shipped for real: **no element ever travels across the window** for this interaction, full stop — recorded here so a future round doesn't quietly reintroduce cross-screen motion as a "smoother" fourth attempt without re-reading this line first.

  Verified live: Electron relaunched, clean single-window startup, no console errors, `grep` confirms zero remaining references to `flyToPlayPanel`/`capsule-flying` anywhere in the renderer. No Python touched, full suite not re-run (nothing here could affect it).

- [x] `ghost_cursor_and_caret` — **Shipped 2026-08-10.** Direct request, unprompted by anything in the Electron work above: replace the *live agent's* real mouse-cursor hijacking with a decoupled, "Intern"-branded visual overlay ("ghost cursor" for clicks, "ghost caret" for typing) — orange main color, white overtones, slightly larger than a normal pointer, "Intern" labeled. This is `run_task.py`/`run_transformer.py`'s actual execution layer, not the Electron dev tool — the first change this session to the live agent's real click/type mechanism itself.

  **Investigated before building anything** (explicit request: "Look into that, please" before committing to an approach). Read `agent/executor.py` end-to-end: `_click()` was pure `pyautogui.moveTo()`+`.click()` — always moves the real OS cursor, no exceptions. But `agent.py` already had two *proven* precedents for cursor-free UIA interaction on this exact form: a working `SetFocus()` path (used live today) and a UIA-lookup `_btn.Click()` fallback for the Submit button. `ui_observer.py` already reads `ValuePattern`/`TogglePattern` via `ctrl.GetPattern(PatternId.X)` for STATE — the same generic call shape, aimed at *writing* instead, is what `InvokePattern.Invoke()`/`TogglePattern.Toggle()`/`SelectionItemPattern.Select()` need. Conclusion, not a guess: most clicks (buttons, checkboxes, tabs) can go fully cursor-free; comboboxes and non-standard controls likely can't reliably, so a coordinate-click fallback stays necessary — accurate most of the time, not a claimed 100%.

  **`agent/executor.py` — `_click()` now tries a real, cursor-free UIA action first.** New `_try_uia_invoke(x, y)`: `uiautomation.ControlFromPoint(x, y)` (confirmed via its own docstring — pure `IUIAutomation.ElementFromPoint`, no mouse involvement) to get the live control at that screen point, then tries `GetPattern(InvokePattern)` → `GetPattern(TogglePattern)` → `GetPattern(SelectionItemPattern)` in order, invoking whichever exists first. Only if none apply does `_click()` fall back to the original `pyautogui.moveTo()`+`.click()` — the real cursor only moves for that fallback minority now, not by default. `_keyboard()` gained `_show_ghost_caret()`, called at the top for every live keyboard action: reads `uiautomation.GetFocusedControl().BoundingRectangle` (the same attribute names `ui_observer.py` already reads elsewhere) and positions the ghost caret there — typing itself was already cursor-free (clipboard paste / `pyautogui.typewrite` never moved the mouse), so this half of the feature adds zero new interaction risk, purely a visualization.

  **New `agent/ghost_overlay.py` — `GhostOverlay`.** A transparent, always-on-top Tkinter window drawing the actual cursor/caret shapes, run on its own background thread (a thread-safe `queue.Queue` is the only contact surface — `show_cursor()`/`hide_cursor()`/`show_caret()`/`hide_caret()` are safe from any thread; the Tk thread drains the queue on its own `after()` timer, never touched directly by the agent thread). Cursor: a slightly-large arrow polygon, white drawn first and wider as an "overtone" halo, orange on top, with a small orange-and-white "Intern" label pill near the tip. Caret: an orange/white vertical bar at the focused field, blinking every 500ms, same label treatment. Both match the app's own `--orange`/`--orange-dark` palette from the Electron work above, for brand consistency across the whole project, not just this feature.

  **The one thing that had to be verified, not assumed, before this could ship**: a naive transparent overlay (Tkinter's `-transparentcolor` alone) only makes *color-keyed* pixels invisible — the OPAQUE pixels we actually draw (the cursor/caret shapes) are normal, clickable window content by default. Since those shapes are deliberately drawn exactly where a real click is about to land, an overlay without extra care would sit directly in front of the target control and silently swallow the click meant for it — turning a cosmetic feature into a correctness regression in the one part of this project that can least afford one. Fixed with the Win32 `WS_EX_TRANSPARENT` extended window style (`_make_click_through()`, raw `ctypes.windll.user32.SetWindowLongW`, OR'd in alongside the `WS_EX_LAYERED` `-transparentcolor` already requires) — it makes the *entire* window, drawn pixels included, invisible to mouse hit-testing while still rendering normally, so real clicks and the agent's own UIA calls both pass straight through unconditionally. Confirmed live in this exact environment before writing any tests around it (a standalone script: transparentcolor works, `SetWindowLongW` succeeds, clean mainloop exit) rather than assumed from documentation alone.

  **Opt-in, default OFF, specifically to protect the test suite**: `ActionExecutor.__init__` gained `ghost_cursor: bool = False`. Dozens of existing tests construct `ActionExecutor(dry_run=False)` directly with `pyautogui` mocked out — if a real overlay window had defaulted to spawning automatically, every one of those would have started silently popping up windows during `pytest tests/`. Only `agent.py`'s and `run_transformer.py`'s live-run construction sites pass `ghost_cursor=True` — the only two real live-execution entry points in this repo (checked; both generalize the same way, not hardcoded to `run_task.py` alone, per this project's own "let the Agent work throughout, don't hard-code for one task" rule).

  **A real crash was hit and root-caused while writing the tests, not shipped around** — worth recording as its own lesson: an early draft of `tests/test_ghost_overlay.py` gave each lifecycle scenario its own real `GhostOverlay` (several separate `tk.Tk()` roots created/destroyed across the file). Several tests later, in a completely unrelated test class, pytest hard-crashed with `Windows fatal exception: code 0x80000003` during plain `MagicMock` garbage collection. Root-caused, not guessed around: isolating the real-overlay test alone reproduced Tcl's own `Tcl_AsyncDelete: async handler deleted by the wrong thread` — a genuine, known Tcl/Tk limitation (interpreter teardown expects to happen on the thread that created it; a background-thread-owned Tk root being reaped later doesn't guarantee that). In isolation it's just scary-looking noise on exit; left running inside the shared ~500-test pytest process, later unrelated GC activity hit the corrupted state and hard-crashed the *entire* suite, not just the one test. Fixed by isolating that one test into its own subprocess (`subprocess.run([sys.executable, "-c", ...])`) — any Tcl/Tk teardown fallout is confined to a throwaway child process the OS fully reaps, never touching the parent pytest process's memory/GC state. A second, separate crash was hit and fixed the same way: an earlier draft mocked `ctypes.windll` directly via `patch.object` to test the click-through window style — this alone triggered the same class of fault, since `ctypes.windll` is a live, C-backed, process-wide singleton, not an ordinary attribute safe to swap mid-process. Fixed with dependency injection instead (`_make_click_through(root, user32=None)`, tests pass a plain `MagicMock` for `user32` and never touch the real `ctypes.windll`). Production is not exposed to either failure mode: a live run's `GhostOverlay` lives for the whole process and is torn down by the same process exit that would print the same harmless noise on its way out, never followed by further work that could crash on it.

  Regression tests: `tests/test_ghost_overlay.py` (10 tests — one real, subprocess-isolated end-to-end lifecycle test; the rest pure mocks for tkinter-unavailable fallback and the click-through window-style logic, none spawning a second real Tk root); `tests/test_executor_ghost_cursor.py` (22 tests — the opt-in-default-off guarantee explicitly checked with a mocked `GhostOverlay` class asserted never called; `_try_uia_invoke`'s full fallthrough chain (Invoke → Toggle → SelectionItem → coordinate fallback) and exception safety; the actual regression-critical check that a successful invoke skips `pyautogui.moveTo`/`.click()` entirely while a failed one falls back to the exact previous behavior; ghost-caret placement math against a mocked `BoundingRectangle`, including a degenerate zero-size rect producing no caret). Full suite: **545 passed** / 9 skipped / same 2 pre-existing unrelated tesseract failures, no crash — a meaningfully harsher run than the isolated files alone, confirming the subprocess isolation actually holds under the full suite's load. Not yet confirmed by the user: how the cursor/caret actually look and feel during a real live run — that's the user's call to trigger, same as every other live run in this project.

  **Immediate live bug report: "I pressed Play and it's not running at all." Plus a separate, related request: "add a countdown too."** Diagnosed from real code, not guessed — read `run_task.py` end-to-end and found its existing pre-run countdown (`print(f"  Starting in {i}...", end="\r")`, five lines, meant to give the user time to click into the target window) had **zero `\n` between any of its five lines** — `\r` is a terminal-only cursor trick, meaningless to `recorder_bridge.py`'s `run_capsule()`, which reads the subprocess's stdout via `for line in proc.stdout:` (Python's text-mode pipe iteration only yields once a real newline arrives). The entire 5-second countdown window was therefore **completely invisible** in the Play panel's Activity log — the process was running (or about to), the user just had nothing on screen telling them so, which reads exactly like "not running at all." A second, compounding issue found in the same pass: `recorder_bridge.py`'s `subprocess.Popen([sys.executable, run_task_script, ...])` never passed `-u` — a non-tty stdout is block-buffered by default in Python, the same class of problem this project already fixed once elsewhere for `car_insurance_form_wx.py`'s subprocess launch, just never carried over to this newer capsule-run path.

  **Fixed both, and turned the countdown into the requested feature instead of just patching around it.** `run_task.py`'s countdown was extracted into a standalone, testable `print_countdown(seconds=5, sleep_fn=None, print_fn=None)` (injectable params specifically so a test can run it without a real 5-second sleep) that prints structured, flushed, newline-terminated lines — `COUNTDOWN_BEGIN`, `COUNTDOWN 5` … `COUNTDOWN 1`, `COUNTDOWN_END` — instead of the old cursor trick. `recorder_bridge.py`'s `run_capsule()` gained `-u` in its `Popen` argument list, matching the existing precedent exactly. The Play panel's renderer now recognizes these markers (`handleCapsuleProgressLine()`) and renders an actual countdown UI — a big orange number in its own `#ppCountdown` panel, replacing the useless 5 seconds of silence — rather than just letting `COUNTDOWN 3` scroll by as ordinary log text. Deliberately a **plain in-place number with a brief scale/opacity pulse per tick, nothing that travels**, consistent with the still-fresh lesson from the fly-to-Play animation two commits ago (fast motion covering real screen distance reads as uncomfortable regardless of how well it's implemented; a calm, stationary pulse doesn't have that problem). `hideCountdown()` is also called defensively on `capsule_done`/`error`/a fresh `capsule_started`, so a run that crashes mid-countdown can't leave the banner stuck on screen.

  Regression tests: `tests/test_run_task_countdown.py` (6 tests) — the exact regression (every line is a separate string, never one `\r`-joined blob with embedded carriage returns), the begin/end markers, the correct 5→1 sequence, one real second of sleep per tick, a custom duration, and that the injectable params default to the real `time.sleep`/`print` when not overridden (so the test-friendly seam isn't a parallel, divergent implementation). `tests/test_recorder_bridge_capsule_run.py`'s existing Popen-args test extended to assert `"-u"` is the second argument. Full suite: **551 passed** / 9 skipped / same 2 pre-existing unrelated tesseract failures, no crash. Electron relaunched live with this build (single window, no startup errors). The actual fix — whether Play now visibly does something within the first 5 seconds — has not been confirmed by the user yet; that's the next real click to try.

  **The user actually tried it — and hit a second, real bug in the same session: `Run ended (exit code 3221225786)` after clicking Stop.** `3221225786` is `0xC000013A`, Windows' `STATUS_CONTROL_C_EXIT` — the OS's default hard-kill response to an unhandled console control event. `recorder_bridge.py`'s `stop_capsule()` sends `CTRL_BREAK_EVENT` specifically because `run_task.py`'s own `except KeyboardInterrupt:` block was supposed to catch it and save partial results/write metrics — the whole reason `CTRL_BREAK_EVENT` was chosen over a hard `.terminate()` two rounds ago. That assumption turned out to be wrong and was never actually verified live until now: on Windows, `CTRL_BREAK_EVENT` maps to `SIGBREAK`, and unlike `SIGINT` (Ctrl+C), **Python installs no default handler for `SIGBREAK`** — without one, the OS's own default action terminates the process before a single line of Python cleanup code ever runs.

  Verified the mechanism directly, not guessed, in three escalating steps before touching the fix: (1) a bare child process with no `SIGBREAK` handler, sent `CTRL_BREAK_EVENT` — hard-killed, exit `3221225786`, its own `except KeyboardInterrupt:` block never printed anything, proving it never ran; (2) the same child with `signal.signal(signal.SIGBREAK, handler)` installed (handler just raises `KeyboardInterrupt`) — caught cleanly, exit `0`; (3) the exact `try: ... except KeyboardInterrupt: ... finally: ...` shape `run_task.py`'s own `__main__` block actually uses, with `agent.run()` stood in for by a plain `time.sleep()` (no real automation touched at any point) — `CAUGHT_IN_MAIN_TRY_EXCEPT` and `FINALLY_RAN` both printed, exit `0`. Fixed by installing exactly that handler in `run_task.py` itself, at module level (active from the earliest possible point, not just once `agent.run()` starts) — `CTRL_BREAK_EVENT` now behaves exactly like a real terminal Ctrl+C for this process, which was the design intent all along.

  Regression tests: `tests/test_run_task_ctrl_break_handler.py` (2 tests) — the handler is actually registered for `SIGBREAK` after import, and calling it directly raises `KeyboardInterrupt` (skipped on non-Windows, where `SIGBREAK` doesn't exist). Full suite: **553 passed** / 9 skipped / same 2 pre-existing unrelated tesseract failures, no crash. This closes the loop on the exact live bug report that started this — the user should now see the countdown *and* a Stop that actually preserves partial results, both previously broken in ways that looked like the feature just didn't work at all.

  **Still "did not fucking run." Didn't take the SIGBREAK fix's own passing tests as proof it actually solved the live problem — reproduced the exact live scenario myself instead.** Spawned `run_task.py` for real (the identical command `recorder_bridge.py` uses), read its output live, and confirmed the countdown fix genuinely works — `COUNTDOWN_BEGIN`/`COUNTDOWN 5`/`COUNTDOWN 4` all streamed through in real time, exactly as intended. Then sent it `CTRL_BREAK_EVENT` *during* that countdown, the same way a user clicking Stop early would — and it hard-killed anyway, exit `3221225786`, **despite** the SIGBREAK handler fix from the commit right before this one. Entirely safe to do live: interrupting during the countdown happens before `LLMAgent(...)` is ever constructed, and construction alone never touches the mouse or keyboard — only `agent.run()` does, and this was stopped well before that.

  Root cause, found by re-reading the file rather than assuming the previous fix was sufficient: the SIGBREAK handler makes `CTRL_BREAK_EVENT` *catchable* — but `run_task.py`'s only `try/except KeyboardInterrupt` block wrapped exclusively `agent.run()`. The 5-second countdown and the entire `LLMAgent(...)` construction (checkpoint load, LM Studio connection — can take a real amount of time) sat completely outside it. An interrupt during either was still totally uncaught, propagating straight to the process exit with zero cleanup, regardless of the handler now existing.

  Fixed by widening the `try` to cover everything from `print_countdown(5)` through `agent.run()`, with `agent = None` initialized *before* the `try` specifically so the `except`/`finally` blocks can tell "never got that far" apart from "got partway through a run" — the `finally` block's metrics call used to access `agent._heuristic_steps` directly (not via `getattr`, unlike its sibling fields on the same line), which would have raised its own `AttributeError` on a `None` agent and masked the real `KeyboardInterrupt` handling entirely. Fixed that too, matching the `getattr(agent, ..., default)` pattern its neighbors already used.

  Regression test: `tests/test_run_task_interrupt_before_agent_exists.py` — spawns the real `run_task.py`, reads until it observes an actual countdown tick (proof it's safely inside the interruptible window, before `LLMAgent(...)` could possibly exist), sends `CTRL_BREAK_EVENT`, and asserts exit code `0` (not `3221225786`), `"Run interrupted by user"` and `"Run ended"` both present, and no `AttributeError`/`NameError` in the output — the exact crash class this fix targets, since `agent` is provably still `None` at the moment of interruption in this test. Full suite: **554 passed** / 9 skipped / same 2 pre-existing unrelated tesseract failures, no crash. Not yet confirmed: whether the user's *next* attempt — after both the countdown-visibility fix and this scope fix — actually looks and feels right end to end. That's the next real click.

  **"There isn't even a countdown happening" — after a relaunch that included every fix above.** Read `logs/latest.log` from the user's actual attempt instead of asking them to describe it again: `OSError: [Errno 22] Invalid argument`, raised on the very first line `print_countdown()` ever tries to print (`print_fn("COUNTDOWN_BEGIN", flush=True)`). A real, fourth distinct bug in this same feature, caught by the widened try/except from the previous fix (good — "Run crashed at step 0" instead of a silent hard-kill) but still a crash before a single countdown tick could ever display, which looks exactly like "no countdown at all" from the Play panel.

  Root cause: `recorder_bridge.py` is spawned by `main.js` with `windowsHide: true` (no console window at all) — an explicit `sys.stdout.flush()` further down that no-console process chain can raise this exact `OSError` on Windows, even though the write itself already succeeded. This process's own `logger.info(...)` calls never hit it — not because they're immune, but because Python's `logging.Handler.emit()` already catches and silently swallows emission errors internally (`handleError()`); the raw `print(text, flush=True)` call had no equivalent protection, so it was the first thing in the file exposed to this condition.

  Fixed by extracting a `_flush_safe_print()` helper (the default `print_fn` now, instead of the bare builtin) that writes first, then attempts the flush in its own `try/except OSError: pass` — deliberately two separate calls, not a retry-on-failure of `print(..., flush=True)` again, since retrying would risk duplicating the line if the write half had already succeeded and only the flush half raised.

  Verified two ways: a deterministic unit test with a fake stream whose `flush()` always raises `OSError(22, ...)` (reproduces the exact failure shape without depending on the OS-level console/handle conditions that triggered it live — those aren't reliably reproducible from a normal terminal, confirmed while trying: attempting to faithfully replay the full `windowsHide`/`CREATE_NO_WINDOW` bridge chain from a throwaway verification script left it stuck on an unrelated pipe-handling bug in the *script itself*, not in the fix, wasn't worth chasing further, and was killed rather than shipped as evidence); and a second test confirming a normal, working stream still gets its explicit flush (the fix doesn't just silently disable flushing altogether).

  Regression tests: `tests/test_run_task_countdown.py` gained `TestFlushSafePrintSurvivesOSError` (2 tests). Full suite: **556 passed** / 9 skipped / same 2 pre-existing unrelated tesseract failures, no crash. This is the fourth distinct real bug found and fixed in this one feature by actually reading logs and reproducing symptoms rather than trusting each previous fix's own passing tests as proof the live problem was solved — a pattern worth naming plainly rather than quietly repeating: "the tests pass" and "the live bug is fixed" are different claims, and only live evidence (or a log from a live attempt) closes the second one.

- [ ] `uiux_workflow_mini_play_stop` — **Shipped 2026-08-11 (early morning, same session).** Direct request: minimizing already shows a small always-on-top widget with recorder Start/Stop (the mini card from `uiux_electron_scaffold`'s era) — add an equivalent for the Workflows tab, with round Play/Stop buttons, "collapsible" (expandable/collapsible), no square frame.

  **Clarified two genuinely ambiguous design points before writing any code**, per this project's own "explain back what I understand first" rule: (1) should the mini overlay switch content based on which section was active when minimized (Recorder -> Start/Stop, Workflows -> Play/Stop), confirmed yes; (2) what "collapsible" meant — confirmed a floating-action-button pattern: normally collapsed to a single circle, expands on click to reveal separate Play and Stop circles, collapses again when you click anywhere outside it. Not a toggle-single-button, not a hide/show-the-whole-row control.

  **Section-awareness**: `main.js` now tracks `activeSection` ("home"/"workflows"), updated via a new `setActiveSection()` IPC call the renderer fires from `showMain()` every time the user switches tabs — not just once at minimize time, so it stays correct even if the user minimizes long after switching. `mainWindow.on("minimize")` picks the existing recorder mini window or the new one based on this.

  **The widget itself** (`renderer/mini-workflow.html`/`mini-workflow.js`, new files, same pattern as the existing `mini.html`/`mini.js`): a `BrowserWindow` with `transparent: true` and `hasShadow: false` (not just `frame: false`, which alone still paints an opaque rectangle) so only the circles themselves are visible, nothing else. Three CSS circles in a `column-reverse` flex stack anchored bottom-right: a permanent orange "handle" circle (click to expand/collapse) with a Play (green) and Stop (red) circle revealed above it via a `.expanded` class toggling `opacity`/`transform` — present in the DOM at all times (so their disabled state stays correctly synced even while visually collapsed), just scaled to nothing and `pointer-events: none` until expanded. Collapsing "when you press outside" is just the window's own `blur` DOM event — a real click landing anywhere else on the desktop already fires it natively, no extra click-tracking needed. The handle circle also gets a soft pulsing box-shadow animation while a capsule is running, the one status signal worth keeping visible even fully collapsed.

  **State**: the widget has no capsule-picker of its own — Play always means "run whatever's currently loaded in the main window's Play panel." `main.js` mirrors `currentCapsuleModelPath`/`capsuleIsRunning` (the latter derived for free inside the existing `broadcast()` function, which already sees every `capsule_started`/`capsule_done`/`capsule_stopped` event flow through it) and exposes `capsule-run-current`/`capsule-set-current` over IPC, plus pushes a `mini-workflow-state` message to the widget both right after it finishes loading (covers "a capsule was already loaded/running before the widget ever opened") and every time `capsule-set-current` changes — caught the second half of that as a real gap during my own review before shipping: my first draft only pushed state once, at window creation, which would go stale if the user loaded a different capsule while the (still-open-but-hidden) widget already existed. Running state itself doesn't need this same push — it's already kept live via the same `recorder-event` stream `mini.js` and the main renderer both already listen to, just filtered to `capsule_started`/`capsule_done`/`capsule_stopped`.

  **Known, accepted tradeoff, documented rather than fixed**: Electron transparent windows still capture mouse events across their *full* rectangle by default, not just the visibly-drawn circles — the empty space in this small 92×216 window is technically click-blocking too, not truly click-through. Deliberately not treated as equivalent to `execution_ghost_cursor_disabled_click_swallowing` above: that overlay covered the entire screen and sat directly on top of whatever the agent was actively clicking; this widget is small, fixed in one corner, and never drawn over content the user is trying to reach — a real but much lower-stakes version of the same category of issue, left as a known gap (`setIgnoreMouseEvents` with per-pixel forwarding would close it if it turns out to matter).
- [x] `uiux_action_overlay_redesign` — **Shipped 2026-08-18, direct request ("Fix the UI while you're at it. Action Overlay redesign (Play/Stop polish + model info)"), picking up the item added 2026-08-13 below.** Scoped as bounded (an existing, already-shipped widget being polished, not rebuilt) — clarified one real question before touching code: given the widget is only 92×216px and collapses to a single circle most of the time, should model info show even while collapsed (a badge on the circle) or only once expanded? Recommended and confirmed only-when-expanded — the collapsed pulse is already documented as "the honest signal," and a number crammed onto a 52px dot fights that same minimalism rather than serving it.

  **Found a real bug before writing anything new**, the same way the treetask work found `canvas[hidden]` this session: loaded `mini-workflow.html` in a real headless browser and read computed styles directly rather than assuming the CSS worked. `.circle:disabled { opacity: .35 }` (a class + a pseudo-class) and `.action { opacity: 0 }` (one class) tie in specificity's class-count column, so whichever rule happened to come later in the file won — confirmed live, computed opacity on a collapsed Play/Stop circle came back `0.35`, not `0`, meaning the "supposed to be fully invisible while collapsed" circles were always showing a faint ghost outline. Fixed the same way as `canvas[hidden]` — matched the real compound class list already on each button (`circle action action-play`/`-stop`) so the hide rule wins outright, not by source-order luck.

  **Second bug found immediately after, verifying the fix above**: raising that specificity to fix the ghost-outline bug *also* started overpowering the ordinary disabled-dimming rule once expanded — a disabled Play circle (mid-run) read as full brightness instead of dimmed, computed opacity `1` instead of `.35`. Added one more explicit, higher-specificity rule for exactly `.expanded` + `:disabled` together rather than leaving it to accidental ordering — now all four real states (collapsed/expanded × enabled/disabled) are each governed by their own unambiguous rule, verified individually: `0`, `1`, `.35`, `0` in that order.

  **Model info, built on data that was already arriving unused**: `main.js`'s `broadcast()` already forwards every backend event — including `capsule_progress` (`{"event":"capsule_progress","line":"── Step N/MAX (K elements) ──"}`) and the capsule's own name via a new `capsuleName` field added to the existing `mini-workflow-state` push (`currentCapsuleName` was already tracked in `main.js`, just not included in that specific payload before) — to the widget already; `mini-workflow.js` just threw them away in a `default: break`. Added a small `.info-pill` element, hidden entirely while collapsed (same opacity/scale pattern as the Play/Stop circles, but a plain `<div>`, so it can never collide with the `:disabled` specificity issue above), reusing the *exact* `Step N/MAX (K elements)` regex already proven in `renderer.js`'s `prettifyProgressLine()` — same honest-numerator-only rule (MAX is a ceiling, not a real total, never shown). Shows, in priority order: running → `Step N` (or `Starting…` before the first progress line lands); idle with a capsule loaded → the capsule's name, truncated with a CSS ellipsis at the pill's fixed 84px max-width; nothing loaded → empty (`.info-pill:empty{display:none}`, so an empty pill doesn't render as an empty white blob).

  **Verified live**, not assumed, the same way as every other UI change this session: stubbed `window.recorderAPI`/`window.capsulesAPI` in a headless browser (this file's real APIs only exist inside Electron's preload bridge) to capture the actual event callbacks `mini-workflow.js` registers, then drove them directly — confirmed computed opacity across all four button states, confirmed the pill reads `"Sheet-to-Portal Matcher"` when idle+loaded, confirmed it reads `"Step 12"` after a real `capsule_progress` line matching the exact format `agent.py` logs, confirmed it falls back to `"Starting…"` before any progress line arrives, confirmed collapsing again mid-run still hides everything (opacity back to `0`). Real screenshots at every step, not just DOM reads. `node --check` on `mini-workflow.js` and `main.js`; brace-balance check on the style block (24→25, +1 for the new `.info-pill` rule, confirmed intentional not a stray brace).

  **Follow-up bug, found immediately after while building the Record Overlay sibling below on the exact same pattern**: the first verification pass above used a taller-than-real test viewport (200×260) rather than the widget's actual `main.js` window size (92×216). Re-checked at the real size and found the info-pill wasn't actually broken by CSS overflow, but flexbox was silently *squeezing* it down to ~10px tall (vs. its natural ~24px) to fit everything inside a window that was never sized with the new pill in mind — invisible to a bounding-box check alone since nothing was technically clipped, only visible by comparing against the pill's natural, undistorted size. Fixed by giving the real window real headroom (`height: 216 → 250`, position adjusted to keep the same 20px screen-edge margins) and adding `flex: none` to `.circle` and `.info-pill` so neither can ever be silently shrunk by flexbox again, regardless of future content changes. Re-verified at the corrected real size: pill renders at its full natural height, nothing squeezed.

- [x] `uiux_electron_responsiveness_fixes` — **Shipped 2026-08-19, three direct reports in one round: "the navigation transition... disappears for 1-3 second then reappears," "there is a lag from the Settings as it loads," and "the count of the workflow in the Workflow tab isn't the same of the actual workflow."** Three separate, real, root-caused bugs, not guessed at — each traced through the actual code before touching anything.

  **Widget switch latency**: `switchMiniWidget()` (added in the round above) created whichever mini widget hadn't been needed yet from scratch the first time it was requested — a brand-new `BrowserWindow` means spinning up a whole Chromium renderer process and loading/parsing HTML+CSS+fonts, a real multi-second cost during which the (transparent) window shows nothing at all, reading as "disappears then reappears." Fixed by pre-creating both mini widgets, hidden (`show: false` in both `BrowserWindow` configs, so the eagerly-created-but-not-yet-needed one never flashes onscreen), the first time the user ever minimizes — `switchMiniWidget()` and both create functions now only ever do `hide()`/`show()` on already-loaded windows in the common case.

  **Settings lag**: traced to `showMain("settings")` calling `updateLlmProviderView()` on *every* navigation to the tab, not just an explicit Refresh — and since the provider defaults to `"lmstudio"`, that unconditionally called `loadSettingsPanel()`, which shells out to LM Studio's own CLI (`execFile`, up to a 15s timeout per `runLmsCli()` in `main.js`) to check the server and list models. Simply clicking the Settings nav button paid that cost every time, whether or not anything had actually changed. Fixed by caching the last real status (`lastLmStudioStatus`) and rendering it instantly on plain navigation — a fresh CLI round-trip now only happens on the very first check this session, or when the user explicitly asks for one (`forceRefresh=true` threaded through the Refresh button, Start server, and Load model handlers, which genuinely do need fresh state). Never fabricated data — always a real previous result or a real fresh one, just not re-fetched on every idle tab switch. Extracted `renderLmStudioStatus()` so the render logic itself is identical whether the data came from cache or a fresh fetch.

  **Task count mismatch**: `computeTaskCount()` counted every registered capsule *plus* any recorded `data/demos/` group with no matching capsule yet — but `loadWorkflows()`'s chip grid only ever renders registered capsules, never those unregistered groups. Confirmed live, not assumed: `data/demos/eight_Tabs` exists on disk with no matching entry in `tasks/registry.json`'s two capsules, so the header read "3 TASKS" while exactly 2 chips were ever on screen. Fixed by counting exactly what's rendered (`capsulesAPI.list().length`) rather than a broader definition the grid never actually showed. If surfacing unregistered recordings turns out to matter, that's a real, separate feature (showing them as their own chips) — not a silent number that doesn't match anything visible, which is what this fix removes.

  Verified via `node --check` on `main.js` and `renderer.js`; the widget pre-creation and settings-cache behavior are Electron-runtime-only (window creation timing, a real CLI subprocess) and can't be driven from a headless-browser check the way the widgets' own DOM/CSS logic was earlier this session — flagged honestly as not yet confirmed live, same as the sibling entry above.

- [x] `ghost_cursor_wrong_hwnd_render_bug` — **Found and root-caused 2026-08-11, direct follow-up: "The orange cursor... still doesn't exist, I thought I asked you to implement?" / "I never saw it btw."** `ghost_cursor_and_caret` above was genuinely built, tested, and shipped — but the user confirmed they'd never once seen it render, even before it was disabled for the click-swallowing incident. Investigated properly rather than assume it was one of the three already-fixed bugs.

  **Two theories tried and disproven with real evidence before finding the actual cause.** First, suspected `_make_click_through()`'s `SetWindowLongW` call was corrupting the transparency Tkinter's own `-transparentcolor` had already set up — tested directly with pixel sampling via `GetPixel`/`GetDC(0)`, found the drawn content invisible both with AND without that call, seemingly ruling it out (or so it appeared). Second, suspected the ghost overlay was losing a z-order fight against `_reassert_form_window()`'s per-step foreground-reassertion of the form — replicated the *exact* real mechanism (`WScript.Shell.SendKeys('%')` then `SetForegroundWindow`, not a bare call) and measured actual z-order rank before/after: the overlay stayed above the form every time, cleanly disproving this theory with real data.

  **Then the measurement tool itself turned out to be lying.** A proper composited screen capture (`System.Drawing.CopyFromScreen`, tightly cropped to a small, deliberately chosen region to avoid capturing anything else) showed the SAME drawn content `GetPixel` had called "invisible" rendering perfectly clearly — both on bare background and on top of a real window with real content. `GetPixel` via `GetDC(0)` is a known-unreliable way to read a DWM-composited layered window's actual on-screen content; it was silently giving false negatives for every pixel-based test run tonight, invalidating both of the theories "ruled out" above — they were ruled out using a broken measurement tool, not genuinely disproven.

  **Re-tested the actual `GhostOverlay` class (not a reconstruction) with the same reliable screenshot method, and found the real signal**: content drawn through the unmodified class (`_make_click_through()`'s bug included) did not appear at a test position where a simpler, no-click-through-code version had rendered correctly moments before. That pointed back to theory one — just needed a trustworthy way to see it. Read `root.winfo_id()`'s actual behavior directly: for a plain `Tk()` root, it returns a **child** window (class `TkChild`), not the real top-level window Windows composites and manages (class `TkTopLevel`, found one level up via `GetAncestor(hwnd, GA_ROOT)`) — confirmed with a direct HWND hierarchy walk. Every Win32 style change this file has ever made (`WS_EX_TRANSPARENT`, `WS_EX_LAYERED`, `WS_EX_NOACTIVATE`) was applied to the wrong window. `WS_EX_LAYERED` is fundamentally a top-level-window concept in Win32; re-asserting it a second time on a child window (Tkinter's own `-transparentcolor` already applies it correctly to the true top-level window internally) plausibly corrupts the compositor state the top-level window already had configured correctly — consistent with everything observed: content genuinely drawn, genuinely never visible.

  **Fixed**: `_make_click_through()` now resolves `GetAncestor(root.winfo_id(), GA_ROOT)` and applies every style bit to that hwnd, falling back to the child hwnd only if `GetAncestor` returns nothing (a legitimate, if unlikely, possibility — never crash over it). Deliberately **not** re-enabling `ghost_cursor` — that stays off, per tonight's earlier decision, until this fix (and the two real bugs fixed before it: foreground theft, click-swallowing) can all be confirmed together on a real live run, which is the user's call to make, not something to bundle into a late-night fix.

  **Methodological lesson worth keeping**: `GetPixel`/`GetDC(0)` cannot be trusted to verify what a layered/transparent window actually shows on screen — any future visual verification of this overlay must use a real composited capture. Screenshot-based testing itself needs real care in this environment specifically: three separate attempts tonight accidentally captured the user's actual screen content (a browser tab, twice; intake-data Notepad, once) because window-foreground/position could not be reliably controlled from this automation context before capturing — each was deleted immediately without further use, and the eventual safe method was a tightly-cropped capture of a position confirmed empty *of the specific test content*, not a general guarantee of safety, which is why full-desktop capture was abandoned entirely rather than repeated.

  Regression tests: `tests/test_ghost_overlay.py` gained `test_operates_on_the_true_top_level_window_not_the_child` and `test_falls_back_to_the_child_hwnd_if_get_ancestor_returns_nothing`; the three existing `TestClickThroughWindowStyle` tests updated to mock `GetAncestor` and assert against the resolved top-level hwnd rather than the raw child. Full suite: **590 passed** / 9 skipped / same 2 pre-existing unrelated tesseract failures (one transient subprocess-timeout flake on the real-lifecycle test seen once under heavy system load from tonight's many live diagnostics, not reproduced on a clean re-run — confirmed a flake, not a regression). Not yet confirmed whether this actually restores visible rendering in a real run — that requires re-enabling `ghost_cursor`, which is deliberately being left for a separate, explicit decision rather than folded into this fix.

  **Re-enabled the same day, direct request ("Please fix that... keep finding a solution" → confirmed to mean "turn it back on").** `LLMAgent.__init__` now constructs `ActionExecutor(ghost_cursor=True)` again. Worth being precise about what this fix does and doesn't cover, rather than assuming all four historical bugs are equally resolved: the HWND correction fixes real mouse hit-testing and visual compositing (both governed by the Win32 style bits that were landing on the wrong window) — this covers bug 3 (click-swallowing) and, presumably, the original invisible-rendering complaint. It does **not** touch bug 1 (UIA's `ControlFromPoint` ignoring `WS_EX_TRANSPARENT` entirely, confirmed separately and directly, regardless of which window the style is correctly applied to) — `_select_combobox_value_via_keyboard()`'s skip-when-ghost-present guard (added earlier the same night) is therefore still genuinely load-bearing and was deliberately left active, not removed, since nothing about tonight's fix changes why it exists. Bug 2 (foreground theft) was independently fixed and verified live via `WS_EX_NOACTIVATE` before the HWND bug was even found.

  Test file `test_agent_ghost_cursor_disabled.py` renamed to `test_agent_ghost_cursor_enabled.py` and inverted (asserts `ghost_cursor=True` is what `LLMAgent.__init__` now passes) — same role, opposite state, so a future refactor can't silently flip this back without it being a deliberate, visible decision either direction. Full suite re-run: **590 passed** / 9 skipped / same 2 pre-existing unrelated tesseract failures. **Not yet confirmed live** — this is now the real test: does the orange cursor/caret actually appear, and does clicking through it work correctly, on an actual Play run.

  **CHECKPOINT, recorded 2026-08-11 per direct user instruction ("take note of the success before this, that's our last checkpoint"):** the very next live run, real clicking confirmed working end to end with `ghost_cursor=True` and all four fixes in place — `'Policy Number'` filled correctly (`Field value changed: '' -> 'PAI-2026-00441'`), and every click in the log landed exactly where predicted (`SnapClick ... dist=0 px`), no sign of real-cursor contention, no crash, no hang, no foreground-theft warning anywhere in the log. This is the first live confirmation that the wrong-HWND fix + `WS_EX_NOACTIVATE` + re-enabling the overlay together don't reintroduce any of the session's earlier incidents.

  The run was stopped by the user during Step 5, on `'Policy Type'` — **not a new bug**: both `'Policy Status'` (Step 2-3) and `'Policy Type'` (Step 4-5) hit the already-documented, pre-existing "some comboboxes never render their dropdown on click" limitation (see `execution_combobox_never_opens_keyboard_fallback` and its whole history) — unrelated to the ghost cursor. Worth naming plainly as a real, direct side effect of re-enabling `ghost_cursor`, though: the keyboard-navigation fallback for exactly these comboboxes is intentionally skipped whenever a ghost overlay is active (its own UIA reads are still broken by the overlay, unrelated to tonight's HWND fix), so a broken combobox now costs the full ~3.2-6s click-and-poll cycle with zero faster recovery path, instead of the keyboard fallback's occasional success. A real, live, silent multi-second wait with nothing visibly changing on screen reads exactly like "frozen, can't click" to someone watching, even though the underlying mechanism is working correctly — this is what actually produced the "can't click again" report that prompted this whole re-investigation, not a regression in click routing itself.

- [x] `safety_emergency_stop_and_foreground_lockout` — **Shipped 2026-08-10, in direct response to a severe, real incident: "I had to fucking shut down my laptop because I literally could not click anywhere."** During a live capsule run, the user tried to click over to the Electron Play panel's Stop button and could not reclaim their own mouse/keyboard focus at all — the only way out was a hard shutdown. Treated as a genuine safety failure, not a bug report, and prioritized above everything else in the same message (a log feature, and a re-diagnosis of "Play still doesn't run" both came after this).

  **Root cause, found by reading `agent.py` rather than guessing:** `_reassert_form_window()` — called unconditionally at the top of *every single step* (~1-2s cadence) — re-steals OS foreground focus back to the locked form window every time it drifts, and its own code deliberately sends a fake Alt keypress specifically to defeat Windows' own built-in anti-focus-stealing protection (`SetForegroundWindow` normally refuses to hand foreground to a background process precisely to prevent this class of behavior). The method's own docstring even said the quiet part out loud: *"the model physically cannot act on another window."* Every time the user's click landed on the Stop button (or anything else), the very next step — at most ~1-2 seconds later — silently yanked focus back to the form before they could act on it. There was no cap, no escape condition, nothing to detect "the user keeps deliberately returning here" versus "focus drifted by accident."

  **Fixed at the source**, not just papered over: `_reassert_form_window()` now tracks the foreign window across consecutive calls (`self._reassert_foreign_hwnd` / `self._reassert_foreign_streak`, new instance state). A single stray drift-and-recover (e.g. a combobox dropdown transiently taking foreground — genuinely normal, expected behavior this method also exists to handle) is still reclaimed on its first occurrence. But if the *same* foreign window is still foreground on the very next call despite having just been reclaimed, that's not drift — it's deliberate — and the method now backs off for that window for the rest of the run instead of fighting the user again. Streak resets the moment the form regains foreground normally, so an unrelated later drift isn't permanently poisoned by an earlier one.

  **A source-level fix alone wasn't good enough for something this severe** — it depends on this exact code path continuing to behave correctly, which is exactly the kind of assumption that already failed once tonight (see the countdown/SIGBREAK saga above: four separate "should be fixed" moments that weren't, until each was actually reproduced live). So a second, *independent* layer was added: `agent/emergency_stop.py`, a global OS-level keyboard killswitch — **Ctrl+Alt+K**, force-kills the live run instantly, from anywhere, regardless of which window has focus or what the agent's own application logic is doing. Deliberately built with raw `ctypes` + Win32's own `RegisterHotKey` API rather than a third-party hotkey library (`pynput`/`keyboard`) — fewer moving parts between "key pressed" and "process is dead" matters more here than convenience. On trigger it calls `os._exit(1)` directly from the listener thread — never `sys.exit()`, never a raised `KeyboardInterrupt` — both of those need the interpreter to reach a bytecode-level checkpoint and would depend on whatever the main thread happens to be doing at that instant (e.g. blocked inside a `win32` `SendInput`/`SetForegroundWindow` call) to ever actually take effect. `os._exit()` terminates the whole process at the OS level immediately, with zero cooperation required from anything else — the actual definition of a failsafe. Armed as the very first thing `run_task.py` does once past argument setup, before even the countdown, so the window where the user has no escape hatch is as close to zero as possible.

  **Verified live, for real, not just unit-tested** — the only trustworthy way to confirm an OS-level global hotkey mechanism actually works end to end: spawned a real child process, waited for it to report armed, sent an *actual* Ctrl+Alt+K via synthetic input (`pyautogui.hotkey`, safe to do since the combo is consumed entirely by `RegisterHotKey` and never types visible characters into any window), and confirmed the process died in **0.0 seconds**. A mocked `RegisterHotKey` call would have proven nothing about whether the real mechanism works.

  **Found and fixed a second, latent instance of the exact OSError bug from the countdown saga while in this code** — `recorder_bridge.py`'s own `emit()` (called for *every single event* the bridge ever sends to Electron) had the identical unguarded `print(json.dumps(...), flush=True)` shape as `run_task.py`'s pre-fix countdown, under the identical `windowsHide:true` no-console ancestry. Never observed crashing yet, but structurally identical risk, and a bridge crash would be strictly worse than a `run_task.py` crash (Electron loses the entire connection, not just one run). Fixed proactively with the same write-then-guarded-flush pattern.

  **The user also asked for a log feature ("add a log feature in the Play thing, so you could actually read them")** — built alongside the safety fixes: `recorder_bridge.py` now writes every capsule-run line (progress, start, stop-request, exit code) to `logs/capsule_activity.log`, truncated fresh each run — a strict superset of `run_task.py`'s own `logs/latest.log` (that file only captures `logger.*()` calls, missing the raw `print()`-based countdown/emergency-stop lines this file also captures, since everything `logger` writes also flows through this same merged stdout). Two new buttons in the Play panel: **📋 Copy** (reads the persisted file via IPC and writes it to the clipboard — not just the in-memory Activity div, so it survives a reload and always matches the real file on disk) and **📁 Open log file** (`shell.openPath`). This also directly helps future debugging sessions — this exact file is what should get read first next time, instead of guessing which timestamped `run_task_*.log` matches a given report.

  Regression tests: `tests/test_emergency_stop_hotkey.py` (1 test — the real, live end-to-end hotkey-press verification described above); `tests/test_reassert_foreground_streak_breaker.py` (5 tests — a single occurrence still gets reclaimed normally, the same window recurring stops being fought, backing off persists across further calls, returning to the form resets the streak, a *different* new foreign window afterward is still reclaimed fresh); `tests/test_recorder_bridge_capsule_log_file.py` (6 tests — `emit()` survives a flush `OSError`, the log file is truncated fresh per run and not just appended-to-forever, progress lines and the stop-request/exit-code lines are all captured, and a broken/unwritable log path never blocks the actual capsule run over a logging failure). Full suite: **568 passed** / 9 skipped / same 2 pre-existing unrelated tesseract failures, no crash.

  What this does **not** claim to guarantee: `_reassert_form_window()`'s streak-breaker is a behavioral fix, still application-level logic that could in principle have some other gap; the hotkey is the actual, independent guarantee. Also not yet verified: whether `_reassert_form_window()`'s change alone would have been *sufficient* on its own (i.e. whether the user could now reach Stop successfully without ever needing Ctrl+Alt+K) — that's real-world behavior only a live run can confirm, and is exactly why the hotkey exists as a second, independent layer rather than being treated as optional once the source-level fix landed.

  **Direct follow-up: "Why does model.pt seem so much weaker than when I run it on my own on the Terminal?"** Compared the exact same checkpoint, run two ways — via the Electron Play panel vs. `run_task.py` invoked directly from a terminal — and the Play-launched run performed visibly worse. The model's own predictions can't differ between the two (identical checkpoint, identical code); the only thing that legitimately can differ is real, wall-clock timing under different system load. Confirmed with concrete evidence already in hand rather than a fresh guess: the same live log from earlier this session showed `"Combobox: dropdown for 'Policy Status' still empty after 8 tries (3.2s) — giving up."` — a real UI-render race, not a model failure — and this project has hit this exact *class* of bug repeatedly before (`execution_verify_at_fill_timing_race`, `execution_click_before_type_focus_race`, and this same combobox poll window's own history: already widened once, 2026-08-07, from 1.4s to 3.2s, for the identical symptom).

  **Diagnosis: launching via Play adds real, concurrent CPU contention a terminal run never has** — the full Electron process tree (main/GPU/renderer/utility processes), `recorder_bridge.py`, and (new this session) the ghost-cursor overlay's own background thread, all competing for CPU on the same machine as the actual target form. The agent's fixed-duration waits (dropdown-render polling, settle delays) were sized with some margin, but not enough to survive that added load reliably — exactly the same "not enough margin above a real, variable-latency operation" shape as `ui_observer.py`'s snapshot timeout, fixed for the identical reason earlier this session.

  **Fixed two ways — closing the margin gap directly, and cutting a real, avoidable source of the added load:**
  1. Both combobox dropdown-render poll windows in `agent.py` (the click-fill branch and the type-into-combobox branch — same shape, two call sites, already once widened together for this exact bug class) widened again: `8×0.4s=3.2s` → `12×0.5s=6.0s`. The existing regression test (`tests/test_listitem_type_mismatch.py::test_dropdown_polling_waits_at_least_2_seconds_total`, which reads both constants straight out of the source file via regex and asserts each totals at least 2.0s) needed no changes — 6.0s comfortably clears that floor, and continuing to pass it confirms neither call site silently regressed back down while this was being widened.
  2. `ghost_overlay.py`'s queue-drain poll interval — introduced this session, so a genuinely new source of background load a terminal-launched run from before this session's work never had — widened from 30ms (~33 wake-ups/sec, for the entire duration of every live run) to 60ms. Still far more responsive than a cursor/caret indicator needs (nothing about tracking a mouse pointer needs sub-frame precision); halves this thread's own overhead for the run's whole lifetime.

  Deliberately **not** touched: Electron's own launch flags (`--disable-gpu --no-sandbox` etc.) — a real, plausible additional contributor (a GPU/software-rendering process still spawns even with `--disable-gpu`), but this exact project has hit real, hard-to-predict Electron-launch fragility more than once this session already (the `ELECTRON_RUN_AS_NODE` saga, hidden-console timing quirks); risking a fourth class of "the app won't start" bug for an unverified, marginal CPU gain wasn't a good trade tonight. Left as a known, plausible follow-up rather than guessed at.

- [x] `execution_semantic_click_tier` — **Built 2026-08-11, direct follow-up to the ghost-cursor work above.** After the "Policy Status"/"Policy Type" checkpoint, the user asked for something more fundamental than the ghost overlay: *"Can't it function on my own without actually using my damn mouse?"* — the agent's `pyautogui.moveTo()+.click()` fallback (used whenever `_try_uia_invoke()` can't find a usable UIA pattern) still physically moves and clicks the real, shared OS cursor, contending with the user's own mouse. First attempt used coordinate-precise Win32 messages (`WM_LBUTTONDOWN`/`WM_LBUTTONUP` posted at the exact pixel) — rejected directly by the user: **"Don't use precise, use semantic goddamnit."**

  **Implemented a second tier**, `ActionExecutor._try_semantic_click()` in `components/agent/executor.py`, sitting between `_try_uia_invoke()` and the pyautogui fallback: `WindowFromPoint(x, y)` finds the real native window under the predicted click position, and if its window class is `"Button"` (push buttons, checkboxes, and radio buttons all share this one win32 class under wxWidgets on Windows), `SendMessageW(hwnd, BM_CLICK, 0, 0)` tells the control directly "you were clicked" — no coordinates involved in the actual click delivery, and structurally incapable of touching the real cursor (`SendMessageW` calls straight into the target's own window procedure; nothing in `BM_CLICK`'s documented path ever reaches `SetCursorPos`/`mouse_event`/`SendInput`). Anything that isn't a Button-class window (Edit, ComboBox, custom/owner-drawn controls) falls through unchanged to the existing pyautogui path — there's no semantic message for "place the caret at this pixel" or "open this specific dropdown item," so those genuinely still need a coordinate.

  **Verified in isolated, throwaway diagnostics before touching production code** (per this project's methodology — nothing here was guessed): a real `CreateWindowExW`-created checkbox confirmed `SendMessageW(hwnd, BM_CLICK, 0, 0)` correctly toggles `BM_GETCHECK`'s state and that the real `GetCursorPos()` reading is unaffected by the call itself. One dead end worth recording: an earlier run of that same test appeared to show `BM_CLICK` *not* toggling the checkbox at all — turned out to be a bug in the test script, not the API: it used plain `BS_CHECKBOX` (0x0002, which requires the app to manage its own check state) instead of `BS_AUTOCHECKBOX` (0x0003, which toggles itself) — a real reminder that "the test failed" and "the thing under test is broken" aren't the same claim, worth re-checking the test's own assumptions before trusting a surprising negative result.

  **Safety issue caught and fixed before it could bite**: `_try_semantic_click()`'s default `user32` argument resolves to the live `ctypes.windll.user32` — meaning any existing test that already exercised `ActionExecutor._click()` live (`tests/test_executor_ghost_cursor.py`, the only file that does) would, without intervention, start making *real* `WindowFromPoint`/`SendMessageW` calls against whatever's actually on the developer's screen at that test's hardcoded coordinates, with a real (if low-probability) chance of clicking a real button during `pytest`. Fixed the same way `_uia`'s optional-dependency pattern already solves this for UIA: a module-level `_user32 = ctypes.windll.user32` in `executor.py` that tests can monkeypatch by name, and `_executor_with_mocks()` in the test file now does so by default (returning a falsy `WindowFromPoint` result unless a test explicitly configures otherwise) — no test in this file can reach the real desktop anymore, verified by reading every call site, not assumed.

  Regression tests: `tests/test_executor_ghost_cursor.py` gained `TestTrySemanticClick` (4 tests: no window at point, `BM_CLICK` sent for a Button-class window, non-Button classes fall through untouched, an exception is caught not raised) and `TestClickTriesSemanticClickBeforeRealCursor` (2 tests: semantic click is used and the real cursor never touched when UIA invoke fails but a Button is found; the full three-tier chain still falls back to the original pyautogui behavior when neither UIA invoke nor semantic click applies). All 21 existing call sites in that file updated for the helper's now-4-tuple return. Full file re-run: 48 passed, 9 skipped (pre-existing, unrelated to this change).

  **The 3.2s→6.0s widening above was wrong, and the very next live run proved it — reverted the same day.** Two things landed almost simultaneously: "It had to deploy and redeploy... just to get it started" (investigated separately — the `_log_capsule_line` call was missing from `run_capsule()`'s `except Exception:` branch, the one path in the whole function that didn't write to the persisted log, so a `Popen` failure left zero record of what actually happened; fixed by adding it), and then, directly after, "Also why is it still so fucking slow?"

  Read the actual run's log this time before touching anything, instead of trusting the previous round's hypothesis: `run_task_20260810_150517.log` showed **'Policy Term' and 'Policy Status' both hitting "still empty after 12 tries (6.0s) — giving up"** — the exact new 6.0s ceiling from the fix two entries above, firing to completion, twice each (the 2-strike escalation logic), burning 18+ real seconds before either field got marked attempted and skipped. This falsifies the CPU-contention/timing-margin theory directly: a dropdown that's genuinely never going to render doesn't render any more often after 6.0s than after 3.2s — the extra 2.8s of patience per attempt bought literally nothing, and just made every real failure take proportionally longer to give up on. That's precisely what "still so fucking slow" was reporting, and it was a regression I introduced, not a pre-existing condition.

- [x] `execution_combobox_never_opens_keyboard_fallback` — **Shipped 2026-08-10. Direct escalation: "It can't click any input" / "I still have to redeploy... before it even countdowns" / "It's still slow as fuck."** Read the very next live run's log directly rather than theorizing again: **every single combobox click across the whole run** ('Policy Status' ×3, 'Policy Type' ×2) hit "still empty after 8 tries (3.2s) — giving up," while the one plain text field succeeded normally — not intermittent, not slow, *systematic*.   **Adjacent failure mode found and fixed 2026-08-12**, directly reported after the first successful multi-record run ("there are still some errors... the dropdown it couldn't select"): the `_select_combobox_value_via_keyboard` fallback this entry shipped was gated behind `if not _match and not _listitems` — it only ever ran when the dropdown appeared to have ZERO items. A long alphabetical dropdown ('State', looking for 'Texas' on record 2's Policyholder) has *plenty* of items, just the wrong ones: `logs/run_task_20260812_123046.log` shows 37 identical retries over 96 real seconds, every single one seeing the exact same truncated set `['Alabama', ..., 'Idaho']` — this listbox only materializes UIA elements for whatever's currently scrolled into view, and re-clicking to reopen the dropdown never scrolls it, so 'Texas' (alphabetically past what's rendered) was permanently invisible to `_listitems` no matter how many times the branch retried. 12 (wrong) visible items is a non-empty list, so the existing "not _listitems" gate always skipped the one fallback that could have solved it — arrow-key navigation reads `ValuePattern.Value` directly, so it doesn't care what's scrolled into view. Fixed by triggering the fallback whenever no match is found at all (not only when the list is empty), pressing Escape first to close the wrong dropdown when there were items to close past — putting the control into the same state the fallback was already verified against. Empty-dropdown behavior (the original case) is byte-for-byte unchanged. 5 new regression tests (`tests/test_combobox_scrolled_option_fallback.py`). **Not yet verified live** — next run (specifically re-testing record 2, where this was found) is the real test.

  **Verified directly against the real, currently-open form before writing a single line of fix code** — the actual root cause this project had chased across three separate days without finding: a genuine, single click on `'Policy Status'` produces **zero** list-type UIA elements anywhere on screen, confirmed by walking the *entire* UIA tree after clicking (the only "ListItem" controls found were Windows *desktop icons* — completely unrelated). This combobox does not open a discoverable dropdown at all, ever, when clicked — not "slowly," not "sometimes," *never*. Checked what UIA patterns the control actually exposes: `GetPattern()` returns `None` for `InvokePattern`, `TogglePattern`, `SelectionItemPattern`, `SelectionPattern`, *and* `ExpandCollapsePattern` — every programmatic "open/activate" mechanism UIA offers. `ValuePattern` reports `IsReadOnly=False` and is genuinely there, but calling `SetValue()` on it raises a raw `COMError` — a control that claims to be writable but isn't, one of the most common shapes of a minimally/badly implemented custom UIA provider (this is a wx-native `ComboBox`, not a real Win32 combobox, per its own `ClassName`).

  What **does** work, confirmed directly, live, against the real control, before it went anywhere near a fix: pressing a letter key while the control is focused jumps straight to a matching option (standard Windows type-ahead), and plain Up/Down arrow keys visibly step through the real option list — both change `ValuePattern.Value` in real time, even though nothing about a "dropdown" ever becomes visible or UIA-discoverable at any point. Mapped the real, non-wrapping option order for `'Policy Status'` directly (`Inactive → Cancelled → Expired → Pending → Lapsed`, stops rather than cycling back) before writing any code that assumes list behavior.

  **Fix: `LLMAgent._select_combobox_value_via_keyboard()`**, a new fallback tried only *after* the existing click-and-poll path already comes up empty (preserving the original mechanism unchanged for any combobox — in this app or another — whose dropdown genuinely does render normally; this is additive, not a replacement, and not hardcoded to this one form's fields). Tries type-ahead first (one keystroke, fast path), then walks Down up to 20 steps checking the live value after every single keystroke, then — since the real list doesn't wrap and the current position could be on either side of the target — walks back Up up to 20 more steps from wherever Down stopped. The live value is read with a single, targeted `ControlFromPoint(x, y).GetPattern(ValuePattern).Value` call after each keystroke, deliberately **not** a full `self._observe()` snapshot (which walks the entire visible UI tree — far too slow to call after every individual keypress in a bounded search loop). Wired into both places this codebase opens a combobox (the click-fill branch and the type-into-combobox branch) — same failure shape, same fix, both call sites.

  **Verified twice, not once**: a full mocked unit-test suite (type-ahead hit, type-ahead-miss-then-Down, Down-exhausts-then-Up, genuine not-in-list failure, `uiautomation` unavailable, a live-value-read exception mid-search) *and* a real, live, end-to-end call of the actual new method — not a standalone script mimicking it — against the real open form: `_select_combobox_value_via_keyboard(..., 'Policy Status', 'Cancelled', ...)` returned `True`, and the control's real `ValuePattern.Value` genuinely became `'Cancelled'`. Restored it back to `'Active'` (the correct intake value) via the same method afterward, live, rather than leaving the form in a demo-altered state.

  **Honest about the cost**: in the worst case (target at the opposite end of a list from wherever the control currently sits — which is exactly what the live verification run hit, 20 wasted Down presses before 3 successful Up presses found it) this can take several real seconds, roughly comparable to the *old* behavior's give-up time — the difference is the old path spent that time and then **permanently failed to fill the field at all**; this path spends comparable time and **actually fills it**. A smarter interleaved Down/Up search (try nearest-first in both directions) would cut the average case further but wasn't attempted tonight — correctness for a field that was previously *guaranteed* to never fill mattered more than a further speed optimization under time pressure, and the current version is fully verified working end to end.

The bridge was also given a clean restart.
