  **The very next relaunch still hit both problems the last two entries had claimed to fix — investigated properly this time using the actual timestamps in the persisted log, not another guess.** "Check most recent logs, its so fucking slow" / "I had to do some shenanigans again in order to bring out the timer." `logs/capsule_activity.log` showed the *exact* cause of both, in one continuous read:

  1. A **23-second silent gap** between `"Capsule run starting"` (the bridge accepting the click) and the *next* line appearing at all — nothing in the Play panel, nothing on screen, for 23 real seconds, before the countdown had even begun. This is precisely "shenanigans to bring out the timer": the timer (countdown) genuinely hadn't started yet, and there was zero feedback explaining why.
  2. My own new combobox keyboard fallback (previous entry) pressed **Down 17-18 times in a row** after the value was already stuck at the end of a 5-item list, before ever trying Up — a real, self-inflicted inefficiency, not the pre-existing dropdown bug.

  **Root cause of the 23-second gap, found by re-reading `run_task.py`'s own code rather than trusting the previous entry's own comment**: the emergency-stop hotkey fix (two entries above) added `from agent.emergency_stop import ...` and `start_emergency_stop_listener()`, with a comment claiming this was "armed before anything else, even before the countdown" — but `from agent.agent import LLMAgent` was listed **first**, textually, in that same block. A `from X import Y` statement fully executes X's own module-level code (here: importing torch and transformers, 20-30s cold) before the *next line of the script* runs, regardless of what that next line does — so the comment described the code's position on the page, not its actual execution order. The emergency hotkey was **not armed** for any of that 23-second window, either — a real safety gap layered on top of the UX one, since the one unconditional escape hatch built specifically after a user was locked out of their own mouse simply didn't exist yet during it.

  **Fixed the ordering for real**: moved the `emergency_stop` import and `start_emergency_stop_listener()` call to genuinely be the first things that run, before `from agent.agent import LLMAgent` is ever reached — and added an immediate `"Loading model and dependencies — this can take up to 20-30s the first time..."` print, so the very first thing the user sees is an explanation, not silence. Verified live, directly: spawned the real process, watched for the `"EmergencyStop: armed"` log line, and confirmed it now appears **before** `COUNTDOWN_BEGIN` (previously it couldn't, since the arm call hadn't even been reached yet by that point).

  **That fix immediately surfaced a second, deeper instance of the exact same shape of gap**: live-tested sending `CTRL_BREAK_EVENT` right after the hotkey armed (i.e., *during* the still-ongoing `LLMAgent` import) and it **still hard-killed** (exit `3221225786`) — because the import statement itself sat textually before the `try/except/finally` that saves partial results (two entries above widened that block to cover `print_countdown()` through `agent.run()`, but the import call above it was never moved). Fixed by moving `from agent.agent import LLMAgent` (and the `VisualDataReader` import next to it) to be the literal first statement *inside* that try block. Nothing meaningful exists to save at that point either way (`agent` is still `None`), but the process now exits via the same clean `KeyboardInterrupt` path as everywhere else instead of hard-killing.

  **Fixed the wasted keypresses in the combobox fallback**: `_select_combobox_value_via_keyboard()` now tracks the value before each keystroke and breaks out of the Down (or Up) loop the instant a keypress doesn't change it — confirmed live the option list doesn't wrap, so a no-op keypress means the boundary has genuinely been reached and every further press in that direction is guaranteed wasted, not just unlucky. Cuts the worst case from `max_steps` (20) blind presses down to roughly the list's real length before switching direction.

  Regression tests: `tests/test_run_task_emergency_stop_armed_before_heavy_imports.py` (3 tests — the emergency-stop import precedes the `LLMAgent` import in source order; the listener is actually *called* before that import line; the `LLMAgent` import is genuinely inside the results-saving `try:` block, anchored on a distinctive multi-line shape rather than a bare `try:` match, since this file already has several unrelated `try/except` blocks that would otherwise match first). Deliberately **not** a live, timing-dependent reproduction of "interrupt during the slow import" — how long that import actually takes varies run to run with OS file-cache warmth, confirmed directly while trying (one live attempt finished in under 2 seconds, too fast to land the interrupt inside the window at all); the structural fact of which block a line lives inside isn't flaky the way wall-clock timing is. `tests/test_combobox_keyboard_fallback.py` gained one more test for the early-exit behavior, reproducing the exact live shape (a short option list, confirms no more than list-length+1 Down presses before giving up). Full suite: **579 passed** / 9 skipped / same 2 pre-existing unrelated tesseract failures, no crash.

  **TENTH ROUND, found 2026-08-12**, directly reported ("There was a keyboard error" → "When the up and down buttons were looping a lot"): `'Primary Use'` burned the FULL `max_steps` budget in both directions (19 Down + 20 Up = 39 keystrokes, ~8s) without ever matching, visually reading as the agent being stuck — the user manually scrolled trying to unstick it. Ruled out a data mismatch first (confirmed record 2's real target, `'Personal/Pleasure'`, is an exact match for one of the form's real dropdown options `["Personal/Pleasure","Commute","Business","Rideshare (Uber/Lyft)","Farm/Ranch","Commercial"]`, read directly from `car_insurance_form_wx.py`'s `USAGE` constant) — so this isn't the earlier `'Pleasure'`-vs-`'Personal/Pleasure'` wording gap some other records in the intake file have. The genuine root cause (why THIS specific control's `ValuePattern` read never resolves) couldn't be pinned down without a live re-test — but a real, concrete, low-risk gap was found regardless: the existing non-wrapping-boundary early-stop (`_v == _prev` → break) only fires on two REAL, non-`None` reads that happen to be equal. If `_live_value()` returns `None` on every single attempt (`ControlFromPoint` not resolving the right control, a stale position, or a genuine read failure), that check never fires at all — `None` is never `"not None and == prev"` — so the search blindly exhausts the full budget in both directions no matter how unreadable the control is. A working control (per this function's own docstring) produces real, changing string reads throughout its search; three unreadable reads in a row is a strong, safe signal the control isn't going to become readable by pressing more keys.

  Fix: track consecutive `None` reads in both the Down and Up loops; stop immediately (return `False`) after 3 in a row instead of continuing to `max_steps`. Doesn't change behavior for any currently-working combobox — a control that reads correctly never hits this path. Deliberately did NOT attempt a deeper fix at the exact resolution/focus mechanism without live re-verification, consistent with this project's own hard-won discipline around guessed combobox fixes. 3 new tests (`TestUnreadableControlStopsEarly` in `tests/test_combobox_keyboard_fallback.py`), including one confirming a single flaky read sandwiched between working ones does NOT falsely trigger the early stop. Not yet verified live — the deeper "why is this one control unreadable" question remains open; the next occurrence's log will now say so explicitly (`"value unreadable N reads in a row"`) instead of silently burning the full budget, giving the next investigation real evidence to start from.

  **ELEVENTH ROUND, found 2026-08-12, the very next run after the tenth**, directly reported ("Another loop damn"): `'Bodily Injury (k$/k$)'` retried the exact same failing search **71 times** across one run. This time it's a genuine, unresolvable data problem, not a code bug in the matching itself: the record's own target value, `'30/60'`, plainly is not one of this control's real options (`['25/50', '50/100', '100/300', '250/500', '500/500', '300/300']`) — confirmed directly from the log, not assumed. No amount of clicking or keyboard-arrow searching could ever find it. The real bug is what happens *after* one failed attempt: the type-into-combobox branch (`agent.py` ~L4401, the `"Combobox: %r not in dropdown — pressing Escape"` `else` clause) had **no escalation/give-up counter of its own at all** — unlike its sibling click-fill branch a few hundred lines above it, which already solved the identical *shape* of bug on 2026-08-09 (`'Payment Frequency'`, 121 retries in one run) via `_combobox_dropdown_fail_counts`, a 2-strike counter that marks the field attempted and moves on. That fix was scoped to the branch it was found in at the time and never carried over to this sibling branch, which fails the exact same way for a different trigger condition (LLM-driven "type" decision vs. transformer-driven navigate-click).

  Fix: reuse the exact same, already-proven `_combobox_dropdown_fail_counts` / `_COMBOBOX_DROPDOWN_FAIL_LIMIT` mechanism in the type-into-combobox branch's failure path, rather than inventing a second one — both branches now share one counter dict (already declared once, in scope for the whole `run()` call) and the same 2-strike limit. On escalation, calls `self._mark_attempted()` on the focused combobox element so it stops being offered as a target, same as every other "give up and move on" mechanism in this file. 5 new tests (`tests/test_combobox_type_branch_escalation.py`), including one pinning the exact incident (gives up on attempt 2, not attempt 71). Full suite otherwise unaffected. Not yet verified live — a **fourth** field-specific case now stacked in the same investigation thread tonight (Texas, Primary Use, Bodily Injury) — worth treating the next live run's combobox behavior as the real cumulative test of this whole thread, not any one fix in isolation.

  **Same-day correction, found immediately on the very next run** (Coverage tab, directly reported: "why does it keep spamming the up and down keys?"): the eleventh round's fix was incomplete. The log showed `'Bodily Injury (k$/k$)'` correctly escalating and logging *"marking attempted and moving on"* — then retrying again on the **very next step anyway**. Root cause: `self._mark_attempted()` is pure bookkeeping — it never moves keyboard focus. Since focus never actually left the failed combobox, the next step's LLM lookup (which doesn't check attempted-state at all — it just answers from the record data) re-triggered the entire fill sequence from scratch, over and over. "Marking attempted and moving on" was a lie the log was telling — attempted, yes; moving on, no.

  Fixed two ways: (1) an **active redirect** to the next visible target after escalating, mirroring the click-fill sibling branch's own already-working redirect logic (`find_visible_empty_target` + focus/click) — the piece deliberately left out of the eleventh-round fix on the (wrong) assumption that marking attempted would be enough. (2) The escalated field is now **also** added to `self._leave_blank_keys` — a 2-strike give-up is just as final and deliberate a decision as a genuine leave-blank answer, so the reclick guard (extended earlier the same day for leave-blank fields) now also protects this field from the transformer's own navigate-click pointer drifting back onto it later, not just this immediate re-ask. Applied to **both** the type-into-combobox branch and its click-fill sibling for consistency (the sibling's original 2026-08-09 fix had the same latent `_leave_blank_keys` gap, just never surfaced it live). 2 new tests confirming `_leave_blank_keys` and `_attempted_keys` are genuinely different signals (attempted alone must stay permissive, per the SEVENTH-round reasoning), and that escalation's key computation agrees with what the reclick guard checks. Not yet verified live.

- [x] `execution_bridge_launch_stdin_inheritance_hang` — **Shipped 2026-08-10, the most severe form of "Play doesn't run" hit this session — a complete, silent hang, not slowness.** After all the fixes above, `run_task.py` launched through the real Electron → bridge chain would sometimes sit `Responding: True` with near-zero CPU for minutes, produce zero stdout, and create zero log file — meaning it hung *before* `logging.basicConfig()`, inside the first ~10 lines of the script, while the identical command run directly in a terminal always worked. Directly observed against real OS process state (`Get-CimInstance Win32_Process`, `Get-Process ... Responding`), not inferred: two real `run_task.py` processes (PID 15200, 2156) confirmed alive, confirmed not crashed, confirmed not computing.

  **First theory, tried and shipped without live confirmation, then found insufficient**: gave the child its own real console (`creationflags=CREATE_NEW_CONSOLE`, replacing `CREATE_NEW_PROCESS_GROUP`), reasoning the console-less `windowsHide` ancestry had already caused two other confirmed bugs this session (the `OSError`-on-flush saga, the emergency-stop import-order saga) and was worth eliminating as a whole class rather than patching another individual symptom. Live process-state evidence directly contradicted this: a process spawned with the new flag (PID 2156) hung exactly the same way as before it.

  **Second theory, found and fixed**: `run_capsule()`'s `Popen` call never set `stdin` at all, so `run_task.py` inherited the *bridge's own* stdin handle — itself a pipe Electron's `main.js` writes JSON commands into. A grandchild silently holding a handle onto a pipe two processes up the chain, spawned from a console-less parent, is a known way to get a process that's blocked (not crashed) on standard-handle setup. Added `stdin=subprocess.DEVNULL`, explicit.

  **Isolated reproduction of this exact theory came back clean in BOTH directions** — before shipping it as a confirmed fix, built a from-scratch harness (three stub scripts, no real `run_task.py`/agent code anywhere, so it stayed clear of "only the user runs live tasks") replicating just the mechanism: an outer process with `stdin=PIPE` (matching Electron), spawning a middle process that itself spawns a grandchild either with `stdin` unset (old behavior) or `stdin=DEVNULL` (the fix). Both modes unblocked in ~1 second. This **disproved the stdin-inheritance theory as a sufficient explanation on its own** — the mechanism, isolated, does not reproduce the hang. Reported this honestly rather than shipping the fix on reasoning alone.

  **Confirmed live anyway, twice, through the real Electron bridge** (per direct user instruction — this is the user's call to make, not something a live-test harness can substitute for): with `stdin=DEVNULL` in place, a real Play click produced a log file within ~1s, a real-time countdown, and a working run reaching Step 1+ — the hang was gone. The isolated repro's failure to reproduce the bug likely means something about the *real* Electron process tree specifically (not just "any nested Python processes with a piped stdin") is the true differentiator — possibly environment-variable inheritance from Electron's own process (a related, confirmed-real instance of this exact class of problem surfaced in the same session: relaunching Electron from a shell carrying `ELECTRON_RUN_AS_NODE=1` made `require("electron")` return a plain path string instead of the real module, crashing `app.whenReady()` — unrelated to this specific hang, but proof inherited environment state from whatever launches Electron can matter here). **This exact mechanism remains not fully understood** — what's confirmed is the fix works, live, reproducibly; not a complete explanation of why.

  **The CREATE_NEW_CONSOLE change, still in place at that point, immediately drew direct, explicit user pushback**: *"I don't want the fucking Terminal to launch goddamnit."* A real console window popping up for every Play click is not acceptable for a GUI app regardless of whether it also happened to coincide with the hang going away. **Reverted to `CREATE_NEW_PROCESS_GROUP`** (the value this flag held all session before this bug, already independently confirmed compatible with `CTRL_BREAK_EVENT`/Stop) — explicitly owning, in the code comment, that more than one variable changed between the last confirmed hang and the first confirmed success (the code fix *and* a full Electron relaunch happened together), which is sloppier than the "one variable at a time" standard this project otherwise holds to.

  **Re-confirmed live a third time, immediately, after the revert**: Play clicked again with `CREATE_NEW_PROCESS_GROUP` + `stdin=DEVNULL` (no `CREATE_NEW_CONSOLE`) — no console window appeared, log file still created within ~1s, countdown still ran in real time, run still reached Step 1+ normally. This isolates `stdin=DEVNULL` as sufficient on its own, in the real environment, without `CREATE_NEW_CONSOLE`. Directly confirmed by the user: *"okay you did it. Good job, it can finally run now without opening the Terminal and without delay."*

  Regression tests: `tests/test_recorder_bridge_capsule_run.py` gained `test_stdin_is_explicitly_devnull_not_inherited` and `test_creationflags_is_new_process_group_not_new_console` (mocked `Popen`, no real process spawned — consistent with this file's existing "only the user runs live tasks" boundary). Full suite: **581 passed** / 9 skipped / same 2 pre-existing unrelated tesseract failures, no crash.

  **Immediately surfaced a separate, real bug once the hang was out of the way**: see `execution_ghost_overlay_breaks_uia_reads` below.

- [ ] `execution_ghost_overlay_breaks_uia_reads` — **Found live 2026-08-10, in the same session as the hang fix above, both live confirmation runs.** With the hang and the console-window regression both fixed, `'Policy Status'` (same failure family as `execution_combobox_never_opens_keyboard_fallback` above) hit its dropdown-never-renders path *and* exhausted the full keyboard-fallback (8 down-tries, then up to 20 more Down/Up steps) **without ever changing its value**, unlike `'Policy Type'`'s successful live verification in that earlier entry. ~17 real seconds spent, twice, and both times the very next step re-clicked the exact same field and was about to repeat the identical failure. The user manually interrupted with the emergency-stop hotkey both times — direct evidence this is disruptive enough to end a run over.

  **First-pass diagnosis (recorded here, then corrected, same day) was wrong**: initially assumed no back-off mechanism existed for a field that keeps failing. Reading `agent.py` more carefully before writing any fix code found this was already handled — `_combobox_dropdown_fail_counts` (a per-label streak counter, `_COMBOBOX_DROPDOWN_FAIL_LIMIT = 2`, added 2026-08-09 for exactly this class of problem) would very likely have fired on the very next attempt; both live runs were killed by the user one step before it could. Caught this before shipping a redundant/wrong fix — the real question was never "why doesn't this back off," it was "why does the keyboard fallback fail at all for a control confirmed live, in this same file's own history, to respond to type-ahead and arrow keys."

  **Root cause, found by direct live UIA probing of the real, currently-open form** (same methodology as `execution_combobox_never_opens_keyboard_fallback`): `ControlFromPoint(cx, cy)` — used by both `_select_combobox_value_via_keyboard()`'s `_live_value()` (agent.py) and `executor.py`'s `_try_uia_invoke()` — was silently resolving to the ghost-cursor overlay's own window (`ghost_overlay.py`, added earlier this same session) instead of the real combobox underneath. Confirmed with a clean, direct before/during/after comparison at the same screen point: before the overlay started, `ControlFromPoint` found the real control; the instant the overlay started — even with *nothing drawn on it yet* — it resolved to `ControlType=PaneControl, Name='tk'`, no patterns at all. This overlay covers the *entire screen* for its whole lifetime (`root.geometry(f"{sw}x{sh}+0+0")`), not just a small area around the cursor, so this affected every read at every point, the whole time the overlay was running — not something specific to Policy Status. `_live_value()` returning `None` on every single call explains everything observed: no keypress was ever recognized as changing the value, so both the type-ahead and Down/Up walks always ran to full exhaustion and always reported failure. This also means `executor.py`'s `_try_uia_invoke()` (the "try a real UIA click before falling back to pyautogui" optimization, tried on *every* click in the agent) has been silently defeated the same way since the overlay was added — not a correctness bug there (the pyautogui fallback still works), but a fully dead optimization the whole time, invisible because nothing ever surfaced the failure.

  **Two cheaper fixes were tried and directly disproven before landing on the real one** — both seemed reasonable, both were tested live rather than assumed:
  1. `ShowWindow(hwnd, SW_HIDE)` / `SetWindowPos` (move the window off-screen), via `ctypes`, synchronous, bypassing the overlay's own async show/hide queue entirely. Disproven directly: `IsWindowVisible()`/`GetWindowRect()` both correctly reflected the change at the real Win32 level immediately (confirmed even after a full second's wait), but `ControlFromPoint` at the same point kept resolving to the window's *stale* prior state regardless — UI Automation's view of this particular window (Tkinter, `overrideredirect`, layered) did not track its own live visibility or position once already established.
  2. The overlay's existing `hide_cursor()`/`hide_caret()` (canvas-only clears) — no effect for the same reason `WS_EX_TRANSPARENT` already didn't help UIA specifically: the window's mere *existence*, not its drawn content or visibility flag, is what was being hit.

  **The only mechanism confirmed, live, to actually work**: genuinely stopping the overlay (destroying its window) and starting a fresh one afterward. Verified directly: `ControlFromPoint` at a point the overlay was covering found the real control again immediately once `stop()` had returned. New `GhostOverlay.hide_for_uia_read()`/`restore_after_uia_read()` do exactly this. Real, measurable overhead (thread teardown + a fresh `Tk()`, confirmed live at ~55ms + ~85ms — cheaper than feared, but non-zero), so scoped carefully:
  - `agent.py`'s `_select_combobox_value_via_keyboard()` now calls this **once** for the whole function call (before the type-ahead/Down/Up search begins, restored in a `finally` after it ends) — not once per keystroke read, which would have multiplied the cost by up to ~40x.
  - `executor.py`'s `_try_uia_invoke()` deliberately does **not** get this fix — it runs on every single click throughout an entire run, where paying this cost per click would be a real, unacceptable slowdown. Left as a documented, known gap: this optimization silently no-ops (falls through to the working pyautogui path) whenever the overlay is active. A cheaper fix for this specific call site (e.g. resizing the overlay to a small, positioned window instead of full-screen, so most on-screen points are never covered at all) is a real architectural option but wasn't attempted tonight — correctness for the combobox fallback (previously guaranteed to fail) mattered more under time pressure than a speed optimization that already has a working fallback.

  Regression tests: `tests/test_ghost_overlay.py` gained `TestHideForUiaRead` (3 tests, mocked stop/start delegation) and extended the file's one real-Tk-window subprocess test to exercise a genuine `hide_for_uia_read()`/`restore_after_uia_read()` cycle against a real window (confirms actual thread teardown/recreation, not just mocks). `tests/test_combobox_keyboard_fallback.py` gained `TestGhostOverlayStoppedForTheWholeCall` (4 tests: hide/restore called exactly once regardless of keystroke count; restore skipped when hide reports nothing was running; restore still happens after an exception; no crash when `.ghost` doesn't exist at all). Full suite: **588 passed** / 9 skipped / same 2 pre-existing unrelated tesseract failures, no crash.

  Not yet re-verified through a complete real Play-launched run (verified directly against the raw mechanism — overlay start/stop/UIA resolution — and via the real `_select_combobox_value_via_keyboard()` code path with a mocked ghost, but not yet via an actual live capsule run reaching this exact field) — that's the next real confirmation, alongside seeing whether `_combobox_dropdown_fail_counts`'s existing 2-strike back-off (never actually disproven, just never fully observed) behaves as expected on a field that's now genuinely fixable.

  **REVERTED the same day, after a severe live regression — reported plainly: "It got worse, still was not solved and also why couldn't I click a damn thing in the form?"** The fix above was verified exactly once per process before shipping (one hide/restore cycle, in a subprocess-isolated test). A real run needed it TWICE in quick succession — two failed `'Policy Status'` attempts before `_combobox_dropdown_fail_counts`'s 2-strike back-off correctly gave up and moved on (confirmed working as designed, directly in the log: `"[OPT2] 'Policy Status' failed to fill 2 times in a row ... marking attempted and moving on."`). Immediately after moving on to the next field (`'Policy Type'`) and clicking to open its dropdown, `logs/capsule_activity.log` recorded: `"Tcl_AsyncDelete: async handler deleted by the wrong thread"` — the exact Tcl/Tk corruption signature `tests/test_ghost_overlay.py`'s own module docstring already documents as a precursor to a hard, process-wide crash (`"Windows fatal exception: code 0x80000003"`) when a Tk root's teardown isn't strictly confined to a single, isolated process. The run then produced zero further output for 17 seconds until the user force-killed it via the emergency-stop hotkey — direct, live evidence of a corrupted, no-longer-click-through, full-screen topmost window sitting over the whole desktop, blocking every click: *"We're not even filling the task anymore. After properly inputting Policy Number, it can't fucking click anymore."*

  This is a worse bug than the one being fixed. Repeated in-process `stop()`→`start()` cycling of this Tk thread is not safe, even though a single cycle, tested in isolation, genuinely is. Reverted immediately: `_select_combobox_value_via_keyboard()`'s `_live_value()` is back to a plain, unguarded read (the ghost-overlay interference this whole entry diagnoses is real and still unfixed at the read level); `GhostOverlay.hide_for_uia_read()`/`restore_after_uia_read()` were removed from `ghost_overlay.py` entirely rather than left defined-but-unused — a working-in-isolation, dangerous-in-practice method sitting in the codebase is a trap for a future "just call it once more" change to re-trigger the exact same crash class. The diagnosis in this entry (the overlay's full-screen window intercepting `ControlFromPoint` for its entire lifetime) remains correct and valuable; only the fix attempt is reverted.

  What actually bounds the damage now, same as before this whole investigation started: `_combobox_dropdown_fail_counts`'s 2-strike back-off, confirmed working live in the very same run that surfaced this crash — a combobox whose dropdown never renders costs at most two ~17s attempts before the agent marks it attempted and moves on, not an infinite loop. A wrong-but-bounded fallback beats a fix that can silently take over the user's entire screen input. A real fix for the underlying UIA-interference bug (e.g. resizing the overlay to a small, positioned window instead of a full-screen one, so most on-screen points are never covered and nothing ever needs to be destroyed/recreated) is a genuine, separate piece of architectural work, not a same-day patch — noted here for whenever that's picked up.

  Regression tests: the `TestHideForUiaRead` class in `tests/test_ghost_overlay.py`, the extended real-Tk hide/restore assertions in that file's one subprocess lifecycle script, and the `TestGhostOverlayStoppedForTheWholeCall` class in `tests/test_combobox_keyboard_fallback.py` were all removed along with the code they tested — they were testing a mechanism that no longer exists, not a regression guard for anything still shipped. Full suite back to **581 passed** / 9 skipped / same 2 pre-existing unrelated tesseract failures, no crash — matching the count from immediately before this fix was ever added.

  **Very next live run, reported plainly: "The Agent couldn't click on the form at all. It's frozen shut."** Directly checked the wxForm process (`Get-Process ... Responding`) — `True`, not actually hung at the OS level, and `capsule_activity.log` showed no `Tcl_AsyncDelete` this time (the crash mechanism above is genuinely gone). What the log showed instead: `'Policy Number'` filled correctly, then `'Policy Status'` hit the (still-unfixed, expected) dead end — 3.2s click-and-poll, then **~11 straight seconds of nothing but Down/Down/Down.../Up/Up/Up keyboard-only actions** (`_select_combobox_value_via_keyboard()` always burning its full budget, since the ghost overlay still breaks every value read, exactly as this entry already documents) with zero clicks and zero visible on-screen change, since the dropdown never renders. From outside, watching the screen, that's indistinguishable from "frozen" even though the process never actually stopped responding — a real, valid UX report even though it wasn't the same crash class as before.

  Since this fallback currently has a **provable 0% success rate** whenever a ghost overlay is present (and `agent.py` hardcodes `ghost_cursor=True` for every real, non-dry-run construction — so every real live run), and re-attempting the stop/restart fix again the same day was clearly not the move, the safe fix was to stop calling it under that exact condition rather than try to repair the read again: `_select_combobox_value_via_keyboard()` now returns `False` immediately, with zero keyboard actions, whenever `self._executor.ghost is not None`. Falls straight through to the existing, already-proven-working `_combobox_dropdown_fail_counts` 2-strike escalation — same eventual outcome (field marked attempted, agent moves on), but the "looks frozen" window drops from ~11s down to the original ~3.2s poll per attempt. Scoped precisely on `ghost is not None`, not a blanket disable — dry_run and any future `ghost_cursor=False` construction still get the real fallback logic, unaffected; only the one condition that's actually proven broken is skipped. No threading, no window lifecycle, no Tcl/Tk touched at all this time — the lowest-risk change available given what's already gone wrong twice today.

  Existing tests all constructed their mock executor via plain `MagicMock()`, which auto-creates `.ghost` as a truthy sub-mock — meaning the new guard would have silently short-circuited every existing test before it ever reached the logic it was written to exercise, without any of them failing (a false-confidence trap, not a crash). Fixed by having `_make_executor_mock()` explicitly set `ghost = None`, and the two `MagicMock()`-direct tests in `TestGracefulDegradation` likewise, so all of them continue to test the real search algorithm exactly as before. New `TestSkippedWhileGhostOverlayIsActive` (2 tests): confirms zero keyboard actions when `ghost` is present, and confirms the fallback still runs normally when it's `None`. Full suite: **583 passed** / 9 skipped / same 2 pre-existing unrelated tesseract failures, no crash.

  Still open, deliberately not fixed today: the actual UIA-read bug this whole entry diagnoses. The keyboard fallback is fully correct, verified-working logic (confirmed live, the very same day) that simply cannot currently prove its own success while a ghost overlay covers the screen — it's disabled under exactly that condition, not deleted. A real fix (e.g. a small, positioned overlay window instead of full-screen, so `ControlFromPoint` mostly never needs to see through it at all) remains genuine future architectural work.

- [ ] `execution_reassert_backoff_unidentified_foreign_window` — **Found live 2026-08-10, directly following the fix above.** With the keyboard-fallback skip in place (11s cut to ~3.2s per dead combobox), the very next live run still reported: *"Still the same problem the fucking screen is still frozen, you can't click on the form after Policy Number."* Read the log directly rather than assuming a repeat of the earlier crash: no `Tcl_AsyncDelete` this time (that mechanism stayed fixed), and the wxForm process itself was `Responding: True` when checked directly — not actually hung at the OS level.

  What the log actually showed: `'Policy Type'` failed to open its dropdown twice, then `'Policy Status'` failed twice more — four unrelated combobox failures back to back, ~19 seconds total, each individually fast (~5s) but stacking into one long stretch with nothing visibly filling in. That's a separate, already-known class of flakiness (some comboboxes just don't reliably render on click — the same open question `execution_combobox_never_opens_keyboard_fallback` and its history already track), not a new regression from today's fix.

  The concerning new detail: `_reassert_form_window()`'s streak-breaker logged `"NOT re-stealing foreground -- hwnd=197334 has held focus across 2/3 consecutive steps..."` twice. Asked the user directly what actually happened when they tried to click, rather than assume — direct answer: *"Clicked the form, literally nothing happened."* That single fact rules out the streak-breaker's own stated assumption ("e.g. the user reaching for Stop") — the user clicked the visible form itself, not some other window, and nothing happened at all, meaning some window OTHER than the form was silently sitting on top of it and swallowing that click, well before the user ever tried.

  Tried to identify hwnd 197334 after the fact (`Get-Process | Where MainWindowHandle -eq 197334`) — no match, but this is not conclusive: `Process.MainWindowHandle` only resolves *decorated* top-level windows, and the ghost overlay is deliberately `overrideredirect` (borderless, no taskbar presence) — exactly the shape of window this lookup method cannot find whether or not it's actually the culprit. Real suspects, in order of plausibility: (1) the ghost overlay itself, somehow holding `GetForegroundWindow()` status despite `WS_EX_TRANSPARENT` (which governs mouse hit-testing, a separate Windows concept from "foreground window" — no contradiction if both are simultaneously true); (2) a same-process wx dialog (e.g. a stray modal) that the existing modal-dismiss logic a few lines below the streak-breaker was never reached to handle, since the streak-breaker's `return` at streak≥2 happens **before** that same-PID check ever runs — a real, structural gap, independent of whether this specific incident was that; (3) some unrelated third-party window (a notification, a sync-client popup) coincidentally grabbing focus. No live evidence yet distinguishes these — the bare hwnd number the warning used to log is useless once the process has exited, which is exactly what happened here.

  Rather than guess a fix for an unidentified window a second time in one day, added **diagnostic-only** logging: the backoff warning now looks up and includes the foreign window's title, class name, and whether it's owned by the *same process* as the locked form (reusing the `win32process` pattern already used a few lines below for the modal-dialog check) — best-effort, wrapped so a lookup failure (window already gone, permissions) can never prevent the actual safety-critical behavior (backing off) from happening. Zero behavior change otherwise. The next occurrence will say definitively whether this is the ghost overlay, a same-process dialog, or something else entirely — and if it's a same-process dialog, that also confirms the structural gap above needs its own fix (moving the same-PID modal check ahead of the streak-breaker's early return, or running it regardless of streak).

  Regression tests: `tests/test_reassert_foreground_streak_breaker.py` gained `TestBackoffDiagnosticLogging` (3 tests — title/class appear in the log; a same-process window is clearly flagged `same_process_as_form=True` vs. `False` for an unrelated one; a lookup failure never prevents backing off). Full suite: **586 passed** / 9 skipped / same 2 pre-existing unrelated tesseract failures, no crash. Diagnosis only — genuinely not yet fixed, waiting on the next live occurrence to identify the actual window before deciding what the real fix is.

  **Identified and fixed the very next live run.** The new diagnostic logging did its job: `"NOT re-stealing foreground -- hwnd=1966806 title='tk' class='TkTopLevel' same_process_as_form=False"` — `title='tk'` and `class='TkTopLevel'` are the literal, unmistakable Tkinter defaults. This is the ghost overlay itself, confirmed, not a same-process dialog and not an unrelated third-party window.

  Verified live, directly, exactly what was happening: `GetForegroundWindow()` checked immediately before vs. after `GhostOverlay().start()` showed the overlay becomes the OS foreground/active window the instant it's created — a normal side effect of creating any new `-topmost`, visible top-level window, and completely independent of `WS_EX_TRANSPARENT` (which only ever governed mouse *hit-testing* — whether a click lands on this window — never window *activation*, a separate Windows concept). `_make_click_through()` applies `WS_EX_TRANSPARENT` only *after* the window already exists and has already been activated, so it never had any effect on this.

  This combined with the streak-breaker (an earlier fix, same day, for an unrelated real incident) to produce the reported bug: `_reassert_form_window()` correctly reclaims foreground the first time (its designed behavior for a single stray drift), but the overlay keeps taking it back — and once the *same* foreign window recurs, the streak-breaker deliberately stops fighting for it, assuming a human is deliberately using another app. Direct user report: *"Clicked the form, literally nothing happened."*

  **Fixed with a second, independent Win32 extended-window-style flag: `WS_EX_NOACTIVATE`** — added to the exact same `SetWindowLongW` call that already sets `WS_EX_TRANSPARENT`/`WS_EX_LAYERED` in `_make_click_through()`. This flag tells Windows the window should never become foreground/active through normal means at all. Verified live, precisely targeting the actual failure mode rather than just the flag's existence: `GetForegroundWindow()` still shows the overlay winning the *very first* activation at creation time (expected — `WS_EX_NOACTIVATE` is applied after that initial activation already happened, and `_reassert_form_window()` already handles a single one-time steal correctly by design, so this doesn't need fixing). The part that matters — simulated `SetForegroundWindow()` reclaim (standing in for `_reassert_form_window()`'s own reclaim call) followed by 5 full seconds of polling across simulated steps — showed foreground staying exactly where it was reclaimed to, with **zero** recurrence, versus the original bug's confirmed pattern of recurring across 2-3+ consecutive steps.

  Regression test: `tests/test_ghost_overlay.py::TestClickThroughWindowStyle::test_sets_ws_ex_noactivate` — confirms the flag is present in the style bits `SetWindowLongW` is actually called with (same mocked-`user32` pattern the sibling `WS_EX_TRANSPARENT` test already uses). Full suite: **587 passed** / 9 skipped / same 2 pre-existing unrelated tesseract failures, no crash.

  Deliberately a small, single-flag, no-threading, no-window-lifecycle change — the opposite shape of the fix that caused today's earlier crash — and verified against the *actual reported failure mode* (recurring theft after reclaim) with real, live before/after measurements, not just confirming the style bit gets set. Not yet re-verified through a live Play run reaching this exact scenario (the next real click is the actual end-to-end confirmation) — but this is the first fix today with direct, mechanism-level live evidence behind it before being shipped, rather than after something broke.

  **The very next live run, reported plainly: "Still unable to click anything whatsoever check most recent logs."** Read the log directly. Good news first: the `WS_EX_NOACTIVATE` fix above genuinely held — `"Re-asserted form foreground"` succeeded twice in this run, with **zero** `"NOT re-stealing foreground"` backoff warnings anywhere in the log. The recurring-theft bug is confirmed gone. But the user still couldn't click, and killed the run only ~13 seconds in, ~1 second after the last logged action.

  One concrete clue pointed the way: the user *could* trigger the emergency-stop hotkey (a global OS-level hook, independent of any window's focus) but apparently could not click. That specifically implicates mouse click-routing, not window activation — a different mechanism than what was just fixed. Rather than theorize again, tested it directly and empirically for the first time this session: spawned a real, disposable Notepad window as a safe click target, started the ghost overlay, showed the cursor icon at a specific point, then fired a **real `pyautogui.click()`** at that exact point and checked which window ended up with `GetForegroundWindow()`.

  Result: the click did **not** reach Notepad. `GetForegroundWindow()` after the click was the overlay's own `'tk'` window. A real, physical mouse click aimed at the drawn ghost-cursor icon was swallowed by the overlay instead of passing through — directly contradicting `WS_EX_TRANSPARENT`, and contradicting `ghost_overlay.py`'s own module docstring, which names click-through as *"the one thing this MUST get right."* That guarantee had only ever been checked by confirming the Windows style bits get set (`TestClickThroughWindowStyle`) — never against an actual click on actual drawn (non-color-keyed) content. Plausible mechanism: `WS_EX_LAYERED` (required for the `-transparentcolor` trick) has its own, separate hit-testing rules for opaque vs. color-keyed pixels that can take precedence over `WS_EX_TRANSPARENT` specifically where real, non-transparent-colored pixels are drawn — exactly where the cursor/caret shapes are. Since the ghost cursor is drawn exactly where the agent just acted — precisely where a user watching the run would naturally try to click too — this directly explains the repeated "can't click the form" reports, including this one, which happened *after* the correct, verified `WS_EX_NOACTIVATE` fix for the unrelated foreground-theft bug.

  **This is the third distinct, confirmed, live-verified bug traced to this one feature in a single session**: (1) breaks every UIA read the whole time it runs; (2) steals and re-steals OS foreground; (3) swallows real physical clicks aimed at its own drawn content. None of the three have a quick, safe, well-understood fix remaining — the two attempted so far cost one severe crash and consumed most of a night's work. Rather than attempt a fourth patch to increasingly subtle Win32/DWM layered-window hit-testing semantics under continued time pressure, **disabled the feature entirely**: `LLMAgent.__init__` now constructs its `ActionExecutor` with `ghost_cursor=False` (was `True`, unconditionally, for every real live run). Reverts to `pyautogui`'s default behavior — moving the real OS cursor for clicks — the same proven, boring mechanism this project used before the ghost cursor existed.

  This one change also retroactively resolves the two "workaround" fixes shipped earlier today for symptoms of the same root cause, without touching either of them: with no overlay ever constructed in a real run, `self._executor.ghost` is `None`, so `_select_combobox_value_via_keyboard()`'s ghost-present skip (added a few entries above) simply never triggers and the keyboard fallback runs its real, already-verified-working logic again — and `executor.py`'s `_try_uia_invoke()` optimization, silently dead the entire time the overlay existed, starts actually working again too. Both were left in place rather than removed (see that entry's own reasoning) — dormant, correct, defensive code for whenever the overlay is properly redesigned and re-enabled.

  Regression test: `tests/test_agent_ghost_cursor_disabled.py` — patches `agent.executor.ActionExecutor` (where `agent.py`'s local `from agent.executor import ActionExecutor` actually resolves at call time, not a module-level attribute of `agent.agent`) and asserts `ghost_cursor=False` is what `LLMAgent.__init__` actually passes, so this can't silently flip back to `True` in a future refactor without that being a deliberate, visible decision. Full suite: **588 passed** / 9 skipped / same 2 pre-existing unrelated tesseract failures, no crash.

  Honest about what's lost: the orange ghost cursor/caret visual — a cosmetic nicety, and one the user had *already* separately reported never being able to find on screen even before any of this (never investigated, now moot). Not lost: anything about correctness or safety — `_try_uia_invoke()`'s real-cursor-skip optimization for supported controls still exists and still applies when a UIA action succeeds; only the purely visual indicator of where a *simulated* click landed is gone, and the real OS cursor visibly moving during automation (standard `pyautogui` behavior, unremarkable before this feature existed) takes its place. **Confirmed live the same night** — one more real run first surfaced a separate, unrelated issue (the Notepad window holding the source intake data wasn't open, so the record cache had 0 fields and every field was correctly left blank — not a code bug, just missing setup; reopened `data_entry_tasks/data_entry_intake.txt` in Notepad and the agent immediately began typing real values again). With that resolved, the user confirmed plainly: *"Okay it works, damn."* Clicking, typing, and focus all functioning normally — this closes out the ghost-overlay incident chain for tonight.

  **THIRD round, same session — a direct correction, not a new request.** *"Revert old container design in the Workflow tabs. I meant I want it shaped like a microchip, you added a background and called it finished. Also the buttons and elements are still spaced together in the READY TO PLAY area for God's sake, fix that."* Round 2's "microchip" (item 1 above) only themed the background/texture of an ordinary rounded card — the card's own outline was still a plain rectangle. That was insufficient; fixed properly this round:

  1. **`.task-chip` now has an actual chip-shaped silhouette**, not just a themed background. A small inline SVG (a rounded body rect plus 12 short "pin" rects along all four edges, like a DIP-package IC) is applied as a CSS `mask-image`/`-webkit-mask-image` on the element, so the *entire rendered element* — background texture included — is clipped into that outline. `aspect-ratio: 1` replaces the old `min-height`, keeping the same footprint as before (~152px grid tiles, per the direct instruction to keep the same size) while giving the mask's square viewBox undistorted geometry. Padding increased to keep text/icon content inside the visible chip body rather than the masked-out pin margins. The old border/box-shadow "selected" state was dropped — a border or outer box-shadow paints around the full rectangular bounding box, not the masked silhouette, so it would show as a rectangle floating around the chip shape instead of hugging it. Selection is now shown by swapping the chip's own fill color (`--ink` → `--accent-blue`) inside the mask instead, which is guaranteed to render correctly since it's part of the masked content itself, not a separate paint layer.

  2. **Real root cause of the Play panel spacing complaint found and fixed.** `#ppCheckpointGroup` and `#ppTestGroup` in `index.html` (the "READY TO PLAY" controls — label, dropdown, button) had *zero CSS rules of their own* — plain block `<div>`s with no gap, relying entirely on their children's own margins, which none of the label/select/button elements actually had. The outer `.play-panel` container's `gap: 16px` (increased in round 2, item 3) only spaces *direct* children, not content nested a level deeper inside these groups — so that earlier fix never touched the actual complaint. Fixed with one new rule (`#ppCheckpointGroup, #ppTestGroup { display: flex; flex-direction: column; gap: 10px; }`), plus supporting padding/gap increases on `.pp-slot`, `.pp-capsule`, `.pp-warning`, and `.rp-btn-row` so the whole panel reads consistently spaced, not just the one group directly named.

- [x] `uiux_task_crud_and_save_to_fix` — **2026-08-17, direct request** ("Add a CRUD function for the Workflows, also I want you to edit the 'Save to' function to the specific workflows not some random data/demos/x"). Found the real problem first, not assumed: two disconnected things were both loosely called "workflow" — capsule registry entries (`tasks/registry.json`, what actually shows as a Tasks-page chip and what Play runs) and raw `data/demos/<name>/` folders (what the Recorder's Save-to dropdown listed, unrelated to any capsule — only `data/demos/eight_Tabs` existed, belonging to nothing). Unified "workflow" = capsule.

  `app_electron/main.js` gained `createTask(name, description)` (registers a real capsule stub AND reserves the matching `data/demos/<name>` folder in one step, so a new task shows up as a chip immediately instead of only existing as an orphan folder), `updateCapsule(name, updates)` (whitelisted patch: description/emoji/trigger_keywords/trigger_apps only — `model_path`/`kind`/`entrypoint` stay off-limits, those come from training/registration not hand-editing), and `deleteCapsule(name)` (registry-entry only, by direct decision confirmed via AskUserQuestion after weighing it as a real destructive-action call — recorded sessions and trained checkpoints are left on disk untouched, so Delete can never destroy recorded work or a trained model as a side effect). All three verified against a throwaway registry file (create/duplicate-reject/update/delete/double-delete-reject, plus a real Python round-trip through `CapsuleRegistry`/`WorkflowCapsule`) before trusting them in the app.

  Tasks page: each chip gained a small pencil button (top-right corner, clear of both the kicker text and the chip's own chamfered corner) opening a centered edit modal — description, an emoji grid (reusing the Play panel's existing `EMOJI_CHOICES`/tile pattern), trigger keywords/apps (hidden for a script-kind capsule, which is never auto-routed), and a Delete button using the already-existing `.btn-danger` style (`var(--error)`, not `--clay` — clay stays reserved for "agent has control," never repurposed as a generic danger color). Renaming was deliberately left out and explained directly rather than silently omitted: a task's name is load-bearing for its `data/demos/<name>` folder and (once trained) its model path, so a rename would mean moving real files — flagged as a separate, harder feature, not bundled in here.

  `populateOutDirOptions()` (the Save-to dropdown) rewritten to list real capsule names (`data/demos/<capsule.name>`) from `capsulesAPI.list()` instead of scanning `data/demos/` directly — filtered to `kind !== "script"` since a script-kind capsule (Scope #2) doesn't record via the Recorder at all, same exclusion `findCapsuleForGroup()` already used elsewhere. `data/demos/eight_Tabs` (the pre-existing orphan folder) now correctly disappears from Save-to — intentional, not a bug: it belongs to no task, which is exactly the problem being fixed.

  Mid-task correction, caught live by the user testing the feature (not by me): the first pass genuinely had no way to rename a task at all. Explained the real constraint (rename = moving files, not just an editable label) rather than silently adding it; asked whether rename should be allowed at least for untrained tasks (safe — no files but the demos folder to move) vs. also for trained ones (risky — `form_filling`'s own `traces/` directory alone is ~22GB). Not yet answered/built — tracked as the next piece of this same node, not a separate one.

  **Regressed and re-fixed 2026-09-01.** Direct report: "the Recorder's 'Save to' dropdown only has eight_tabs." Root-caused with `git log -S` on the filter line, not guessed: commit `dcc14ee0` (2026-08-25, adding `kind="url"` capsule support) had silently changed this node's own correct `kind !== "script"` filter to `kind === "agent"` — a positive check that only matches a registry entry carrying the literal `"kind": "agent"` key. `form_filling`'s entry has no `"kind"` key at all (it relies on the Python `WorkflowCapsule` dataclass's `kind="agent"` default, which the JS-side `listCapsules()` — a raw JSON read of `registry.json`, no dataclass involved — never applies), so it came back as `kind: undefined` and matched nothing. Zero recordable capsules meant `populateOutDirOptions()` gave up immediately, leaving the single hardcoded, orphaned `<option>data/demos/eight_Tabs</option>` baked directly into `index.html` as the only choice ever shown — the exact same class of bug this node already fixed once, reintroduced by a later, unrelated change.

  Fixed by restoring the exclusion-based filter (`kind !== "script" && kind !== "url"`), matching `findCapsuleForGroup()`'s own established pattern elsewhere in the same file — this generalizes to any future `kind` that isn't script/url, unlike a positive match against one literal string, which is exactly what broke. Replaced the stale hardcoded HTML option with a neutral "Loading tasks…" placeholder so a future silent failure can't masquerade as a real choice again. Also, per direct request, the dropdown's visible text now shows just the workflow's name instead of the raw `data/demos/<name>` path — the value actually passed to the recorder is unchanged, only the label shown to the human. Verified against the real `registry.json` directly (Node one-liner simulating the filter) before committing. Commit `059d2d99` on this branch.
